Rapid Mixing at the Uniqueness Threshold
Xiaoyu Chen, Zongchen Chen, Yitong Yin, Xinyuan Zhang

Abstract
Over the past decades, a fascinating computational phase transition has been identified in sampling from Gibbs distributions. Specifically, for the hardcore model on graphs with n n vertices and maximum degree Δ \Delta , the computational complexity of sampling from the Gibbs distribution, defined over the independent sets of the graph with vertex-weight λ > 0 \lambda>0 , undergoes a sharp transition at the critical threshold λ c ​ ( Δ ) := ( Δ − 1 ) Δ − 1 ( Δ − 2 ) Δ \lambda_{c}(\Delta):=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}} , known as the tree-uniqueness threshold: • In the uniqueness regime where λ < λ c ​ ( Δ ) \lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta) , a local Markov chain for sampling from the Gibbs distribution known as Glauber dynamics has an optimal mixing time of O ⁡ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) O(n\log n) . • In the non-uniqueness regime where λ > λ c ​ ( Δ ) \lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta) , the Glauber dynamics exhibits exponential mixing time; furthermore, the sampling problem becomes intractable unless 𝖱𝖯 = 𝖭𝖯 \mathsf{RP}=\mathsf{NP} . The computational complexity at the critical point λ = λ c ​ ( Δ ) \lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta) remains poorly understood, as previous algorithmic and hardness results all required a constant slack from this threshold. In this paper, we resolve this open question at the critical phase transition threshold, thus completing the picture of the computational phase transition. We show that for the hardcore model on graphs with maximum degree Δ ≥ 3 \Delta\geq 3 at the uniqueness threshold λ = λ c ​ ( Δ ) \lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta) , the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is upper bounded by a polynomial in n n , but is not nearly linear in the worst case: specifically, it falls between O ~ ​ ( n ( 2 + 4 ​ e ) + O ⁡ ( 1 / Δ ) ) \tilde{O}\left(n^{(2+4\mathrm{e})+O(1/\Delta)}\right) and Ω ⁡ ( n 4 / 3 ) \Omega\left(n^{4/3}\right) . For the Ising model (either antiferromagnetic or ferromagnetic), we establish similar results. For the Ising model on graphs with maximum degree Δ ≥ 3 \Delta\geq 3 at the critical temperature β \beta where | β | = β c ​ ( Δ ) |\beta|=\beta_{c}(\Delta) , with the tree-uniqueness threshold β c ​ ( Δ ) \beta_{c}(\Delta) defined by ( Δ − 1 ) tanh β c ( Δ ) = 1 (\Delta-1)\tanh\beta_{c}(\Delta)=1 , we show that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is upper bounded by O ~ ​ ( n 3 + O ⁡ ( 1 / Δ ) ) \tilde{O}\left(n^{3+O(1/\Delta)}\right) and lower bounded by Ω ⁡ ( n 3 / 2 ) \Omega\left(n^{3/2}\right) in the worst case. For the Ising model specified by a critical interaction matrix J J with ‖ J ‖ 2 = 1 \left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1 , we obtain an upper bound O ~ ​ ( n 3 / 2 ) \tilde{O}(n^{3/2}) for the mixing time, matching the lower bound Ω ⁡ ( n 3 / 2 ) \Omega\left(n^{3/2}\right) on the complete graph up to a logarithmic factor. Our mixing time upper bounds hold regardless of whether the maximum degree Δ \Delta is constant. These bounds are derived from a new interpretation and analysis of the localization scheme method introduced by Chen and Eldan [ 20 ] , applied to the field dynamics for the hardcore model and the proximal sampler for the Ising model. As key steps in both our upper and lower bounds, we establish sub-linear upper and lower bounds for spectral independence at the critical point for worst-case instances.



1
Introduction

One of the most remarkable critical phenomena in computation established over the past decades is the computational phase transition in two-spin systems at the uniqueness threshold.

The hardcore model is a quintessential example of a two-spin system that exhibits such a phase transition.
Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, and let
\mathcal{I}(G)
be the set of all independent sets of
G
.
The Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the hardcore model on
G
defines a distribution over
\mathcal{I}(G)
,
where each independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}(G)
is assigned a probability:

\displaystyle\mu(I)=\frac{\lambda^{|I|}}{Z},

where
\lambda>0
is a parameter known as the
fugacity
,
and
Z=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)}\lambda^{|I|}
is the normalizing constant, referred to as the
partition function
.

Given an instance of the hardcore model on a graph
G
with fugacity
\lambda>0
,
a fundamental problem is to generate random samples approximately from the associated Gibbs distribution
\mu
.
This problem is computationally equivalent to the approximate counting problem of estimating the partition function
Z
,
which has broad applications in statistical physics and statistical inference.
Intuitively, these computational tasks become more intractable as
\lambda
increases,
since finding large independent sets is known to be computationally challenging.

In a seminal work
[
28
]
, Dyer, Frieze, and Jerrum raised the question of the critical threshold for the fugacity
\lambda>0
beyond which an efficient sampler ceases to exist for hardcore models on graphs with maximum degree
\Delta
.
They questioned
1
1

1

To be precise, the original wording in
[
28
]
states: “One might even rashly conjecture (though we shall not do so) that this critical
\lambda
is the same as that marking the boundary between unique and multiple Gibbs measures in the independent set (hard core gas) model in the regular infinite tree of degree
\Delta
(the so-called Bethe lattice).”

whether this critical threshold for computation actually coincides with the tree uniqueness threshold
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}
,
which indicates the phase transition of the uniqueness of Gibbs measure on the infinite
\Delta
-regular tree,
as well as the decay of correlation within such a measure.

This beautiful computational phase transition regarding sampling from the hardcore model has been established in the past decades.
A significant step was made by Weitz
[
64
]
, who showed that if
\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
,
then there exists a polynomial-time deterministic approximation algorithm for the partition function on graphs with constant maximum degree
\Delta
.
Weitz’s result has been highly influential, stimulating numerous follow-up works,
including Barvinok’s algorithm based on Taylor’s theorem and the interpolation of zero-free polynomials
[
8
,
54
]
.
Due to standard self-reducibility, these counting results extend straightforwardly to approximate sampling.
Meanwhile, Sly
[
59
]
showed that in a neighborhood just beyond the uniqueness threshold
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, there is no polynomial-time algorithm for approximate counting or sampling unless
\mathsf{RP}=\mathsf{NP}
.
This intractability was later extended to the entire non-uniqueness regime
\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta)

[
58
,
37
]
.

While these near-critical results provide theoretical foundations for the computational phase transition of sampling,
the algorithms based on deterministic counting tend to be slow.
This is because these algorithms typically rely on enumerating connected substructures of logarithmic size.
As a result, while their running time is polynomial in the size of the graph
n
, the exponent typically depends on model parameters,
such as the maximum degree of the graph.

On the other hand,
randomized approaches for sampling and counting, such as Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithms, can be much more efficient.
Among these, Glauber dynamics, also called (random-scan) Gibbs sampling or heat-bath dynamics, is perhaps one of the simplest and most popular MCMC method for sampling from Gibbs distributions.
When applied to the hardcore model, if the current independent set is
I_{t}
, then the next one
I_{t+1}
is generated as follows:

1.

Choose a vertex
v
uniformly at random;

2.

If
I_{t}\cup\{v\}\notin\mathcal{I}(G)
, then set
I_{t+1}=I_{t}
;

3.

Otherwise, set
I_{t+1}=I_{t}\cup\{v\}
with probability
\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}
and otherwise set
I_{t+1}=I_{t}\setminus\{v\}
with the remaining probability.

It is well known that the Glauber dynamics is ergodic and converges to the target distribution
\mu
.
The mixing time characterizes the number of steps the chain requires to be sufficiently close to
\mu
.
Formally, it is defined as the minimum
t
such that the distribution of
I_{t}
is
(1/4)
-close to
\mu
in the total variation distance regardless of the initialization. See
Section

2.2
for details.

Despite the simplicity of Glauber dynamics,
establishing its mixing time can be extremely challenging.
For years, progress on rapid mixing in the near-critical regime
[
63
]
lagged behind that of deterministic counting algorithms.
Recently, however,
Anari, Liu and Oveis Gharan introduced a novel and powerful tool called
spectral independence
for analyzing Glauber dynamics
[
7
]
.
Since its introduction, the spectral independence method has seen great success in obtaining optimal (nearly linear) mixing time for a large family of models under various parameter regimes, see e.g.
[
23
,
47
,
11
,
3
,
20
,
18
,
19
,
22
,
21
,
25
,
4
]
.

Roughly speaking, the notion of spectral independence characterizes the decay of correlations in a spectral way so that the mixing of Glauber dynamics could be proved inductively.
Moreover, the spectral independence property (with the constant independent of
n
) occurs in the same regime as previously established correlation decay properties such as strong spatial mixing
[
64
]
or zero-freeness of partition function
[
54
]
.
In particular, all these nice properties happen if and only if the fugacity is below the critical value
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.

For the hardcore model, a fascinating and drastic transition in the mixing of Glauber dynamics for sampling from the Gibbs distribution
has been established at the uniqueness threshold:

•

Uniqueness regime

[
7
,
23
,
3
,
20
,
18
]
: For any graph of
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta
, if
\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, then the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is
O(n\log n)
,
where the constant in
O(\cdot)
depends only on the slackness of
\lambda
to
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.

•

Non-uniqueness regime

[
53
,
59
,
58
,
37
]
: Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exists an infinite family of graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
, such that for any graph from the family of
n
vertices, if
\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, then the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is
\exp(\Omega(n))
.
Furthermore, if
\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, there is no polynomial-time algorithm for approximate sampling on graphs with maximum degree
\Delta
unless
\mathsf{RP}=\mathsf{NP}
.

The critical behavior described above provides an almost complete picture for the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the hardcore model on graphs with maximum degree
\Delta
: as the parameter
\lambda
increases from the subcritical (uniqueness) regime to the supercritical (non-uniqueness) regime, the mixing time transitions from nearly linear to exponential, with the phase transition occurring at the critical point
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.
However, one important case remains unresolved: the sampling problem
at the uniqueness threshold
, where
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.

In fact, not only do we not know the mixing time of Glauber dynamics at criticality, we barely know anything rigorously, regarding either tractability or hardness, of the critical hardcore model.
All previous algorithms in the subcritical regime, whether deterministic or randomized, cease to be efficient when
\lambda
approaches
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
from below.
Similarly, the proofs of hardness in the supercritical regime in
[
58
,
37
]
fail when
\lambda
approaches
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
from above.
As far as we know, there are no rigorously efficient algorithms or established computational hardness for the critical hardcore model on general graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
or on random
\Delta
-regular graphs.

The situation is similar for the Ising model, another extensively studied two-spin system defined on graphs; see
Section

1.2
for the definition.
While we still know almost nothing about the critical Ising model on general graphs,
we have gained more insights into specific families of graphs.
For the ferromagnetic mean-field Ising model (i.e., on the complete graph), it has been shown that at the critical temperature, the Glauber dynamics mixes in time
\Theta(n^{3/2})

[
45
,
27
]
.
On the planar grid
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, Lubetzky and Sly showed that the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics for the critical Ising model is polynomial but not linear in the size of the graph
[
48
]
.
More recently, Bauerschmidt and Dagallier proved polynomial mixing time upper bounds in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 5

[
10
]
.
These results confirm the conjectures from statistical physics that the Ising model undergoes a critical slowdown: the mixing time evolves from nearly linear in the subcritical regime, to polynomial but not nearly linear at criticality, and finally to exponential in the supercritical regime.

We further note that these critical results for the Ising model on complete graphs or lattice do not carry over to the hardcore model.
In the hardcore model, both complete graphs and complete bipartite graphs are trivial at the critical point;
while on the planar grid, the exact value of the critical point remains unknown, with only numerical predictions available
[
12
]
.

1.1
Rapid mixing for the critical hardcore model

We establish polynomial mixing time for the hardcore model at critical fugacity.

Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected graph, and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
denote the fugacity.
Recall that the Gibbs distribution
\mu
for the hardcore model, supported on the collection of independent sets
\mathcal{I}(G)
, is given by:

\displaystyle\forall I\in\mathcal{I}(G),\quad\mu(I)\propto\lambda^{\left|I\right|}.

Let
\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}
be the critical fugacity for degree
\Delta\geq 3
.
For a graph
G
of maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
, the
critical hardcore model
on
G
is the hardcore model with critical fugacity
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.

Theorem 1.1

(Hardcore Model at the Critical Fugacity)
.

Consider the Glauber dynamics for the critical hardcore model.

•

(Upper bound)
For any graph
G
of
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical hardcore model on
G
is
O\left(n^{(2+4\mathrm{e})+\frac{4\mathrm{e}}{\Delta-2}}\log\Delta\right)
.

•

(Lower bound)
Fix a constant
\Delta\geq 3
. There exists an infinite family of graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
, such that for any graph
G
from the family of
n
vertices, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical hardcore model on
G
is
\Omega\left(n^{4/3}\right)
.

Our mixing time upper bound holds for any maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
,
regardless of whether
\Delta
is bounded by a constant.
Furthermore, our lower bound
\Omega\left(n^{4/3}\right)
refutes the optimal near linear mixing time,
effectively separating the critical case from the subcritical case.

1.2
Rapid mixing for the critical Ising model

Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected graph. Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
denote the inverse temperature, and let
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
denote the external fields. The Gibbs distribution for the Ising model is given by:

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{V},\quad\mu(x)\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{\intercal}A_{G}\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{h}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\right),

(1)

where
A_{G}
is the adjacency matrix of
G
. When
\beta>0
, the Ising model is called
ferromagnetic
, while it is called
antiferromagnetic
when
\beta<0
.

The tree-uniqueness threshold of the Ising model (with worst-case or zero external fields
\boldsymbol{h}
) is characterized by a critical inverse temperature
\beta_{c}(\Delta)
for every degree
\Delta\geq 3
, defined by:

(\Delta-1)\tanh\beta_{c}(\Delta)=1.

For a graph
G
of maximum degree
\Delta
, the
critical ferromagnetic (respectively, antiferromagnetic) Ising model
on
G
is the Ising model at critical temperature
\beta=\beta_{c}(\Delta)
(respectively,
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)
) with zero external fields.

An optimal mixing time of
O(n\log n)
has been established for the Glauber dynamics within the uniqueness regime when
|\beta|<\beta_{c}(\Delta)

[
3
,
23
,
20
]
.
And slow mixing can be similarly deduced from
[
53
,
59
,
58
,
37
]
when
|\beta|>\beta_{c}(\Delta)
.
We remark that while for the antiferromagnetic Ising model the sampling problem is computationally hard when
\beta<-\beta_{c}(\Delta)

[
59
,
58
,
37
]
, for the ferromagnetic Ising model there exists an efficient approximate sampler for all
\beta>0

[
38
,
56
]
.

We prove upper and lower bounds on the mixing time
of the Glauber dynamics for the Ising model at the critical temperature.

Theorem 1.2

(Ising Model at the Critical Temperature)
.

Consider the Glauber dynamics for the critical Ising model (either ferromagnetic or antiferromagnetic) with zero external fields.

•

(Upper bound)
For any graph
G
of
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical Ising model on
G
is
O\left(n^{3+\frac{4}{\Delta-2}}\log n\right)
;

•

(Lower bound)
Fix a constant
\Delta\geq 3
. There exists an infinite family of graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
, such that for any graph
G
from the family of
n
vertices, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical Ising model on
G
is
\Omega(n^{3/2})
.

Like in
Theorem

1.1
, our mixing time upper bound holds for any maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
possibly unbounded.
Furthermore, the upper bound also holds under arbitrary external fields, though the constant in big-O in the mixing time inevitably depends on the magnitude of the fields (this can be avoided by initializing the Glauber dynamics from a state aligned with the signs of the external fields
\boldsymbol{h}
).

Compared to
Theorem

1.1
for the hardcore model, the gap between the upper and lower bounds in
Theorem

1.2
is much smaller.
Notably, the exponent
3/2
in the lower bound aligns with the critical mean-field ferromagnetic Ising model on the complete graph
[
45
,
27
]
.

More generally, the Ising model can be specified by an
interaction matrix

J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
which is symmetric and positive semidefinite. The Gibbs distribution
\mu
is then defined as follows:

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},\quad\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{\intercal}J\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{h}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\right).

(2)

Here the assumption of
J
being positive semidefinite is without loss of generality, since the Ising model with interaction matrix
J+aI
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
is equivalent to the original one.
Note that
J=\beta A_{G}
recovers the previous definition (
1
) for the Ising model defined on a graph
G
.

Recent works have established
O(n\log n)
mixing time of Glauber dynamics when the interaction matrix satisfies
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}<1

[
30
,
3
,
20
]
.
Very recently, it was shown that the sampling and counting problems become intractable once
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}>1

[
41
,
35
]
.
These results suggest that the critical Ising model with general interaction matrices should correspond to the case where
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
.
Indeed, for the mean-field Ising model on the complete graph, where the interaction matrix is a rank-one matrix given by
J=\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}^{\intercal}
, the critical temperature occurs exactly at
\beta^{\prime}=1
, in which case
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
.

The following theorem establishes a nearly tight bound for the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics for the Ising model specified by an interaction matrix
J
with critical
2
-norm
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
.

Theorem 1.3

(Ising Model at the Critical Interaction Norm)
.

Consider the Glauber dynamics for the Ising model with interaction matrix
J
and zero external fields.

•

(Upper bound)
For any symmetric and positive semidefinite
J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the Ising model specified by
J
is
O\left(n^{3/2}\log n\right)
.

•

(Lower bound
[
45
]
)
The mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical mean-field Ising model specified by
J=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}^{\intercal}
is
\Omega(n^{3/2})
.

As discussed after
Theorem

1.2
, the mixing time upper bound in
Theorem

1.3
holds under arbitrary external fields as well.

Remark 1.4
.

Concurrently and independently to our work, Prodromidis and Sly
[
55
]
also showed polynomial mixing of the Glauber dynamics for the critical ferromagnetic Ising model. After posting our paper, we became aware that Bauerschmidt, Bodineau, and Dagallier had previously obtained a similar polynomial mixing result as
[
55
]
in their survey
[
9
, Example 6.19]
. Both
[
55
,
9
]
focused on the ferromagnetic Ising model on graphs by extending the approach from
[
10
]
. Besides, the mixing time upper bounds they obtained are large polynomials, while in our work we aim to obtain a sharp exponent as much as we can.

1.3
Deterministic counting at criticality

As discussed earlier, previous deterministic counting algorithms fail at the critical point including Weitz’s self-avoiding walk tree algorithm and Barvinok’s Taylor polynomial interpolation method.
We show that there exist simple deterministic counting algorithms for the partition function which work for the critical hardcore and Ising model, with a running time
\exp({O}(n^{c}))
for some constant
c<1
; in particular, the running time is sub-exponential in
n
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\Delta\geq 3
be a constant.
There exist deterministic algorithms for estimating the partition functions for the hardcore and Ising models at criticality on graphs of
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta
, achieving a relative error of
1\pm n^{-\Omega(1)}
in time that is sub-exponential in
n
.

We remark that one could attempt to utilize Weitz’s algorithm by replacing the exponential decay with the polynomial decay in the strong spatial mixing property, which would yield a significantly weakened approximation, such as a PTAS for the log-partition function (see
[
60
, Theorem 5.5]
).
However, in order to obtain an estimation within
O(n^{-1})
error bound, the depth of the truncated self-avoiding walk tree needs to be
\Omega(n^{2})
, which is even larger than the maximum depth of the self-avoiding walk tree.
Thus, it may fail to obtain a better algorithm than the brute-force enumeration by simply applying Weitz’s algorithm when at criticality.

Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In
Section

2
we give preliminaries.
In
Section

3
we present a new interpretation of localization schemes introduced in
[
20
]
with a new analysis for upper bounding the mixing time of Glauber dynamics.
In
Section

4
we establish
O(\sqrt{n})
spectral independence for the critical Ising model with interaction norm
1
, which allows us to obtain a sharper mixing time upper bound in this case.
We establish the lower bounds on the mixing time in
Section

5
.
Finally, we present deterministic counting algorithms in
Section

6
and prove
Theorem

1.5
.

Remark 1.6
.

In previous versions of the paper, as well as in the proceeding of the Annual ACM Symposium on Theory of Computing (STOC 2025), we claimed that the critical Ising model is
O(\sqrt{n})
-spectrally independent, in both the graphical setting with
|\beta|=\beta_{c}(\Delta)
and the general interaction setting with
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
. We later found a serious loophole in our proof for the graphical case, invalidating our claim of
O(\sqrt{n})
-spectral independence.
For this reason, our mixing result for the critical graphical Ising model states as
\tilde{O}\left(n^{3+O(1/\Delta)}\right)
, worse than the previous, falsely claimed
\tilde{O}\left(n^{2+O(1/\Delta)}\right)
upper bound.
While we still believe that
O(\sqrt{n})
-spectral independence should hold, we are unable to fix our previous proof or find an alternative approach.
Meanwhile, our results for the general interaction setting with
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}=1
remain unaffected and valid.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

Let
\mu
be a distribution over
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
and
\tau\in\{-1,+1\}^{\Lambda}
be a partial configuration on
\Lambda\subseteq[n]
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{\Lambda}=\tau\right]>0
. The distribution
\mu^{\tau}
follows from the law of
X\sim\mu
conditioned on
X_{\Lambda}=\tau
. For any
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the distribution
\boldsymbol{\lambda}*\mu
satisfies:

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},\quad(\boldsymbol{\lambda}*\mu)(\sigma)\propto\prod_{i\in[n]:\,\sigma_{i}=+1}\lambda_{i}\cdot\mu(\sigma).

(3)

The operation in (
3
) is usually called magnetizing a distribution with local fields
[
17
]
or tilting the measure by external field
[
3
]
in previous works.

2.2
Markov chains and mixing times

2.2.1
Basic definitions

Let
\Omega
be a finite state space, and
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain over
\Omega
with transition matrix
P
.

•

The Markov chain is
irreducible
if for all
x,y\in\Omega
, there exists
t\geq 0
with
P^{t}(x,y)>0
;

•

The Markov chain is
aperiodic
if
\gcd\{t\geq 1:P^{t}(x,x)\}=1
for all
x\in\Omega
.

The fundamental theorem of Markov chains shows that the Markov chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has a unique
stationary distribution
, i.e. a distribution
\mu
satisfying
\mu P=\mu
, if
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is irreducible and aperiodic.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain with transition matrix
P
and stationary distribution
\mu
. The
mixing time
measures the speed of the Markov chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converging to its stationary distribution
\mu
, which is formally defined as

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{mix}}:=\max_{x\in\Omega}\min\left\{t:d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(P^{t}(x,\cdot),\mu\right)<\frac{1}{4}\right\},

where
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the
total variational distance
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution supported over the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
. The Glauber dynamics on
\mu
updates a configuration
X
to
Y
according to the following rules:

1.

Select integer
1\leq i\leq n
uniformly at random;

2.

Sample
Y
from distribution
\mu
conditioning on
Y_{j}=X_{j}
for all
j\neq i
.

2.2.2
Functional inequalities

Let
\mu
be a distribution over a finite state space
\Omega
, and
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain over
\Omega
with the transition matrix
P
and the stationary distribution
\mu
. The
expectation
,
variance
,
entropy
, and the inner product is defined as follows:

•

Expectation: For all
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\sum_{x\in\Omega}\mu(x)f(x)
;

•

Variance: For all
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]-\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\right)^{2}
;

•

Entropy: For all
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\log f\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\log\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]
;

•

Inner product: For all
f,g:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mu}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\cdot g\right]
.

In particular, we will use the convention
0\log 0=0
in the definition of entropy.

Definition 2.1

(Poincaré inequality and modified log-Sobolev inequality)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain over
\Omega
with the transition matrix
P
and stationary distribution
\mu
.

1.

We say the
Poincaré inequality
holds with constant
\lambda>0
if

\displaystyle\forall f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\quad\lambda\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq\mathcal{E}_{P}(f,f),

where
\mathcal{E}_{P}(f,g):=\left\langle f,(I-P)g\right\rangle
is the
Dirichlet form
.

2.

We say the
modified log-Sobolev inequality
holds with constant
\rho>0
if

\displaystyle\forall f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad\rho\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq\mathcal{E}_{P}(f,\log f).

By the Courant-Fischer theorem, the Poincaré inequality holds with constant
\lambda
if and only if
\lambda\leq 1-\lambda_{2}(P)
, where
\lambda_{2}(P)
is the second largest eigenvalue of
P
, and
1-\lambda_{2}(P)
is the
spectral gap
.

Suppose the eigenvalues of
P
are non-negative. The
relaxation time

T_{\mathrm{rel}}
is defined as the inverse of the spectral gap, specifically
T_{\mathrm{rel}}=\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{2}(P)}
. By
[
44
, Theorem 12.4]
, the mixing time of
P
can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{mix}}\leq T_{\mathrm{rel}}\cdot\log\frac{4}{\mu_{\min}},\quad\text{where $\mu_{\min}:=\min_{x\in\Omega}\mu(x)$.}

With
modified log-Sobolev inequality
, a better mixing time bound can be achieved
[
13
]
:

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{mix}}\leq\frac{1}{\rho}\left(\log\log\frac{1}{\mu_{\min}}+3\right).

For distribution supported over the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
, we introduce the
approximate tensorization of variance and entropy
.

Definition 2.2

(Approximate tensorization of variance/entropy
[
14
]
)
.

Suppose a distribution
\mu
is supported over a subset
\Omega
of the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
. The distribution
\mu
satisfies the
approximate tensorization of variance
with constant
C
, if

\displaystyle\forall f\in\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\quad\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq C\cdot\sum_{v\in[n]}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[f\right]\right],

where
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[f\right]\right]:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{\mu^{X_{[n]\setminus\{v\}}}}\left[f\right]\right]
denotes the average of local variance.
Here, the conditional distribution
\mu^{X_{[n]\setminus\{v\}}}
is
Y\sim\mu
conditioned on the event that
X_{w}=Y_{w}
for all
w\in[n]\setminus\{v\}
.
Similarly, the distribution
\mu
exhibits the
approximate tensorization of entropy
with constant
C
, if

\displaystyle\forall f\in\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq C\cdot\sum_{v\in[n]}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}_{v}\left[f\right]\right].

where
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}_{v}\left[f\right]\right]
is defined accordingly.

We note that the definition of variance/entropy tensorization can be defined accordingly to general
\phi
-entropy (
\phi
-divergence).
We refer to
Definition

3.12
for details.

The approximate tensorization of variance/entropy is closely related to the Poincaré inequality and modified log-Sobolev inequalities for the Glauber dynamics on the target distribution
\mu
, and thereby implies the an upper bound for the mixing time of Glauber dynamics.

Lemma 2.3

(Folklore)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over a subset of the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
.

1.

The distribution
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of variance with constant
C
if and only if the Poincaré inequality for the Glauber dynamics on
\mu
holds with constant
\frac{1}{Cn}
.
As a corollary, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is bounded by
Cn\log\frac{4}{\mu_{\min}}
.

2.

If
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant
C
, then the modified log-Sobolev inequality for the Glauber dynamics holds with constant
\frac{1}{Cn}
. As a corollary, the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is bounded by
Cn\left(\log\log\frac{1}{\mu_{\min}}+3\right)
.

We refer to
[
23
, Fact 3.5 and Fact A.3]
for the proof of this lemma.

2.2.3
Spectral independence

Definition 2.4

(Spectral independence
[
7
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over a subset of the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
. Let
X\sim\mu
. The
influence matrix

\Psi_{\mu}
of
\mu
is defined as

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mu}(i,j)=\begin{cases}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{j}=+1\mid X_{i}=+1\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{j}=+1\mid X_{i}=-1\right]&\text{if }\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i}\right]\in(-1,1),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The distribution
\mu
is
C
-
spectrally independent
if
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq C
.
Furthermore, we say
\mu
is
C
-spectrally independent for all pinning
if for every feasible pinning
\tau
,
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu^{\tau}})\leq C
.

Remark 2.5
.

For a set
S\in 2^{[n]}
, we can represent it as some vector
X\in\left\{-1,+1\right\}^{n}
by setting
X_{i}=+1
for
i\in S
and
X_{j}=-1
for
j\not\in S
.
Hence, any distribution
\nu
on
2^{[n]}
can be considered as a distribution on
\left\{-1,+1\right\}^{n}
.
And the notion of spectral independence can be defined for the distribution
\nu
accordingly.

We list several equivalent definitions of spectral independence.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over a subset
\mathcal{X}
of the Boolean hypercube
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
. The following statements are equivalent.

1.

\mu
is
C
-spectrally independent;

2.

\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)\preceq C\cdot\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathrm{Var}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}\right]\right\}_{i\in[n]}
, where
\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)
is the covariance matrix of
\mu
defined as:

\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)_{i,j}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}\right]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{j}\right],

and we use the notation
A\preceq B
to indicate that
A-B
is negative semi-definite;

3.

For all functions
f=\frac{\nu}{\mu}
, where
\nu
is a distribution absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}p_{i}\left(\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}}-1\right)^{2}+(1-p_{i})\left(\frac{1-q_{i}}{1-p_{i}}-1\right)^{2}\leq C\cdot\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right],

(4)

where
q_{i}
denotes
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{X\sim\nu}\left[X_{i}=+1\right]
, and
p_{i}
denotes
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}=+1\right]
.

Remark 2.7
.

The third statement (
4
) is an analogue of the entropic independence introduced in
[
2
]
, which evaluates the “independence” of homogeneous distributions via the higher-order random walk
P_{n\leftrightarrow 1}=D_{n\to 1}U_{1\to n}
.

Remark 2.8
.

The condition
\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)\preceq C\cdot\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathrm{Var}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}\right]\right\}_{i\in[n]}
can be defined similarly for distributions
\mu
over
\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.
If
X\sim\mu
, then let
Y=2X-\boldsymbol{1}
, where
\boldsymbol{1}
is the all
1
vector.
Let the distribution of
Y
be
\nu
.
It is direct to see that
\nu
is a distribution over
\left\{-1,+1\right\}^{n}
.
We can also verify that
\mathrm{Cov}(\nu)=4\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)
and
\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim\nu}\left[Y_{i}\right]=4\mathrm{Var}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}\right]
for each
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

The equivalence of
Items

1
and
2
follows from the identity:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mu}(i,j)=\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)_{i,j}}{\mathrm{Var}_{X\sim\mu}\left[X_{i}\right]}.

(5)

The equivalence of
Items

1
and
3
requires the knowledge on high dimensional expander. We refer to
[
62
]
for the background.
Let
\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}
and
\nu^{\mathrm{hom}}
be the homogenization of
\mu
and
\nu
respectively, and
f^{\mathrm{hom}}=\frac{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}}{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}}
be the homogenization of
f
.
The left-hand-side of (
4
) is
n
times the variance of
\frac{\nu D_{n\to 1}}{\mu D_{n\to 1}}=U_{1\to n}f
, where
D_{n\to 1}
is the higher-order down walk from dimension
n
faces (subsets with
n
elements) to dimension
1
faces, and
U_{1\to n}
is its adjoint operator. Formally,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}p_{i}\left(\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}}-1\right)^{2}+(1-p_{i})\left(\frac{1-q_{i}}{1-p_{i}}-1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=n\mathrm{Var}_{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}D_{n\to 1}}\left[U_{1\to n}f^{\mathrm{hom}}\right]

\displaystyle=n\left\langle f^{\mathrm{hom}}-1,P_{n\leftrightarrow 1}\left(f^{\mathrm{hom}}-1\right)\right\rangle_{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}},

(6)

where
P_{n\leftrightarrow 1}=D_{n\to 1}U_{1\to n}
is the higher-order down-up walk. By Courant–Fischer theorem,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(P_{1\leftrightarrow n})=\lambda_{2}(P_{n\leftrightarrow 1})=\sup_{f^{\mathrm{hom}}:\{-1,+1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\frac{\left\langle f^{\mathrm{hom}}-1,P_{n\leftrightarrow 1}\left(f^{\mathrm{hom}}-1\right)\right\rangle_{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}}}{\mathrm{Var}_{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}}\left[f^{\mathrm{hom}}\right]}.

(7)

By
[
7
, Theorem 1.3]
, the second eigenvalue of the higher-order down-up walk
P_{1\leftrightarrow n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(P_{1\leftrightarrow n})=\frac{1}{n}+\frac{n-1}{n}\lambda_{2}(P^{\perp}_{1\leftrightarrow n})=\frac{1}{n}+\frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})-1\right)=\frac{1}{n}\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu}\right),

(8)

where
P_{1\leftrightarrow n}^{\perp}
denotes the non-lazy up-down walk of
P_{1\leftrightarrow n}
.
The equivalence of
Items

1
and
3
then follows from (
6
), (
7
), (
8
) and
\mathrm{Var}_{\mu^{\mathrm{hom}}}\left[f^{\mathrm{hom}}\right]=\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]
.
∎

2.3
Self-avoiding walk tree

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph.
Given a vertex
r\in V
, a self-avoiding walk
v_{0},v_{1},\cdots,v_{\ell}
from
r
(i.e.,
v_{0}=r
) is a sequence of vertices such that
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
are adjacent (
0\leq i<\ell
); and
v_{0},\cdots,v_{\ell-1}
are distinct vertices (but
v_{\ell}
may equal to some vertex in
v_{0},\cdots,v_{\ell-2}
).
Intuitively, the walk from
r
is forced to stop once it reaches some vertex that has been passed before.

Let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of a Ising model on the graph
G
with parameter
\beta
and inhomogeneous external field
(\lambda_{v})_{v\in V}
.
Fix an order
\prec
on
V
.
For every vertex
r\in V
, the self-avoiding walk (SAW) tree
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{G,r}
is a tree with pinning and external field
[
64
]
.
Each vertex
u
of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{G,r}
represents a self-avoiding walk
p_{u}
on
G
initiated from
r
.
The vertex
u
is usually considered as a copy of
v_{\ell}
(the last vertex in
p_{u}
); and therefore carries the same external field
\lambda_{v_{\ell}}
.
Vertices in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{G,r}
are organized as follow:

•

For vertices
u,v
in the SAW tree,
u
is a child of
v
if and only if
p_{u}
extends
p_{v}
(i.e.,
p_{v}
can be obtained by removing the last vertex in
p_{u}
).

•

For a leaf node
u
in the SAW tree with the path
p_{u}
be
v_{0},\cdots,v_{\ell}
, either degree of
v_{\ell}
in
G
is 1, or
v_{\ell}=v_{i}
for some
0\leq i\leq\ell-2
.
In the later case,
u
will have a pinning
+1
(resp.
-1
) if
v_{i+1}\prec v_{\ell-1}
(resp.
v_{i+1}\succ v_{\ell-1}
).

The pinnings, external fields, and parameter
\beta
then define a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
of Ising model on the SAW tree.
For each vertex
u
in the SAW tree, we also define
\mu_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}
as
\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
projected to the subtree rooted at
u
.

2.4
Large deviation and local limit theorem

The following local limit theorem plays a key role in our lower bound proofs.

Lemma 2.9

(
[
57
]
)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent and identically distributed random variables supported over a finite subset
\Omega=\{\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{1}\right]
and
\Sigma=\mathrm{Cov}\left(X_{1}\right)
.
Suppose
X_{1}
is not supported over any sublattice and
\mathrm{det}(\Sigma)>0
. There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
(only relies on
X_{1}
) such that the following holds for sufficiently large
n
:

For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3n^{2/3}
, it holds that

\displaystyle C_{1}\leq\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[S_{n}=\boldsymbol{x}\right]}{n^{-d/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}(\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu})^{T}\Sigma^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu})\right)}\leq C_{2}.

(9)

Furthermore, if
X_{1}
is symmetric, i.e.
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{x}\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{x}\right]
for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
, then for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3n^{3/4}
, the Gaussian approximation (
9
) holds.

Remark 2.10
.

The local limit theorem stated in
[
57
]
is of the following form:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[S_{n}=\boldsymbol{x}\right]}{\phi(\boldsymbol{x})}=\exp\left(n\sum_{k=3}^{+\infty}Q_{k}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}}{n}\right)\right)\left(1+O\left(\left\lVert\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}}{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)\right),

where
\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\mathrm{det}(n\Sigma)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}(\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu})^{T}\Sigma^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu})\right)
, the lattice point
\boldsymbol{x}
satisfying
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}=o(n)
, and
Q_{k}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
whose coefficients are determined by the distribution of
X_{1}
. This implies (
9
) when
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3n^{2/3}
. Furthermore, when random variables are symmetric,
Q_{3}
vanishes, allowing the Gaussian approximation (
9
) holds for a larger regime
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3n^{3/4}
. This follows from that the semi-invariants (the coefficients in the Taylor’s expansion of the logarithm of the moment generating function) of degree
3
of a symmetric random variable with mean
\boldsymbol{0}
are zeroes. As a corollary, the constant
3
in
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}-n\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3n^{2/3}
or
3n^{3/4}
can be replaced by arbitrary universal constant.

2.5
Birthday paradox

Our percolation proof for spectral independence utilizes the following birthday bound.

Lemma 2.11
.

Suppose
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent and identically distributed copies of a random variable
X
on
[n]
. Let
T
be the smallest index
i
with
X_{i}=X_{j}
for some
0\leq j<i
, then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[T\geq\ell\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2n}\right).

Proof.

Let
p_{k}=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X=k\right]
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[T\geq\ell\right]=\ell!\sum_{1\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{\ell}\leq n}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{x_{i}}.

(10)

To upper bound the elementary symmetric polynomial, we view it as a optimization program:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{\ell}\leq n}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{x_{i}}

(11)

\displaystyle\textrm{s.t.}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}=1.

By a standard application of the method of Lagrange multiplier, the maximum is achieved when
p_{1}=p_{2}=\ldots=p_{k}
and
p_{k+1}=\ldots=p_{n}=0
. Combining (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[T\geq\ell\right]\leq\max_{\ell\leq k\leq n}\ell!\binom{k}{\ell}\frac{1}{k^{\ell}}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\ell(\ell-1)}{2n}\right).

This concludes the proof.
∎

2.6
The probabilistic method

In our lower bound proofs, particularly for the constructions of hard instances,
we utilize the following non-standard application of the probabilistic method.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
X
and
Y
be random variables over a finite support
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}
where
Y>0
holds.
Then there exists a realization
(x,y)

of
(X,Y)
such that

\displaystyle\frac{x}{y}\geq\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[Y\right]}.

Proof.

Let
Z=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[Y\right]\cdot X-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\right]\cdot Y
. Then we have
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[Z\right]=0
.
By the probabilistic method, there must exist a realization
z
of
Z
and corresponding realization
(x,y)
of
(X,Y)
, such that
0\leq z=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[Y\right]\cdot x-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\right]\cdot y
, and
y>0
as
Y>0
. This implies
x/y\geq{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\right]}/{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[Y\right]}
.
∎

3
Upper Bounds

Our proof of upper bounds involves two steps.
In this section, we present a new proof of the localization scheme approach developed in
[
20
]
and upper bounds the mixing time by an integral of the spectral independence constant, a fact that has been implicitly implied in
[
20
]
.
Combining known bounds on the spectral independence and crude bounds near the critical point, we establish rapid mixing of Glauber dynamics for both critical hardcore and Ising models.
In
Section

4
, we establish spectral independence for the critical Ising model with a bound of
O(\sqrt{n})
.
This allows us to obtain a sharper exponent in the mixing time for the critical Ising model,
not far from the lower bound which will be proved in
Section

5
.

This section is organized as follows. In
Section

3.1
, we outline the proof of the mixing time upper bound for the hardcore model by interpreting the field dynamics as a continuous-time down-up walk. In
Section

3.2
, we introduce the information-theoretic view of localization schemes, including the field dynamics and proximal samplers as working examples. Finally, we apply this information-theoretic perspective to critical hardcore model and Ising model in
Sections

3.3
and
3.4
respectively.

3.1
Backgrounds and proof overview

3.1.1
Matroid, down-up walks, and field dynamics

Perhaps it is fair to say that the spectral independence approach and its successful and exciting applications all starts from the breakthrough in sampling basis of matroids
[
6
]
.
We begin by reviewing the method for analyzing the basis exchange process for sampling basis of matroids, also called the down-up walks.

Let
V
be a set of
n
elements.
We do not formally define matroids here but instead consider
\mu
to be a distribution supported on a family of
r
-subsets of
V
that satisfy nice properties, e.g. spanning trees of a given graph.
The down and up walks are naturally defined for such distributions.
For any
0\leq k\leq r
, we define the down walk
D_{r\to k}
as the Markov kernel from a given
r
-subset
T
to a random
k
-subset
S\subseteq T
by removing
r-k
elements from
T
chosen uniformly at random.
The up walk
U_{k\to r}
is the time-reversal for
D_{r\to k}
, i.e., the adjoint operator.
More precisely, if
\mu_{r}=\mu
is the original distribution over
r
-subsets and
\mu_{k}=\mu_{r}D_{r\to k}
is the resulted distribution over
k
-subsets from the down walk,
then the up walk
U_{k\to r}
is defined such that

\displaystyle\mu_{k}(S)U_{k\to r}(S,T)=\mu_{r}(T)D_{r\to k}(T,S)

(12)

for any
k
-subset
S
and
r
-subset
T
.
These Markov kernels are crucial for inductively proving rapid mixing of the down-up walk, defined by the kernel
P_{r\leftrightarrow(r-1)}=D_{r\to(r-1)}U_{(r-1)\to r}
.
One way to explain the idea of the proof is that we study the mixing property for the whole family of associated down-up walks
P_{r\leftrightarrow k}=D_{r\to k}U_{k\to r}
and use induction on
k
. Crucially, the inductive step requires one to study the mixing property of a local walk and the overall proof forms an elegant local-to-global argument
[
39
,
26
]
.

Meanwhile, if the distribution
\mu
we want to sample from is not supported on subsets of fixed size, such down and up walks are not suitable for the exact same analysis.
To get around this, Anari, Liu and Oveis Gharan
[
7
]
noticed a simple and clever way: They define a bijective mapping from subsets of
V
to
n
-subsets of
U=V\times\{0,1\}
, i.e., each subset
T\subseteq V
one-to-one corresponds to an
n
-subset
\{(v,\mathbbm{1}_{\{v\in T\}}):v\in V\}\subseteq U
.
Thus, any distribution
\mu
over subsets of
V
of any size can be understood as an equivalent distribution
\pi
over (legal)
n
-subsets of
U
.
The intuition behind this idea is homogenization of the associated partition function.
In particular, the down-up walk for
\pi
recovers the Glauber dynamics for sampling from
\mu
, and the local-to-global analysis also can be applied in a generalized form
[
1
]
, giving rise to the notion of spectral independence.

While the homogenization trick works nicely, the resulting mixing time has poor dependency on other parameters such as the maximum degree of the graph or the spectral independence constant.
To improve this, Chen, Feng, Yin and Zhang
[
17
]
developed a new approach. They introduced a new Markov chain named as field dynamics, and compared its mixing property with the Glauber dynamics.
Their approach can also be viewed as a homogenization but in a highly non-trivial way.
Later, the same authors showed that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is nearly linear in the uniqueness regime, independent of the maximum degree
[
18
]
.

At about the same time, Chen and Eldan
[
20
]
developed a new framework for proving mixing bounds for Markov
chains. Not only they established the same result for both field dynamics and Glauber dynamics as in
[
18
]
, their approach more generally applies to a large family of Markov chains, including for example down-up walks for basis of matroids and the proximal sampler for continuous (log-concave) distributions.
Chen and Eldan study the field dynamics and other Markov chains by defining and understanding the associated localization schemes, a measure-valued stochastic process with martingale behaviors.
The approach developed in
[
20
]
relies heavily on tools from stochastic calculus.

One of the contributions of this work, from our point of view, is to present a more intuitive explanation of the localization scheme method from
[
20
]
.
Our starting point is the following key observation:
the field dynamics is the down-up walk run in continuous time
.
Thus, previous approaches for analyzing down-up walks on fixed-size subsets can be naturally carried over to, for example, the hardcore model over independent sets of any size.
In particular, we are able to recover previous results on field dynamics
[
18
,
20
]
and furthermore obtain new mixing results all the way up to the critical point, and our proof does not require much knowledge from stochastic calculus.

3.1.2
Field dynamics is continuous-time down-up walk

Let us present more details on this observation.
For any
\theta\in[0,1]
, we define
D_{1\to\theta}
to be the Markov kernel that, given a subset
T
, generates a random subset
S\subseteq T
by independently including each element of
T
into
S
with probability
\theta
.
Namely, each element in
T
flips a coin independently to decide if it should be kept or removed.
We note that the Markov kernel
D_{1\to\theta}
is a natural analog of the (discrete-time) down walk
D_{r\to k}
introduced earlier (i.e., removing
r-k
elements uniformly at random).

Let
\mu_{1}=\mu
be the original distribution, and
\mu_{\theta}=\mu_{1}D_{1\to\theta}
be the distribution resulted from the down walk. Note that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[|S|\right]=\theta\,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{1}}\left[|T|\right]
, and thus
\theta
can be understood as the expected relative size of the subset (analogous to
\mu_{k}
on
k
-subsets).

These Markov kernels
D_{1\to\theta}
in fact describes a stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
on
2^{V}

which we simply refer to it as the
continuous-time down walk
.
It is formally defined as follows:
X_{0}
is a random subset generated from
\mu_{1}
, and for each
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle X_{t}=\{v\in V:v\in X_{0}\wedge r_{v}>t\}

(13)

where
\{r_{v}\}_{v\in V}
is a collection of independent random variables uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
.
In particular,
X_{1}=\varnothing
.
In other words, we place a clock at each element
v
in
X_{0}
which rings at a random time
r_{v}
independently and uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
, and when the clock rings
v
is removed.
The continuous-time down walk
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a decreasing process and a time-inhomogeneous Markov process.
We observe that for any
t\in[0,1]
,
it holds

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{t}=S\mid X_{0}=T\right]=D_{1\to(1-t)}(T,S),

and the distribution of
X_{t}
is exactly
\mu_{1-t}
.

As in the analysis for matroids, we need the analogous notions for the up walks.
Mathematically, they are just Markov kernels
U_{\theta\to 1}
adjoint to the down walk kernels
D_{1\to\theta}
(i.e., satisfying an analog of (
12
)).
However, it would be more intuitive to understand them through the time reversal of the continuous-time down walk, which we call the
continuous-time up walk
and denote it by
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
.
Specifically, a path
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
of the continuous-time up walk can be generated by sampling
Y_{1}
from
\mu_{1}
, and putting independent clocks at each element in
Y_{1}
, so that when the clock at
v
rings we add
v
to the current set.

From the general Markov process theory, the continuous-time up walk
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
is also a time-inhomogeneous Markov process and, crucially, it depends on the target distribution
\mu_{1}
(observe that this feature is also shared by the matroid up walks).
It is an increasing process and its final state
Y_{1}
is distributed as
\mu_{1}
.
Moreover, both
Y_{\theta}
and
X_{1-\theta}
have the same distribution
\mu_{\theta}
.
The transitions of
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
are described by the Markov kernels
U_{\theta\to 1}
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y_{1}=T\mid Y_{\theta}=S\right]=U_{\theta\to 1}(S,T).

In particular,
D_{1\to\theta}
and
U_{\theta\to 1}
are adjoint to each other.

Given the Markov kernels
D_{1\to\theta}
and
U_{\theta\to 1}
for the continuous-time down and up walks respectively, we are now ready to define the
continuous-time down-up walk
, whose Markov kernel is given by
P_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=D_{1\to\theta}U_{\theta\to 1}
where
\theta\in[0,1]
is a parameter.
A moment of thought reveals that the continuous-time down-up walk consists of two steps, where in the first step we drop each element from the current subset independently with probability
1-\theta
, and in the second step we try to add back elements according to a suitable conditional distribution.
It turns out that this is
exactly
the field dynamics introduced in
[
17
]
.
In other words, what field dynamics does is exactly running the down-up walk but in continuous time.

With this understanding in mind, to analyze mixing properties of the field dynamics, we could simply follow the inductive approach taken for analyzing the (discrete-time) down-up walk.
The only distinction is that the inductive step involving taking differences of
P_{r\leftrightarrow k}
for adjacent
k
’s now becomes taking derivative of
P_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}
with respect to
\theta
.

We will give a complete proof of rapid mixing in
Section

3.2
soon assuming entropic stability.
Here we only explain the high-level idea.
Let
\nu
be a distribution over
2^{V}
.
To prove rapid mixing of the field dynamics, we need to show that the
\chi^{2}
or KL divergence
D(\cdot\,\|\,\cdot)
from
\nu
to
\mu
contracts along the continuous-time down walk.
Recall that
\mu_{\theta}=\mu D_{1\to\theta}
and let

\nu_{\theta}=\nu D_{1\to\theta}
.
We wish to show that for all
\theta\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle D\left(\nu_{\theta}\,\|\,\mu_{\theta}\right)\leq(1-\gamma(\theta))D\left(\nu\,\|\,\mu\right),

(14)

where
\gamma(\theta)
describes the contraction rate.
Rapid mixing of the field dynamics follows immediately from (
14
).
The local-to-global argument essentially corresponds to upper bounding the derivative of the divergence
D\left(\nu_{\theta}\,\|\,\mu_{\theta}\right)
with respect to
\theta
. Namely, if we can prove for some
\alpha(\theta)
that

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}D\left(\nu_{\theta}\,\|\,\mu_{\theta}\right)\leq\alpha(\theta)\left(D\left(\nu\,\|\,\mu\right)-D\left(\nu_{\theta}\,\|\,\mu_{\theta}\right)\right),

(15)

then by Grönwall’s inequality, (
14
) immediately follows.
The inequality (
15
) is basically the entropic stability condition introduced in
[
20
]
, a notion generalizing both spectral and entropic independence.

3.2
An information-theoretic view of localization schemes

In this subsection, we aim to provide an intuitive interpretation of the localization schemes introduced in
[
20
]
,
requiring minimal knowledge of stochastic calculus.
Our approach can be seen as the information-theoretic view of stochastic localization
[
29
]
, extended also to the field dynamics.

This section is organized as follows.
In
Section

3.2.1
we study localization schemes by introducing a pair of noising and denoising Markov processes which are time reversals of each other, and
in
Section

3.2.2
we define the associated Markov kernels as well as the Markov chains induced by them; our running examples are the field dynamics in the discrete space
2^{V}
(applied to the hardcore model) and the proximal sampler in the continuous space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(applied to the Ising model).
In
Section

3.2.3
, we introduce the approximate conservation of entropy which implies, for example, rapid mixing of the field dynamics and proximal sampler, and furthermore rapid mixing of the Glauber dynamics under mild extra assumptions.
Finally, in
Section

3.2.4
we define entropic stability introduced in
[
20
]
(stated slightly differently) which implies approximate conservation of entropy; this implication can be viewed as a continuous analog of the local-to-global analysis for matroid down-up walks.

3.2.1
Noising and denoising processes

Let
\mu
represent the target distribution over a state space
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, from which we seek to generate random samples.
For simplicity we assume
\mathcal{X}
is
finite
in this paper, which is sufficient for our applications in hardcore and Ising models.

Noising Process.

Consider a stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
taking values in
\mathcal{X}
,
where
X_{0}
is drawn from the target distribution
\mu
.
This process progressively modifies the distribution over time, and is referred to as the
noising process
(also called
forward process
or
down walk
under various settings).
In this paper, we focus on the continuous-time case where
\mathcal{T}=[0,1]
, though extending this to the discrete-time case, where
\mathcal{T}=\{0,1,\dots,T\}
,
T\in\mathbb{N}
, is straightforward.
The stochastic process
(X_{t})
we consider here is a
Markov process
, though it is generally
not
time-homogeneous.
For simplicity we assume the law of
X_{1}
is a Dirac measure concentrated at
\boldsymbol{0}
; in other words,
(X_{t})
always arrives at the origin at the end of the process.

Denoising Process.

Now, consider the time reversal of
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, denoted by
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
.
The time-reversed process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
is also a
Markov process
, though, like the original process, it is generally
not
time-homogeneous.
We refer to
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
as the
denoising process
(also called
backward process
or
up walk
under various settings); crucially, it depends on
\mu
, the distribution of
Y_{1}
.
Note that we have the distributional equality
Y_{\theta}\overset{d}{=}X_{1-\theta}
.
The initial point
Y_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
is set to the origin, which corresponds to
X_{1}
in the noising process.
This denoising process is called an
observation process
in
[
52
]
and is more important in the analysis.

Examples of noising and denoising processes.

Before moving on, we look at some examples first.
The important noising/denoising processes that will be considered in this paper is the continuous-time down/up walk and the Brownian bridge/Föllmer processes.

Definition 3.1

(continuous-time down and up walks)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over
2^{V}
where
V=[n]
.
The
continuous-time down walk

(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with initial distribution
X_{0}\sim\mu
is a continuous-time stochastic process constructed as follows.
Each
v\in V
is associated with an independent random variable
r_{v}
, uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, the process is defined by:

X_{t}=\{v\in V:v\in X_{0}\wedge t<r_{v}\}.

As
t
increases, elements are progressively removed from
X_{t}
, until eventually
X_{1}=\varnothing
.

The associated denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
, i.e., the
continuous-time up walk
will be defined as a revealing or observing process.
Let
Y_{0}=\varnothing
and
Y_{1}\sim\mu
be sampled in the beginning.
Each vertex
v\in V
is associated with an independent random variable
s_{v}
, uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
.
Then, for each
\theta\in[0,1]
, the process is defined by:

\displaystyle Y_{\theta}=\left\{v\in V:v\in Y_{1}\land\theta>s_{v}\right\}.

As
\theta
increases, the elements in
Y_{1}
is revealed progressively.

We remark that the continuous-time up walk
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
is a Markov process since it is the time reversal of
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, even though the way we describe it does not indicate this.

The continuous time down/up walks are defined in the discrete space
2^{V}
for set families.
We now consider the analog of them in the continuous space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
\mu
be the target distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
In the discrete case of set families, the continuous-time down walk starts from a random subset, gradually removes elements, and eventually reaches the empty set at time
t=1
.
In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a natural analog of the empty set is the origin point
\boldsymbol{0}
, and starting from a random point
X\sim\mu
, we wish to design a random continuous path (i.e., a stochastic process) connecting
X
and the origin
\boldsymbol{0}
.
This motivates us to consider the
(standard) Brownian bridge process
, which is the (standard) Brownian motion
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
conditioned on
B_{1}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
The following standard fact about the Brownian bridge process is helpful to us.

Fact 3.2
.

Let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
denote the standard Brownian motion.
Then the continuous-time stochastic process
(B^{\mathrm{br}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
defined as

B^{\mathrm{br}}_{t}=(1-t)B_{\frac{t}{1-t}}

is a standard Brownian bridge process whose probability distribution is the conditional distribution of
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
subject to the condition that
B_{1}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Similarly, the process
(B^{\mathrm{br}^{\prime}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
defined as

B^{\mathrm{br}^{\prime}}_{t}=tB_{\frac{1-t}{t}}

is also a standard Brownian bridge process.

We now define the continuous-space analog of down and up walks using Brownian bridges.
For the noising process, we run the Brownian bridge but further assuming starting at a random point (generated from
\mu
plus a pushforward step); we still call it the Brownian bridge process for simplicity.
For the denoising process, we recover the Föllmer process and stochastic localization (with a fixed driving matrix).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(B^{\mathrm{br}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a
r
-dimensional standard Brownian bridge.
Let
\mu
be an initial distribution over
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and let
L\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be a driving matrix.
The
Brownian bridge process

(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with driving matrix
L

starts at
X_{0}\sim\mu
, and for all
t\in(0,1]
, evolves as

\displaystyle X_{t}=(1-t)LX_{0}+B^{\mathrm{br}}_{t}.

(16)

At time
t=0
, it jumps from
X_{0}
to
LX_{0}
.
Then, as
t
increases, it evolves from
LX_{0}
to
\boldsymbol{0}
via a Brownian bridge.

Let
(B_{\theta}^{\mathrm{br^{\prime}}})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
be a
r
-dimensional standard Brownian bridge.
The
Föllmer process

(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}

starts at
Y_{0}=0
and is defined by
first sample
Y_{1}\sim\mu
and for
\theta\in[0,1)
, it evolves as

\displaystyle Y_{\theta}=\theta LY_{1}+B^{\mathrm{br^{\prime}}}_{\theta}.

(17)

As
t
increases, it evolves from
\boldsymbol{0}
to
LY_{1}
.
And, finally, when
\theta=1
, it jumps from
LY_{1}
to
Y_{1}
.

The Föllmer process
[
33
,
34
]
has attracted a lot of attention in recent years and is especially helpful in establishing various functional inequalities, see for examples
[
43
,
31
,
32
,
50
,
40
]
.
The Föllmer process is also known to be closely related to stochastic localization, see
[
49
, Lemma 4.1]
.
Here our treatment of the Föllmer process is slightly informal.
For a standard and formal definition, one needs to write down the stochastic differential equation and show the solution has the same law as the stochastic process defined by (
17
).
For our purposes, however, we only need the existence of the Föllmer process as a Markov process and the explicit forms of Markov kernels associated with it (defined in the next subsection). This is sufficient for us to derive rapid mixing of associated Markov chains and further the Glauber dynamics.

Remark 3.4
.

By
Fact

3.2
, the Brownian bridge process (
16
) can be equivalently represented as

\displaystyle X_{t}=(1-t)\left(LX_{0}+B_{\frac{t}{1-t}}\right).

Taking
\tilde{X}_{t}=(1+t)X_{\frac{t}{1+t}}
for
t\in(0,\infty)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{t}=LX_{0}+B_{t},

(18)

which is a more common representation in literature for the noising process.
Similarly, the Föllmer process (
17
) can be equivalently represented as

\displaystyle Y_{\theta}=(1-\theta)\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}LY_{1}+B_{\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}}\right).

Taking
\tilde{Y}_{t}=(1+t)Y_{\frac{t}{1+t}}
for
t\in(0,\infty)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{t}=tLY_{1}+B_{t},

(19)

which is again a more common representation in literature for the denoising process or stochastic localization
[
29
,
40
]
.
We remark that
Eq.

19
is called the
linear observation process
in
[
52
]
.

3.2.2
Associated Markov kernels and Markov chains

Given a pair of noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
, we study the Markov kernels associated with them and define a family of discrete-time Markov chains from these kernels.
These Markov chains are not only important themselves, but also allow us to derive rapid mixing of the Glauber dynamics.
A summary of noising and denoising processes discussed in this paper, and their associated Markov chains is provided in
Table

1
.

Markov chain

Field dynamics

(
Definition

3.6
)

Proximal sampler

(
Definition

3.7
)

Domain
2
2

2

Though we only apply the proximal sampler to discrete distributions, it is applicable to continuous-space distributions.

2^{[n]}

Null information state
3

3

The state that
X_{1}
equals almost surely.

(
Definition

3.1
)

(

3.3
)

(

3.1
)

(

3.3
)

Table 1
:
Two examples of localization schemes: Field dynamics and Proximal sampler

Markov Kernels.

Both the noising process
(X_{t})
and the denoising process
(Y_{\theta})
can be characterized by a collection of double-indexed Markov kernels
(P_{\theta\to\eta})_{0\leq\eta\leq\theta\leq 1}
and their time reversals
(Q_{\eta\to\theta})_{0\leq\eta\leq\theta\leq 1}
.
For any
0\leq\eta\leq\theta\leq 1
, the following holds:

\displaystyle P_{\theta\to\eta}(x,\cdot):=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\eta}\in\cdot\mid X_{1-\theta}=x\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y_{\eta}\in\cdot\mid Y_{\theta}=x\right];

\displaystyle Q_{\eta\to\theta}(x,\cdot):=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y_{\theta}\in\cdot\mid Y_{\eta}=x\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\theta}\in\cdot\mid X_{1-\eta}=x\right].

Observe that for all
\theta\in[0,1]
, it holds
Q_{\theta\to\theta}=P_{\theta\to\theta}=\mathrm{id}
, and for all
0\leq\eta\leq\zeta\leq\theta\leq 1
, it holds
Q_{\eta\to\theta}=Q_{\eta\to\zeta}Q_{\zeta\to\theta}
and
P_{\theta\to\eta}=P_{\theta\to\zeta}P_{\zeta\to\eta}
.

Remark 3.5
.

The measure-valued stochastic process
\left(Q_{\theta\to 1}(Y_{\theta},\cdot)\right)_{\theta\in[0,1]}
where
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
is the denoising process, corresponds to the
localization process

(\nu_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
in
[
20
]
. Specifically, when
\theta=0
, the measure
Q_{\theta\to 1}(Y_{\theta},\cdot)=Q_{0\to 1}(\boldsymbol{0},\cdot)
is always the target distribution
\mu
since
Y_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
; and when
\theta=1
, the measure
Q_{\theta\to 1}(Y_{\theta},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{Y_{1}}
is a random Dirac measure where
Y_{1}
is distributed as
\mu
.
However, it is non-trivial to rigorously verify that this is indeed a localization process.
We remark that in
[
20
]
the notion of localization processes is defined abstractly and generally, without the presence of noising and denoising processes.

Densities.

Recall that
X_{0}
is generated from the target distribution
\mu
.
Let
\mu_{\theta}
denote the law of
Y_{\theta}\overset{d}{=}X_{1-\theta}
(so
\mu_{1}=\mu
); that is,
\mu_{\theta}:=\mu_{1}P_{1\to\theta}
.
More generally, it holds

\displaystyle\mu_{\theta}P_{\theta\to\eta}=\mu_{\eta}\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\eta}Q_{\eta\to\theta}=\mu_{\theta}.

We remark that
Q_{\eta\to\theta}
and
P_{\theta\to\eta}
are adjoint to each other, i.e., for any two integrable functions
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, it holds
\left\langle f,P_{\theta\to\eta}g\right\rangle_{\mu_{\theta}}=\left\langle Q_{\eta\to\theta}f,g\right\rangle_{\mu_{\eta}}
.

Markov Chains.

The noising and denoising processes allow us to define a family of discrete-time Markov chains associated with them.
For any
\theta\in[0,1]
, we define the Markov kernel

\displaystyle K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=P_{1\to\theta}Q_{\theta\to 1}.

Since
P_{1\to\theta}
and
Q_{\theta\to 1}
are adjoint to each other, the Markov kernel
K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}
is reversible with respect to
\mu=\mu_{1}
.
Observe that the chain
P_{1\to 1}=\mathrm{id}
does not move at all, while the chain
P_{1\to 0}
moves to the stationary distribution
\mu
in a single step.

Examples of associated Markov chains.

The examples of noising and denoising processes can be used to define families of discrete-time Markov chains as described above.
Here, we do short calculations to see how the continuous-time down-up walks recover the field dynamics
[
17
]
, and the Brownian bridge and Föllmer processes recover the proximal sampler
[
42
]
.

The continuous-time down-up walk is exactly the field dynamics introduced in
[
17
]
.
We adopt the continuous-time down walk as the noising process starting from
X_{0}\sim\mu
.
For
\theta\in[0,1]
. The Markov kernel of this process,
P_{1\to\theta}(S,\cdot)
, independently discards each element
v\in S
with probability
1-\theta
. Correspondingly, the Markov kernel
Q_{\theta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
of the continuous-time up walk samples a subset
T\supseteq S
with probability proportional to
(1-\theta)*\mu(T)
, where
(1-\theta)*\mu
is obtained by magnetizing
\mu
with local fields
1-\theta
(see (
3
)). In particular, we have:

\displaystyle\forall T\subseteq[n],\quad\left((1-\theta)*\mu\right)(T)\propto(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|}\cdot\mu(T).

(20)

Specifically, the Markov kernel for this denoising process is given by:

\displaystyle Q_{\theta\to 1}(S,T)\propto\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{0}=T\text{ and }X_{1-\theta}=S\right]=\mu(T)(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}\theta^{\left|S\right|},

which means
Q_{\theta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
is the distribution
(1-\theta)*\mu
conditioned on all
v\in S
being occupied.
The continuous-time down-up walk
K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=P_{1\to\theta}Q_{\theta\to 1}
then recovers the field dynamics, formally defined as below.

Definition 3.6

(Field dynamics,
[
17
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over
2^{V}
, where
V
is a finite ground set. Let
\theta\in(0,1)
be a parameter. The field dynamics with parameter
\theta
is a Markov chain with stationary distribution
\mu
.
Given a subset
T\in 2^{V}
, it updates
T
to a new subset
T^{\prime}
as:

1.

(Noising Step) Remove each
v\in T
with probability
1-\theta
independently, and denote the resulting subset by
S\subseteq T
;

2.

(Denoising Step) Draw a new subset
T^{\prime}\in 2^{V}
from the distribution
(1-\theta)*\mu
conditioned on
T^{\prime}\supseteq S
.

The Brownian bridge and Föllmer processes recover the proximal sampler introduced in
[
42
]
.
Consider the Brownian bridge process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with driving matrix
L\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
that starts from
X_{0}\sim\mu
.
For a fixed
\theta\in[0,1)
and configuration
\boldsymbol{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}
, the noising process
P_{1\to\theta}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
first adds Gaussian noise to vector
L\boldsymbol{x}
and then scales it by a factor of
\theta
. The Gaussian noise follows a multivariate Gaussian distribution with covariance matrix
\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}I_{r}
, where
I_{r}
is the
r\times r
identity matrix. For any state
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, the Föllmer process
Q_{\theta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{y},\cdot)
, by definition, satisfies

\displaystyle Q_{\theta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle\propto\mu(\boldsymbol{z})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\theta(1-\theta)}\left(\theta L\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{y}\right)^{\intercal}\left(\theta L\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\propto\mu(\boldsymbol{z})\exp\left(-\frac{\theta}{2(1-\theta)}\boldsymbol{z}^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{z}+\frac{1}{1-\theta}\boldsymbol{y}^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{z}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{X}.

The Markov kernel
K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=P_{1\to\theta}Q_{\theta\to 1}
corresponds to the proximal sampler defined below.

Definition 3.7

(Proximal sampler,
[
42
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over a finite state space
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
\theta\in(0,1)
be a parameter and
L\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be a matrix.
The proximal sampler with parameter
\theta
and driving matrix
L
is a Markov chain with stationary distribution
\mu
.
Given a vector
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, it updates
\boldsymbol{x}
to a new vector
\boldsymbol{z}
as:

1.

(Noising Step) Sample
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
from
\mathcal{N}(\theta L\boldsymbol{x},\theta(1-\theta)I_{r})
, which is the Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
with mean
\theta L\boldsymbol{x}
and covariance
\theta(1-\theta)I_{r}
;

2.

(Denoising Step) Sample a new vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{X}
from the distribution
\nu
defined over
\mathcal{X}
as:

\displaystyle\nu(\boldsymbol{z})\propto\mu(\boldsymbol{z})\exp\left(-\frac{\theta}{2(1-\theta)}\boldsymbol{z}^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{z}+\frac{1}{1-\theta}\boldsymbol{y}^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{z}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{X}.

We now apply the proximal sampler to the Ising model.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{\pm 1\}^{n}
and let distribution
\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{h}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\right)
for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{X}
be the Gibbs distribution of the Ising model with interaction matrix
J=L^{\intercal}L
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}
.
Take the same matrix
L
as the driving matrix in the proximal sampler.
Then for any
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\theta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)\boldsymbol{z}^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{h}_{\star}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{z}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{z}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}

where
\boldsymbol{h}_{\star}=\boldsymbol{h}+\frac{1}{1-\theta}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{y}
;
that is,
Q_{\theta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{y},\cdot)
is the Gibbs distribution of another Ising model.
In particular, if we take
\theta=1/2
then
Q_{\theta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\propto\exp\left(\boldsymbol{h}_{\star}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{z}\right)
becomes a product distribution; this essentially recovers the Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation, see e.g.
[
5
]
.
Therefore, under this setting the proximal sampler takes a pleasant form.
From
\boldsymbol{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
it moves to
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
by:

1.

(Noising Step) Sample
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
from
\mathcal{N}(L\boldsymbol{x},I_{r})
;

2.

(Denoising Step) Sample a new vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
from the product distribution
\nu
defined as:

\displaystyle\nu(\boldsymbol{z})\propto\exp\left((\boldsymbol{h}+L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{y})^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{z}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{z}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}.

3.2.3
Approximate conservation of entropy

The framework of localization scheme provides a rich toolbox for analyzing the mixing time of Markov chains.
In this section, we will introduce these tools aligning with the context of information-theoretic view for localization scheme.

Relative Entropy.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function.
Then, for a convex function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, the (relative)
\phi
-entropy
functional is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\textstyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\phi(f)\right]-\phi(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]).

Remark 3.8
.

Typical
\phi
-entropy functionals are variance and relative entropy.
Specifically:

•

When
\phi(x)=x^{2}
, the
\phi
-entropy corresponds to the variance functional

\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]=\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f^{2}\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]^{2}.

•

When
\phi(x)=x\log x
, the
\phi
-entropy corresponds to relative entropy functional

\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]=\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}\left[f\right]:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\log f\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\log\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right].

For
\theta\in[0,1]
, define
f_{\theta}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as
f_{\theta}:=Q_{\theta\to 1}f
(so
f_{1}=f
); that is,
f_{\theta}(x)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]
.
Observe that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]
.
More generally, for all
0\leq\eta\leq\theta\leq 1
, it holds that

\displaystyle Q_{\eta\to\theta}f_{\theta}=f_{\eta}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\theta\to\eta}f_{\eta}=f_{\theta}.

The following law of total entropy for

\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right]=\textstyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[\phi(f_{\theta})\right]-\phi(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right])

plays a key role.

Lemma 3.9

(Law of Total Entropy)
.

For any
0\leq\eta\leq\theta\leq 1
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right]=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\eta}}\left[f_{\eta}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\eta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(x,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]\right].

Entropy Conservation and Tensorization.

A key aspect of the theory of localization scheme is its ability to establish an approximate conservation of entropy.

Definition 3.10

(Approximate Conservation of Entropy)
.

Given a time
\theta\in[0,1]
and a parameter
K>0
,
a noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with
X_{0}\sim\mu
is said to satisfy

K
-approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy from time
\theta
(or equivalently, the corresponding denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
satisfies
K
-approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy up to time
\theta
)
if, for all
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq K\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right],

where
Q_{\cdot\to\cdot}
represents the Markov kernel of the corresponding denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
.

The approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy, when established for suitable
\phi
,
can imply the Poincaré or modified log-Sobolev inequalities for the Markov chains
K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=P_{1\to\theta}Q_{\theta\to 1}
,
thereby leading to rapid mixing of these chains.
For the specific noising and denoising processes we consider,

K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}
may be either the field dynamics or the proximal sampler.
Therefore, the corresponding approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy may imply the rapid mixing of these samplers.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function.
Suppose there exists a noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
X_{0}\sim\mu
, and for a time
\theta\in[0,1]
, the process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
satisfies
(1/\kappa)
-approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy from time
\theta
.
Then, the corresponding Markov kernel
K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}=P_{1\to\theta}Q_{\theta\to 1}
admits
\phi
-entropy decay with rate
\kappa
.
That is, for every function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have
