\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}f\right]\leq(1-\kappa)\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right].

(21)

Lemma

3.11
is a simple consequence of the law of total entropy.
To prove
Lemma

3.11
, we will consider
\phi
-entropy with respect to random variables.
Let
F,G
be two random variables on the same probability space such that
F
is
R_{\geq 0}
-valued.
We can define the
\phi
-entropy and conditional
\phi
-entropy on
F
and
G
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right]

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(F)\right]-\phi(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\right]),

(22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid G\right]

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(F)\mid G\right]-\phi(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid G\right]).

(23)

Proof of
Lemma

3.11
.

Let
X_{0}\sim\mu
,
Y_{\theta}\sim P_{1\to\theta}(X_{0},\cdot)
and
Y_{1}\sim Q_{\theta\to 1}(Y_{\theta},\cdot)
be random variables in the same probability space.
Let
F=f(Y_{1})
, and by the conservation of entropy, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa}\left(\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right]-\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\right]\right),

(law of total entropy)

which, after rearranging terms, implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\right]\leq(1-\kappa)\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right].

By another application of the law of total entropy, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\mid X_{0}\right]\right]+\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\mid X_{0}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\mid X_{0}\right]\right]=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[K_{1\leftrightarrow\theta}f\right],

(non-negativity of
\phi
-entropy)

where the last inequality is also known as the data processing inequality in information theory.
∎

When applied to the analysis of Glauber dynamics,
another important consequence of the approximate conservation of entropy is a “lifting” of the mixing properties in a sub-critical regime up to the critical threshold.
In particular, these mixing properties are captured by the abstraction of approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy,
which, with appropriate choices of
\phi
, corresponds to the Poincaré or modified log-Sobolev inequalities for Glauber dynamics.

Definition 3.12

(Approximate Tensorization of Entropy)
.

Given a parameter
C>0
, a distribution
\mu
, supported on
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, is said to satisfy
C
-approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy
if, for all
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu^{x_{-i}}}\left[f\right]\right],

where
x_{-i}
denotes the partial configuration
x([n]\setminus\left\{i\right\})
.

The notion of approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy is closely related to the mixing time of Glauber dynamics.

•

Variance case
:
When
\phi(x)=x^{2}
,
the
\chi^{2}
-entropy corresponds to variance.
In this context, the approximate tensorization of variance with parameter
C
is equivalent to the well-known Poincaré inequality with constant
C
, which leads to a
1/(Cn)
lower bound on the spectral gap of the Glauber dynamics.

•

Entropy case
:
When
\phi(x)=x\log x
, where the
\phi
-entropy corresponds to the relative entropy.
Here, the approximate tensorization of entropy indicates a decay of relative entropy at a rate of
1/(Cn)
.
This decay of entropy further implies an upper bound on the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics of the form
Cn(\log\log 1/\mu_{\min})
, where
\mu_{\min}=\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)
.

The following lemma formalizes a “lifting” of the approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy within the context of the noising-denoising framework we adopt.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function.
Suppose there exists a noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
X_{0}\sim\mu
,
and for a time
\theta\in[0,1]
, the following conditions hold:

1.

The noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
satisfies
K
-approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy from time
\theta
.

2.

The distribution
Q_{\theta\to 1}(X_{1-\theta},\cdot)
always satisfies
C
-approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy.

Then, the distribution
\mu
satisfies
CK
-approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy.

This “lifting” phenomenon has been observed in various contexts in prior work.
Notably, it includes the approximate tensorization of entropy through uniform block factorization of entropy
[
23
, Lemma 2.3]
, the boosting theorem of field dynamics
[
17
, Theorem 1.9]
, and annealing via stochastic localization
[
20
, Section 4]
.
All these results can be unified within the abstract framework of localization schemes (see
[
20
]
) and are thereby encompassed by
Lemma

3.13
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.13
.

We re-express the conditions of
Lemma

3.13
using the
\phi
-entropy for random variables defined in (
22
) and (
23
).

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function, and let
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
be the corresponding denoising process, which is the time-reversed process of the noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
.
Note that
Y_{1}\sim\mu
.
We define
F:=f(Y_{1})
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=Y_{1}([n]\setminus\left\{i\right\})
for each
i\in[n]
.
Thus,
F,\mathcal{P}_{i},Y_{\theta}
are random variables on the same probability space.
In particular, we have
Y_{\theta}\sim\mu_{\theta}
and
(Y_{1}\mid Y_{\theta})\sim Q_{\theta\to 1}(Y_{\theta},\cdot)
.

Then, the approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy, as required in
Item

1
of
Lemma

3.13
, can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right]

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\right].

(24)

Similarly, the approximate tensorization of
\phi
-entropy, as required in
Item

2
of
Lemma

3.13
, means that the following always holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta}\right]\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid\mathcal{P}_{i},Y_{\theta}\right]|Y_{\theta}\right].

(25)

Plugging (
25
) into (
24
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\right]

\displaystyle\leq CK\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid\mathcal{P}_{i},Y_{\theta}\right]|Y_{\theta}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=CK\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid\mathcal{P}_{i},Y_{\theta}\right]\mid\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]\right],

(26)

where the equality follows from the law of total expectation.

We apply the law of total entropy (
Lemma

3.9
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta},\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]\mid\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]-\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F\mid Y_{\theta},\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]\mid\mathcal{P}_{i}\right]\leq\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}\left[F\mid\mathcal{P}_{i}\right],

(27)

where the last inequality follows from the non-negativity of
\phi
-entropy.

Finally, we conclude the proof by combining (
26
) and (
27
).
∎

3.2.4
Entropic stability

The following concept of entropic stability quantifies the rate of local conservation of
\phi
-entropy in a noising/denoising process.

Definition 3.14

(Entropic Stability)
.

Given a function
C:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
we say the target distribution
\mu
is
\phi
-entropically stable with rate
C
with respect to a noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
where
X_{0}\sim\mu
if, for any
\eta\in[0,1]
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\eta^{+}}\frac{1}{\theta-\eta}\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(x,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]\leq\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right],

(28)

where
Q_{\cdot\to\cdot}
represents the Markov kernel of the corresponding denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
, which is the time-reversed process of
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Remark 3.15
.

When
\phi=x^{2}
,
\mu
is said to be
spectrally stable
with rate
C
; when
\phi=x\log x
,
\mu
is said to be
entropically stable
with rate
C
.

The notion of the entropic stability is closely related to the convexity of the
\phi
-entropy functional.
Let
\mathcal{E}(\theta)=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right]
for
\theta\in[0,1]
.
If we take expectation for
x\sim\mu_{\eta}
on both side of (
28
), then, by the law of total entropy, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\eta)\leq C(\eta)\cdot\frac{\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\eta)}{1-\eta},

which is equivalent to the convexity of function
\mathcal{E}
when
C(\eta)=1
.
In this perspective, (
28
) can be seen as a relaxed version of convexity.

Theorem 3.16
.

If the target distribution
\mu
is
\phi
-entropically stable with rate
C
with respect to a noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
where
X_{0}\sim\mu
,
then for every
\theta\in[0,1]
,
it satisfies
K
-approximate conservation of
\phi
-entropy from time
\theta
,
where

K=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\mathrm{d}\eta\right).

That is,
for all
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right].

(29)

where
Q_{\cdot\to\cdot}
represents the Markov kernel of the corresponding denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
.

Proof.

Fix
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f\right]=1
.
Let us define for
\theta\in[0,1]
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\theta)=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right].

In particular,
\mathcal{E}(1)=\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu}\left[f\right]
and
\mathcal{E}(0)=0
.
Also,
\mathcal{E}
is monotone increasing by the data processing inequality (this can be easily derived from
Lemma

3.9
).
For any
\eta\in[0,1)
we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta\to\eta^{+}}\frac{1}{\theta-\eta}\left(\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\theta}}\left[f_{\theta}\right]-\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\eta}}\left[f_{\eta}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta\to\eta^{+}}\frac{1}{\theta-\eta}\,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\eta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(x,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]\right]

(
Lemma

3.9
(Law of total entropy))

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\eta}}\left[\lim_{\theta\to\eta^{+}}\frac{1}{\theta-\eta}\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(x,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\eta}}\left[\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\eta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right]

(
(C,\phi)
-Entropic Stability)

\displaystyle=\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\left(\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{1}}\left[f_{1}\right]-\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{\mu_{\eta}}\left[f_{\eta}\right]\right)

(
Lemma

3.9
(Law of total entropy))

\displaystyle=\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\left(\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\eta)\right).

We can apply Grönwall’s inequality to obtain the theorem, or more directly

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}(1)}{\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\theta)}\right)=\int_{0}^{\theta}\left(\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\eta}\log\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\eta)}\right)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\eta=\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\eta)}{\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\leq\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

Observe
\mathcal{E}(1)-\mathcal{E}(\theta)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}^{\phi}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right]
by the law of total entropy. The theorem follows.
∎

3.3
Hardcore model and field dynamics

We now apply the abstract framework developed in the previous sections to analyze the Glauber dynamics for the Gibbs distribution of the critical hardcore model.
The noising process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and the denoising process
(Y_{\theta})_{\theta\in[0,1]}
considered in this context is the
continuous-time down/up walk
(see
Definition

3.1
).
In particular, for
\eta\in[0,1]
, recall the distribution
(1-\eta)*\mu
as defined in (
20
).

The following lemma establishes a connection between
\chi^{2}
-entropic (i.e., spectral) stability with respect to the continuous-time down walk and spectral independence. We refer the readers to a recent work
[
16
]
for more discussion on this topic.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over
2^{[n]}
.
If there exists a function
C:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that for every
\eta\in[0,1]
, the distribution
(1-\eta)*\mu
is
C(\eta)
-spectrally independent for all pinning,
then
\mu
is spectrally stable with rate
C
with respect to the continuous-time down walk
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
where
X_{0}\sim\mu
.

The proof of
Lemma

3.17
is deferred to
Section

A.1
.

The following bounds on spectral independence and the approximate tensorization of variance (i.e., a Poincaré inequality) have been established for the Gibbs distribution of the hardcore model.

Lemma 3.18

(
[
24
]
)
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\Delta\geq 3
,
the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the hardcore model on any graph with a maximum degree at most
\Delta
and fugacity
\lambda\leq(1-\delta)\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
is
\frac{4\mathrm{e}(1+\frac{1}{\Delta-2})}{\delta}
-spectrally independent for all pinning. Formally, for any pinning
\tau
, the influence matrix satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu^{\tau}}\right)\leq\frac{4\mathrm{e}(1+\frac{1}{\Delta-2})}{\delta}.

We remark that a crude bound (
32/\delta
-spectral independence) was established in
[
24
]
.
Meanwhile, it is direct to get the bound stated in
Lemma

3.18
by going through the same proof with a more careful analysis.
We leave important details into
Appendix

C
.

Lemma 3.19

(
[
15
]
)
.

For any
\Delta\geq 3
,
the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the hardcore model on any graph
G=(V,E)
with a maximum degree at most
\Delta
and fugacity
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2\Delta}
satisfies
C
-approximate tensorization of variance with constant
C=2
(see
Definition

3.12
for a formal definition).

Proof of the upper bound in
Theorem

1.1
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with sufficiently many vertices, specifically
n=|V|\geq 1000
, and maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
.
Let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of the hardcore model on
G
with fugacity
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.
By
Theorem

3.16
, for all
\theta\in[0,1]
, the distribution
\mu_{\theta}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall f:\mathcal{I}(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right].

Note that for any independent set
I
in
G
, the distribution
Q_{\eta\to 1}(I,\cdot)
is the Gibbs distribution of hardcore model with fugacity
\lambda_{\eta}=(1-\eta)\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

3.17
,

\mu
is spectrally stable with rate
C
with respect to the continuous-time down walk
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
where
X_{0}\sim\mu
,
where
C:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\eta\in[0,1],\quad C(\eta)=\min\left(\frac{c^{\star}}{\eta},n\right),

with
c^{\star}=4\mathrm{e}(1+\frac{1}{\Delta-2})
. The bound
C(\eta)\leq n
follows from the crude upper bound
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq n
for spectral independence. Hence, when
\theta>c^{\star}/n
, the approximate conservation of variance holds with constant

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(n\cdot\int_{0}^{c^{\star}/n}\frac{1}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta+\int_{c^{\star}/n}^{\theta}\frac{c^{\star}}{\eta(1-\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(2c^{\star}+c^{\star}\log\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}+c^{\star}\log\frac{n}{c^{\star}}\right).

Fix
\theta=\frac{9}{10}
. It satisfies
\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)=O\left(n^{c^{\star}}\right)
. Combining
Lemma

3.19
and
Lemma

3.13
, the distribution
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of variance with constant
O\left(n^{c^{\star}}\right)
.

According to
Lemma

2.3
, this result gives an
\Omega(1/n^{1+c^{\star}})
lower bound on the spectral gap of the Glauber dynamics, which implies an
O\left(n^{2+c^{\star}}\right)
upper bound on the mixing time.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Although our proof of the mixing time for the critical hardcore model (
Theorem

1.1
) is constructed within the general framework of the (information-theoretical view of the) localization scheme,
it is noteworthy that a similar (though cruder) polynomial upper bound can also be obtained by applying the original machinery in
[
17
]
without resorting to localization schemes.

Given a graph
G
with maximum degree
\Delta
, let
\mu_{\lambda}
be the Gibbs distribution of the hardcore model on
G
with fugacity
\lambda
.
Let
\mathrm{gap}(\mu_{\lambda})
denote the spectral gap of the Glauber dynamics for
\mu_{\lambda}
.
Applied to the hardcore model,
[
17
, Theorem 1.9]
guarantees that if
\lambda\leq(1-\delta)\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, then for every
\theta\in(0,1)
, the following inequality holds
4
4

4

The base of the exponential in
[
17
, Theorem 1.9]
is
\theta/2
. But it can be improved to
\theta
by going through the proof carefully.
The extra factor
1/2
arises primarily from
[
17
, Theorem 2.5]
, which provides a crude lower bound of
(\frac{\ell}{2n})^{2\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil+1}
for the quantity
(\frac{\ell-\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil}{n-\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil})^{2\left\lceil\eta\right\rceil+1}
.
From the setting of
[
17
, Theorem 1.9]
, we have
\ell/n=\theta
, and both
\ell
and
n
are made sufficiently large.
Therefore, this bound can be improved.

\displaystyle\mathrm{gap}(\mu_{\lambda})\geq\theta^{C(\delta)}\min_{\tau}\mathrm{gap}(\mu^{\tau}_{\theta\lambda}),

(30)

where
\mu_{\theta\lambda}
is the Gibbs distribution of the hardcore model on
G
with fugacity
\theta\lambda
; the minimum is taken over all possible pinnings
\tau
; and
C(\delta)
is a function of
\delta
(roughly
\min\left\{n,c^{\circ}/\delta\right\}
for some universal constant
c^{\circ}>c^{\star}
).
This implies that we can define a function
f(t):=\min_{\tau}\mathrm{gap}(\mu^{\tau}_{(1-t)\lambda_{c}})
, and from (
30
), we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\log f(t+h)-\log f(t)}{h}\leq\limsup_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{C(t)\log\frac{1-t}{1-t-h}}{h}=\frac{C(t)}{1-t}.

Let
\theta=9/10
, by
Lemma

3.19
, we have
f(\theta)\geq(2n)^{-1}
.
By “integrating” w.r.t.
\log f
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{gap}(\mu_{\lambda_{c}})\geq f(0)\geq f(\theta)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C(t)}{1-t}\mathrm{d}t\right)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

3.4
Ising model and proximal sampler

We now move on to the critical Ising model.
The noising/denoising process considered in this context is the
Brownian bridge process
and
Föllmer process
, which are defined in
Definition

3.3
.

The following theorem establishes the approximate tensorization of entropy for the Gibbs distribution of the Ising model via spectral independence.

Theorem 3.21

(
[
20
, Theorem 49]
)
.

Let
J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a positive semi-definite matrix.
If there exists a function
C:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all external fields
\boldsymbol{h}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t\in[0,1]
,
the distribution
\mu_{(1-t)J,\boldsymbol{h}^{\star}}
is
C(t)
-spectrally independent,
then for any external fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the Gibbs distribution
\mu=\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
satisfies the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant
\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)
.
5
5

5

We remark that the integral in
[
20
, Theorem 49]
differs slightly from the one presented here. The distinction arises from the different definitions of the Ising model. In their work, they define the Ising model specified by an interaction matrix
J
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}
as

\forall\boldsymbol{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},\quad\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\propto\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{\intercal}J\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{h}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\right).

Theorem

3.21
was originally proved in
[
20
]
using the framework of stochastic localization,
with the proof relying extensively on stochastic calculus.
Here, we re-establish the result through our noising/denoising interpretation of localization schemes,
providing a more elementary proof.
To this end, we introduce the following lemma, which connects entropic stability with spectral independence.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
J=L^{\intercal}L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a positive semi-definite matrix.
If there exists a function
C:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that for all external fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\eta\in[0,1)
,
the distribution
\mu_{J_{\eta},\boldsymbol{h}}
, where
J_{\eta}=(1-\frac{\eta}{1-\eta})J
, is
C(\eta)
-spectrally independent,
then
for any
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the distribution
\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
is entropically stable with rate
\frac{\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}}{1-\eta}\cdot C
with respect to the Brownian bridge process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with driving matrix
L
where
X_{0}\sim\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
.

The proof of
Lemma

3.22
is deferred to
Section

A.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.21
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the Brownian bridge process starting from the Gibbs distribution
\mu=\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
with driving matrix
L\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
, where
J=L^{\intercal}L
.
By
Lemma

3.22
,
\mu
is entropically stable with rate
\frac{\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}}{1-\eta}\cdot C^{\star}
with respect to
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, where the function
C^{\star}:[0,1/2]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is given by:

\displaystyle C^{\star}(\eta)=C\left(\frac{\eta}{1-\eta}\right).

Let
\theta=1/2
. By
Theorem

3.16
, we have the following conservation of entropy:

\displaystyle\forall f:\{-1,+1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}\left[f\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C^{\star}(\eta)}{1-\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{\theta}\frac{C\left(\eta/(1-\eta)\right)}{(1-\eta)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right)\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mu_{\theta}}\left[\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f\right]\right].

Recall that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the distribution
Q_{\theta\to 1}(x,\cdot)
is a product distribution over
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
when
\theta=1/2
(see the discussion after
Definition

3.7
), which exhibits the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant
1
. Together with
Lemma

3.13
, we conclude the proof of
Theorem

3.21
.
∎

3.4.1
Mixing at the critical temperature

To prove the mixing time upper bound in
Theorem

1.2
, we need the following spectral independence result for sub-critical Ising models.

Lemma 3.23

(
[
24
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph with
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta\geq 3
,
and let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of the Ising model on
G
with inverse temperature
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
.
If
(\Delta-1)\tanh\left|\beta\right|\leq 1-\delta
for some
\delta>0
, then
\mu
is
\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-1}\frac{1}{\delta}
-spectrally independent.

We now proceed to prove the upper bound in
Theorem

1.2
.

Proof of the upper bound in
Theorem

1.2
.

By symmetry, we only consider the
\beta>0
case.
When
\beta<0
, the theorem can be proved by essentially the same proof.
Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with maximum degree
\Delta
and
n
vertices.
Let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of the Ising model on
G
with inverse temperature satisfying
\beta=\beta_{c}
and with arbitrary fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By definition, the interaction matrix
J
is given by
J=\beta_{c}\left(\Delta I+A_{G}\right)\succeq 0
, where
A_{G}
is the adjacency matrix of
G
.
By
Theorem

3.21
and
Lemma

3.23
, and applying a change of variables, the distribution
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant
\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)
, where
C:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is given by

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,1],\quad C(t)=\min\left(\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-1}\cdot\frac{1}{1-(\Delta-1)\tanh\left(\beta_{c}t\right)},n\right).

By a straightforward calculations, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-(\Delta-1)\tanh\left(\beta_{c}t\right)}\cdot\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(2\beta_{c}t\right)+1}{\Delta-(\Delta-2)\exp\left(2\beta_{c}t\right)}\cdot\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta-1}+\frac{2\exp\left(2\beta_{c}t\right)}{\Delta-(\Delta-2)\exp\left(2\beta_{c}t\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta-1}+\frac{2}{\Delta-2}\cdot\frac{1}{\exp\left(2\beta_{c}(1-t)\right)-1},

(31)

where in the last equation, we use the fact that
\exp(2\beta_{c})=\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
from the definition of the critical inverse temperature.
Thus, the integral
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta_{c}\Delta\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 2\beta_{c}\Delta+2\beta_{c}\Delta\int_{0}^{1-1/n}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\left(\text{change of variable and~\eqref{eq:integral-partial}}\right)\quad

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta_{c}\Delta+\frac{2\beta_{c}\Delta}{\Delta-1}+\frac{4\beta_{c}\Delta}{\Delta-2}\cdot\int_{1/n}^{1}\frac{1}{\exp\left(2\beta_{c}t\right)-1}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{\leq}2\beta_{c}\Delta+\frac{2\beta_{c}\Delta}{\Delta-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{4\beta_{c}\Delta}{\Delta-2}\cdot\frac{\log\left(\exp(2\beta_{c})-1\right)-\log\left(\exp\left(2\beta_{c}/n\right)-1\right)}{2\beta_{c}}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}2\beta_{c}\Delta+\frac{2\beta_{c}\Delta}{\Delta-2}+\frac{4\Delta}{\Delta-2}+\frac{2\Delta\log n}{\Delta-2}

\displaystyle(\beta_{c}\Delta\leq 3)\quad

\displaystyle\leq 24+\frac{2\Delta\log n}{\Delta-2}.

Here, the inequality
(\star)
follows from
\int\frac{1}{\exp\left(2\beta t\right)-1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\log\left(\exp\left(2\beta t\right)-1\right)}{2\beta}-t+C
and
(*)
follows from the inequalities
\log\left(\exp(2\beta_{c})-1\right)\leq\log(2\mathrm{e}^{2}\beta_{c})
where we use
0\leq\beta_{c}\leq 1
, and
-\log\left(\exp\left(2\beta_{c}/n\right)-1\right)\leq-\log\left(2\beta_{c}/n\right)=\log n-\log(2\beta_{c})
.
Therefore, the Gibbs distribution
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant

\displaystyle\exp\left(\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\leq\exp\left(24+\frac{2\Delta\log n}{\Delta-2}\right)=O\left(n^{2+\frac{4}{\Delta-2}}\right).

This indicates that the Glauber dynamics on the Gibbs distribution of the critical Ising model mixes within time
O\left(n^{3+\frac{4}{\Delta-2}}\log n\right)
.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

Despite of the success in analyzing Glauber dynamics for the critical hardcore model,
the current approach based on
field dynamics may not be suitable for the critical Ising model.
Specifically, the Ising model with the critical inverse temperature
\beta_{c}(\Delta)
and a slightly biased external field
\exp\left(h\right)=1-\delta
exhibits a spectral independence bound of
\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\right)
, in contrast to the
O\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
-spectral independence as in the hardcore model. This results in a sub-exponential mixing time upper bound by simply applying a similar integration
but in the context of field dynamics.

3.4.2
Mixing at the critical interaction norm

We now consider the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the Ising model specified by an interaction matrix
J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
where
J
is symmetric and positive semi-definite.
Let
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the external fields.
The Gibbs distribution
\mu=\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},\quad\mu(\boldsymbol{x})\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{\intercal}J\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{h}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{x}\right).

(32)

We present a stronger upper bound than that stated in
Theorem

1.3
, extending the same
O\left(n^{3/2}\log n\right)
mixing time bound beyond criticality by a margin of
O\left(1/\sqrt{n}\right)
.

Theorem 3.25
.

For any symmetric matrix
J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that
0\preceq J\preceq\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}\right)I
for some constant
\alpha\geq 0
, and for any
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics for
\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}

is
O(n^{3/2}\log n)
.

In the mean-field Ising model, it has been shown in
[
27
]
that the Glauber dynamics mixes in time
\Theta(n^{3/2})
when the inverse temperature
\beta
lies in a
\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
-length window centered at its critical threshold.
Theorem

3.25
indicates a similar phenomenon for Ising models with general interaction matrices.

To prove
Theorem

3.25
, it suffices to consider the critical Ising model specified by a rank-one interaction matrix,
and the general case follows from the needle decomposition
[
2
, Corollary 30]
and
[
30
]
which decomposes the Ising model of interest into a mixture of rank-one Ising models satisfying nice properties.
First, we introduce the following lemma that establishes spectral independence for the critical rank-one Ising model.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
J=\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a rank-one matrix, and let
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Consider the Gibbs distribution
\mu=\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
of the Ising model with interaction matrix
J
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}
.

1.

(
[
2
, Proposition 31]
)
If
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1-\delta
for some
\delta>0
, then
\mu
is
\frac{1}{\delta}
-spectrally independent.

2.

If
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}
for some constant
\alpha\geq 0
, then
\mu
is
O_{\alpha}(\sqrt{n})
-spectrally independent.

The sub-critical case (
Item

1
) has already been established in
[
2
, Proposition 31]
.
We provide an alternative proof for this case, which is also generalizable to the critical point (
Item

2
).
This proof of
Lemma

3.26
is detailed in
Section

4
.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

3.25
. The proof follows a structure analogous to that of
Theorem

1.2
, so we will omit certain calculations that are similar to those previously discussed.

Proof of
Theorem

3.25
.

Let
n
be sufficiently large, specifically
n\geq 10\alpha^{2}
. Let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of the Ising model specified by the rank-one interaction matrix
J=\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By
Theorem

3.21
and
Lemma

3.26
, and applying a change of variables, the distribution
\mu
satisfies the approximate tensorization of entropy with constant
\exp\left(\int_{0}^{1+\alpha/\sqrt{n}}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)
, where
C:[0,1+\alpha/\sqrt{n}]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is given by

\displaystyle C(t)=\begin{cases}\min\left(\frac{1}{1-t},K\sqrt{n}\right)&t\in[0,1],\\
K\sqrt{n}&t\in[1,1+3\alpha/\sqrt{n}].\end{cases}

Here,
K\geq 0
is a sufficiently large constant. By straightforward calculations, we have

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{0}^{1+\alpha/\sqrt{n}}C(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\leq\exp\left((\alpha+1)K+\frac{1}{2}\log n\right)=O\left(n^{1/2}\right).

This implies that the Glauber dynamics of any rank-one Ising model with
\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}
has an
\Omega(n^{-1/2})
modified log-Sobolev constant. Together with the needle decomposition
[
2
, Corollary 30]
6
6

6

We remark that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}
should be the correct criteria in the statement of the needle decomposition.
, the Glauber dynamics of any Ising model with
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}
has an
\Omega(n^{-1/2})
modified log-Sobolev constant, thereby implying an
O\left(n^{3/2}\log n\right)
mixing time.
∎

4
O(\sqrt{n})
-Spectral Independence at Criticality

In this section, we consider the Ising model where criticality is characterized by the norm of the rank-one interaction matrix
J=\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{u}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.
In this context, we prove
Lemma

3.26
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.26
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
u_{i}\neq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Let
D=\mathrm{diag}\left\{u_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
be the diagonal matrix with
D_{ii}=u_{i}
.
Since
u_{i}\neq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, it holds that
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})=\lambda_{\max}(D^{-1}\Psi_{\mu}D)
; and we will bound
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})=\lambda_{\max}(D^{-1}\Psi_{\mu}D)
by

\displaystyle\left\lVert D^{-1}\Psi_{\mu}D\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|u_{i}\right|^{-1}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\left|u_{j}\right|\left|\Psi_{\mu}(i,j)\right|.

(33)

Let
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{i}
be the self-avoiding walk tree (recall its definition at
Section

2.3
) rooted at element
1\leq i\leq n
, and let
\mu_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}
be the corresponding Gibbs distribution on
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{i}
.
By (
33
) and the preservation of total influence in the self-avoiding walk tree
[
24
, Lemma 8]
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|u_{i}\right|^{-1}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\left|u_{j}\right|\left|\Psi_{\mu}(i,j)\right|\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|u_{i}\right|^{-1}\sum_{v\in V\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{SAW}}_{i}\right)}\left|u_{v}\right|\cdot\left|\Psi_{\mu_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}}(i,v)\right|.

(34)

Fixing the starting element
1\leq i\leq n
, the vertices
v
in the self-avoiding walk tree
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{\mathrm{SAW}}
correspond one-to-one to tuples
(e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})
with
e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
being pairwise distinct and
e_{0}=i
. Furthermore, by the conditional independence of influence
[
24
, Lemma 15]
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{T_{i}^{\mathrm{SAW}}}(i,v)\right|=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|\Psi_{T_{i}^{\mathrm{SAW}}}(e_{i-1},e_{i})\right|\leq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|\tanh(u_{e_{i-1}}u_{e_{i}})\right|\leq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|u_{e_{i-1}}\right|\left|u_{e_{i}}\right|.

Together with (
34
), we can bound the maximum eigenvalue of the influence matrix as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in V^{\ell}\\
e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}\text{ are pairwise distinct, }e_{0}=i\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|u_{e_{i}}\right|^{2}.

(35)

Let
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables on
[n]
such that
x_{i}
takes value
k
with probability proportional to
u_{k}^{2}
, and
T
is the first index with
e_{T}=e_{j}
for some
0\leq j<T
.
Then, by (
35
),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[T\geq\ell\right]\cdot\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2\ell}.

(36)

Now, we prove
Item

1
of
Lemma

3.26
. Assume
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1-\delta
. From (
36
), we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2\ell}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}.

Next, we prove
Item

2
of
Lemma

3.26
. Assume
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}
for some constant
\alpha\geq 0
.
By
Lemma

2.11
, the right-hand side of (
36
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[T\geq\ell\right]\cdot\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2\ell}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{(\ell-1)^{2}}{2n}\right)\cdot\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq 1+2\sqrt{n}\cdot\underbrace{\sum_{k\cdot\sqrt{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\right)\cdot\exp\left(\alpha k\right)}_{I_{n}}.

(37)

Note that
I_{n}
converges to
\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp\left(\alpha t\right)\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Thus, from (
36
) and (
37
), the maximum eigenvalue of the influence matrix
\Psi_{\mu}
is
O(\sqrt{n})
. This concludes the proof.
∎

5
Lower Bounds

In this section, we prove the lower bounds on the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for both the critical hardcore and Ising models, as stated in
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
.

These lower bounds on mixing time are derived from the lower bounds on spectral independence.
First, we present such a lower bound for the critical hardcore model.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\Delta\geq 3
be a constant.
For all
n\geq 1
, there exists a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
\left|L\right|=\left|R\right|=2n
and maximum degree at most
\Delta

such that the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the hardcore model on
G
with critical fugacity
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu}\right)=\Omega(n^{1/3}),

where
\Psi_{\mu}
is the influence matrix of
\mu
.

According to the the universality of spectral independence
[
4
, Theorem 3.1]
,
this lower bound of
\Omega(n^{1/3})
on spectral independence implies a corresponding lower bound of
\Omega(n^{4/3})
on the relaxation time of Glauber dynamics on the same instances.
Consequently, this proves the lower bound of
\Omega(n^{4/3})
on the mixing time of Glauber dynamics in
Theorem

1.1
.

Next, we present a lower bound for the critical anti-ferromagnetic Ising model.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\Delta\geq 3
be a constant.
For all
n\geq 1
, there exists a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
\left|L\right|=\left|R\right|=n
and maximum degree at most
\Delta

such that the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the anti-ferromagnetic Ising model on graph
G
with zero external field and critical inverse temperature
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu}\right)\geq\frac{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{s}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}}{2n}=\Omega\left(\sqrt{n}\right),

(38)

where
\Psi_{\mu}
is the influence matrix of
\mu
, and
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\cup R}
is defined as
\boldsymbol{s}_{u}=\begin{cases}1&u\in L;\\
-1&u\in R.\end{cases}

For each bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
constructed in
Theorem

5.2
, flipping the spins on one side (say
R
) transforms the model into a ferromagnetic Ising model with zero external field.
Formally, let
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution of the anti-ferromagnetic Ising model on
G
defined in
Theorem

5.2
, and let
\widetilde{\mu}
be the Gibbs distribution of the ferromagnetic Ising model on the same graph
G
with opposite inverse temperature
\beta=\beta_{c}(\Delta)
and zero external field.
It is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in L\cup R,\quad\Psi_{\widetilde{\mu}}(u,v)=\begin{cases}\Psi_{\mu}(u,v)&\text{$u,v$ are on the same side};\\
-\Psi_{\mu}(u,v)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(39)

Therefore, we have
\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\widetilde{\mu}}\boldsymbol{1}=\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}
, which leads to the following lower bound for the critical ferromagnetic Ising model.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\Delta\geq 3
be a constant.
For all
n\geq 1
, there exists a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
\left|L\right|=\left|R\right|=n
and maximum degree at most
\Delta

such that the Gibbs distribution
\mu
of the ferromagnetic Ising model on graph
G
with zero external field and critical inverse temperature
\beta=\beta_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu}\right)\geq\frac{\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{1}}{\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{1}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{1}}{2n}=\Omega\left(\sqrt{n}\right),

where
\Psi_{\mu}
is the influence matrix of
\mu
, and
\boldsymbol{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{L\cup R}
denotes the all-one vector.

Again, by the universality of spectral independence,
the lower bounds of
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
on spectral independence, as established in
Theorem

5.2
and
Corollary

5.3
, imply corresponding lower bounds of
\Omega(n^{3/2})
on the relaxation time (and consequently, the mixing time) of Glauber dynamics on the same instances.
This proves the lower bound in
Theorem

1.2
.

This section is organized as follows. In
Section

5.1
, we outline the proof of mixing time lower bounds by introducing the anti-concentration phenomenon in the critical models. In the remaining sections (
Section

5.2
and
Section

5.3
), we prove
Theorem

5.1
and
Theorem

5.2
respectively.

5.1
Proof overview for the mixing lower bounds

The hard instances used to prove the lower bounds in
Theorems

5.1
and
5.2
are well studied in the literature:
random regular bipartite graphs.
The torpid mixing of local Markov chains on these instances in the super-critical (non-uniqueness) regime is well known
[
53
]
,
and such random graphs have been widely utilized as gadgets in complexity reductions to establish computational hardness in this regime
[
59
,
58
,
36
,
37
]
.

The slow mixing and computational hardness arise from the fact that,
in the super-critical regime, the probability mass of the magnetization (represented by the difference in average sign of spins between the two sides of the bipartition) becomes bimodal.
This bimodal property creates bottlenecks in the state space and leads to long-range correlations in the Gibbs measure,
resulting in slow mixing of local Markov chains and supporting gadget reductions for computational hardness.

This situation changes significantly at criticality:
the bimodality of the Gibbs measure no longer holds due to the uniqueness of Gibbs measure,
yet the Gibbs measure lacks the strong concentration observed in the sub-critical (uniqueness) regime.
Instead, at criticality, the Gibbs measure exhibits an
anti-concentration
phenomenon,
which prevents Glauber dynamics from mixing ideally within
\widetilde{O}(n)
steps.
Specifically, the anti-concentration is characterized by the property
that the magnetization of a sample from the Gibbs distribution is
\Omega(n^{\alpha})
for some
\alpha>-1/2

with large probability,
rather than concentrating around
\Theta(n^{-1/2})
as in a product distribution.
This critical behavior, akin to that observed in the mean-field Ising model
[
45
,
27
]
, is illustrated in
Figure

1
.

concentration

magnetization
\mu

(a)

sub-critical

unimodal

anti-concentration

magnetization
\mu

(b)

critical

bimodal

magnetization
\mu

(c)

super-critical

Figure 1
:
Density of magnetization in hardcore/Ising models

To leverage the anti-concentration in the Gibbs measure
\mu
to prove a lower bound on the mixing time,
we examine the quadratic form
\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}
of the influence matrix
\Psi_{\mu}
using a signed vector
\boldsymbol{s}
with opposite signs on the two sides of bipartition.
This quadratic form, up to a constant factor, is approximately
n^{2}
times the variance of the magnetization.
By the anti-concentration property, this variance is lower bounded as
\Omega\left(n^{2\alpha}\right)
for some
\alpha>-1/2
.
From this, we can derive a lower bound on spectral independence, as the largest eigenvalue of the influence matrix is bounded by

\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu})\geq\frac{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{s}}=\Omega\left(n^{1+2\alpha}\right)
.
By the universality of spectral independence, as established in
[
4
]
,
this translates into a lower bound of
\Omega\left(n^{2+2\alpha}\right)
on the mixing time of the Glauber dynamics.

The spectral independence approach for deriving a mixing time lower bound offers a notable advantage over the traditional conductance-based method, such as that in
[
53
]
, by generalizing the notion of cuts in the state space.
From a functional analysis perspective, the spectral independence method allows for the use of more general testing functions, beyond the Boolean functions typically employed in conductance-based bounds.
This flexibility enables us to identify a lower bound on the mixing time that is polynomially bounded.
Consider the Gibbs distribution
\mu
, supported on
\Omega\subseteq\{-1,+1\}^{L\cup R}
,
induced by the hardcore model on a random
\Delta
-regular bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with critical fugacity
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
.
The approximate tensorization of variance with constant
C
, which is equivalent to the Poincaré inequality for Glauber dynamics with constant
\frac{1}{Cn}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]\leq C\sum_{v\in V}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[f\right]\right],

(40)

where
f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega}
is a testing function.
In contrast to conductance-based lower bounds, which are derived using the Boolean testing function
\boldsymbol{X}\mapsto\boldsymbol{1}[\sum_{u\in L}X_{u}>\sum_{v\in R}X_{v}]
to identify a cut in the state space
\Omega
,
the spectral independence approach uses a linear testing function
\boldsymbol{X}\mapsto\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{X}
, where
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\cup R}
is a signed vector defined by letting
s_{u}=1
for
u\in L
; and
s_{v}=-1
for
v\in R
.
This generalization of cuts allows for a more refined analysis of the Poincaré constant and nicely describes the anti-concentration phenomenon discussed above.
In particular, the variance of this testing function
f
with
f(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{X}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]=\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{X}\right]=\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)\boldsymbol{s}.

Meanwhile, the sum of local variances of
f
is bounded as:

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[f\right]\right]=\sum_{v\in V}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{X}\right]\right]=\sum_{v\in V}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left[X_{v}\right]\right]\cdot s_{v}^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[X_{v}\right]\right\}_{v\in L\cup R}\boldsymbol{s}.

By leveraging the anti-concentration in the critical Gibbs measure, we obtain:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{Cov}(\mu)\boldsymbol{s}=\Omega\left(n^{1+2\alpha}\right)\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}\left[X_{v}\right]\right\}_{v\in L\cup R}\boldsymbol{s}.

Thus, combining these observations, the Poincaré constant
C
in the approximate tensorization of variance (
40
) is lower bounded by
\Omega\left(n^{1+2\alpha}\right)
for some
\alpha>-\frac{1}{2}
.
This rules out the possibility of achieving a near-optimal
\tilde{O}(n)
relaxation time for the Glauber dynamics.

Finally, to establish the anti-concentration in the critical Gibbs measure
which is essential for deriving the spectral independence lower bound,
we face a significant technical challenge:
the absence of bimodality in the critical regime.
This lack of bimodality prevents us from leveraging the optimization-based techniques for approximating the log-partition function,
which have been primary tools for analyzing the density of magnetization, especially in the context of torpid mixing and computational hardness in the super-critical regime.
To overcome this obstacle, we shift our focus from the log-partition function to the partition function itself.
By employing a local limit theorem for large deviations, we can analyze the partition function’s behavior in the critical regime.
This approach not only helps us establish the necessary anti-concentration property but also offers a novel perspective on the analysis of critical Gibbs measures, which may be of independent interest.

5.2
Critical hardcore model on random symmetric bipartite graphs

In this section, we will prove
Theorem

5.1
. We first show the spectral independence lower bound via anti-concentration in
Section

5.2.1
, and then establish the anti-concentration in
Section

5.2.2
.

The hard instances we consider are the hardcore model on random symmetric bipartite graphs with a maximum degree
\Delta
and critical fugacity
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}
.
The random symmetric bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
is constructed as follows:

•

Fix a constant
\Delta\geq 3
. Let
L=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{2n}\}
and
R=\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{2n}\}
be the vertex sets.

•

Sample
\Delta
perfect matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{\Delta}
in the complete graph
K_{2n}
on vertices
[2n]
uniformly and independently at random.
For each
(u,v)\in M_{k}
for some
1\leq k\leq\Delta
, add edges
(\ell_{u},r_{v})
and
(\ell_{v},r_{u})
to the bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
.

We use
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
to denote the law of the random symmetric bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
.
This construction of symmetric bipartite graphs has been used as a gadget for establishing computational hardness for super-critical anti-ferromagentic two-spin systems (such as in
[
58
]
).

5.2.1
Spectral correlation via anti-concentration

The lower bound in
Theorem

5.1
establishes a spectral correlation property for the critical hardcore Gibbs measure.
This lower bound can be implied by the anti-concentration of the Gibbs measure on the random instances
G=(L,R,E)\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
.

Recall
L=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{2n}\}
and
R=\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{2n}\}
.
For
S,T\subseteq[2n]
, let
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\subseteq L\cup R
be the vertex set define as
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}:=\{\ell_{i},r_{j}\mid i\in S,j\in T\}
.
For integers
0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n
,
we define:

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\text{ with}\\
|S\setminus T|=A,|T\setminus S|=B,|S\cap T|=C\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right],

where
w_{G}(I)
represents the weight of the vertex set
I
in the hardcore model on
G
.
Specifically,
w_{G}(I)=\lambda^{|I|}
with
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
if
I
is an independent set in
G
, and
w_{G}(I)=0
otherwise.
For a given bipartite graph
G=(L\cup R,E)
, let
\mu_{G}
be a distribution over
2^{L\cup R}
be the Gibbs distribution of the hardcore model defined as

\displaystyle\forall\text{ independent set }I,\quad\mu_{G}(I)\propto w_{G}(I)=\lambda^{\left|I\right|}.

We may also consider
\mu_{G}
as a distribution over
\left\{0,1\right\}^{L\cup R}
(i.e., use
1
to indicate a vertex is in the independent set; and use
0
to indicate that it is not) for technical convenience.

The following lemma establishes an anti-concentration property, which provides a key to proving the spectral correlation described in
Theorem

5.1
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|A-B|>\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C}>\varepsilon\cdot\sum_{0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n}\alpha_{A,B,C}

(41)

holds for all sufficiently large
n>0
and all integers
0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n
.

Remark 5.5
.

Lemma

5.4
captures an anti-concentration of the Gibbs measure on the random instance
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
.
Suppose a random vertex set
I\subseteq L\cup R
is drawn with a probability proportional to the expected weight
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(I)\right]
, representing a “typical” sample of configurations in a random instance
G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
.
Then, the inequality (
41
) guarantees that, with a non-negligible probability, the difference between the numbers of occupied vertices in
L
and
R
will be substantial, specifically bounded as
\Omega(n^{2/3})
. This phenomenon is as illustrated in
Figure

1
.

Theorem

5.1
follows from
Lemma

5.4
. To see this, we first need to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with positive measure in
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
, it holds

\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu_{G}})\geq\frac{4\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G})\boldsymbol{s}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{s}}=\frac{2}{n}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\in I\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},r_{j}\in I\right]\right).

where
\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G})
7
7

7

Here, we consider
\mu_{G}
as a distribution over
\left\{0,1\right\}^{L\cup R}
, so that its covariance matrix can be defined. We refer to
Remark

2.8
for a detailed discussion.
denotes the covariance matrix for
\mu_{G}
and the vector
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\cup R}
is defined as:

\boldsymbol{s}_{u}=\begin{cases}1&u\in L,\\
-1&u\in R.\end{cases}

Proof.

Consider the hardcore Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
on graph
G=(L,R,E)
.
The influence matrix
\Psi_{\mu_{G}}
is related to the covariance matrix
\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G})
through the equation (
5
):

\Psi_{\mu_{G}}=\Pi^{-1}\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G}),

where
\Pi=\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[u\right](1-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[u\right])\right\}_{u\in L\cup R}
is the diagonal matrix of variances.
Here,
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[u\right]
represents the marginal probability that vertex
u
is occupied in an independent set sampled from
\mu_{G}
.
Since
\Pi\preceq\frac{1}{4}I
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu_{G}})\geq 4\lambda_{\max}(\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G})).

(42)

Let
(X,Y)\in\{0,1\}^{L\cup R}
be the indicator vector for the vertex set
I\sim\mu_{G}
,
where
X
corresponds to the vertices in
L
and
Y
corresponds to those in
R
.
By the Courant-Fischer theorem, we have:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G}))\geq\frac{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\mathrm{Cov}(\mu_{G})\boldsymbol{s}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{s}}=\frac{1}{4n}\mathrm{Var}_{(X,Y)\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2n}X_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{2n}Y_{i}\right].

(43)

By the symmetry of the bipartite graph
G
with positive measure in
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
, the marginal probability that vertex
\ell_{i}\in L
are occupied is equal to that of the corresponding vertex
r_{i}\in R
, for each
1\leq i\leq 2n
.
Therefore, the variance in (
43
) can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{(X,Y)\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2n}X_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{2n}Y_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(X,Y)\sim\mu_{G}}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(X,Y)\sim\mu_{G}}\left[X_{i}Y_{j}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\in I\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},r_{j}\in I\right]\right)

(44)

The lemma follows by combining (
42
), (
43
), and (
44
).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
(via
Lemma

5.4
).

Let
Z_{G}=\sum_{I\subseteq L\cup R}w_{G}(I)
denote the partition function, and for
u,v\in L\cup R
, define

Z_{G}^{u\leftarrow+,v\leftarrow+}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq L\cup R\\
I\supseteq\{u,v\}\end{subarray}}w_{G}(I),

which represents the portion of the partition function
Z_{G}
contributed by the independent sets
I
that contain both
u
and
v
.

We observe that the anti-concentration inequality (
41
) stated in
Lemma

5.4
translates to the following bound:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right)\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}\right]}=\Omega(n^{4/3}).

(45)

To prove this, we first express the expected value
\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]\\
i,j\in S\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}|S|^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle(\text{by symmetry of }\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta))\qquad

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}\frac{|S|^{2}+|T|^{2}}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

where recall
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}:=\{\ell_{i},r_{j}\mid i\in S,j\in T\}
for
S,T\subseteq[2n]
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]=\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}|S||T|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

Thus, the numerator of (
45
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}\frac{|S|^{2}+|T|^{2}}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]-\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}|S||T|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{S\subseteq[2n],T\subseteq[2n]}(|S|-|T|)^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n}(A-B)^{2}\alpha_{A,B,C}.

Now, applying the anti-concentration inequality (
41
) from
Lemma

5.4
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n}(A-B)^{2}\alpha_{A,B,C}\geq n^{4/3}\eta^{2}\varepsilon\sum_{0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n}\alpha_{A,B,C}=n^{4/3}\eta^{2}\varepsilon\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}\right].

Thus, inequality (
45
) holds by assuming
Lemma

5.4
.

Next, applying the probabilistic method as described in
Lemma

2.12
to inequality (
45
),
it ensures the existence of a graph
G=(L,R,E)
such that:

\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},\ell_{j}\in I\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{I\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\ell_{i},r_{j}\in I\right]\right)=\frac{\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2n}\left(Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right)}{Z_{G}}=\Omega(n^{4/3}).

Finally, by
Lemma

5.6
, this implies
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu_{G}})=\Omega(n^{1/3})
, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.2.2
A local limit theorem for large deviation

It remains to prove the anti-concentration claimed in
Lemma

5.4
,
which is the main technical lemma for establishing the lower bound on the mixing time.
The key idea of the proof is as follows:

•

Near the center
: We express
\alpha_{A,B,C}
as a ratio of probabilities that a random walk reaches the lattice point
(A,B,C)
at some point in time.
This allows us to leverage a local limit theorem to obtain a tight approximation of
\alpha_{A,B,C}
around the “center of mass” at

(A,B,C)\approx\boldsymbol{m}:=\left(\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2n}{\Delta^{2}}\right).

•

Far from the center
: For values of
(A,B,C)
that deviate significantly from this center, we show that
\alpha_{A,B,C}
decays exponentially.
This phenomenon is well studied and is critical in proving computational hardness in the super-critical regime
[
58
,
37
]
.

The following lemmas formalize these intuitions.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
. There exist a finite
\gamma=\gamma(\Delta)>1
and a positive-definite quadratic form
Q(\cdot,\cdot)=Q_{\Delta}(\cdot,\cdot)
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
There exists a factor
Z=Z(\Delta,n)
, depending only on
\Delta
and
n
,
such that for any integers
0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n
satisfying

\left\|(A,B,C)-\boldsymbol{m}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2n^{2/3},

where
\boldsymbol{m}:=\left(\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2n}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle Z\leq\frac{\alpha_{A,B,C}}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{n}Q(\theta_{A}+\theta_{B},\theta_{C})\right)}\leq\gamma\cdot Z,

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{A},\theta_{B},\theta_{C}):=(A,B,C)-\boldsymbol{m}
represents the deviation from the “center of mass”
\boldsymbol{m}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta>0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
For integers
0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n
satisfying both the following conditions:

1.

\left|A+B-(m_{1}+m_{2})\right|>n^{2/3}
or
\left|C-m_{3}\right|>n^{2/3}
;

2.

\left|A-B\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{2/3}
,

where
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})=\left(\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2n}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}\leq\exp\left(-\varepsilon\cdot n^{1/3}\right)\cdot\max_{0\leq a,b,c\leq 2n}\alpha_{a,b,c}.

With
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.8
, we can now prove the anti-concentration claimed in
Lemma

5.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.4
(via
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.8
).

It is sufficient to establish the anti-concentration property as described in (
41
).

Note that if
Lemma

5.8
holds with parameters
\varepsilon,\eta>0
, then it automatically holds for all smaller positive parameters
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
and
\eta^{\prime}<\eta
.
Therefore, we can assume without loss of generality that
Lemma

5.8
holds with parameters
\varepsilon,\eta\in\left(0,0.5\right)
.

Assume
n
is sufficiently large.
By
Lemma

5.7
, for any integer tuples
(A,B,C)
and
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C)
with
A+B=A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}
,
if
\left\lVert(A,B,C)-\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2n^{2/3}
and
\left\lVert(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C)-\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2n^{2/3}
,
then it holds

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}\leq{\gamma}\cdot\alpha_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C},

(46)

where
\gamma=\gamma(\Delta)
is the constant factor fixed in
Lemma

5.7
.

Thus, for any integers
k
and
C
with
\left|k-(\boldsymbol{m}_{1}+\boldsymbol{m}_{2})\right|\leq n^{2/3}
and
\left|C-\boldsymbol{m}_{3}\right|\leq n^{2/3}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A+B=k\\
\left|A-B\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C}\overset{(\star)}{\leq}\gamma\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A+B=k\\
\eta\cdot n^{2/3}<\left|A-B\right|\leq 2\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C}\leq\gamma\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A+B=k\\
\left|A-B\right|>\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C},

(47)

where inequality
(\star)
follows from (
46
) and the fact that both
A
and
B
are
2n^{2/3}
-close to the center
\boldsymbol{m}_{1}=\boldsymbol{m}_{2}
when
\left|A+B-(\boldsymbol{m}_{1}+\boldsymbol{m}_{2})\right|\leq n^{2/3}
and
\left|A-B\right|\leq 2\eta n^{2/3}
are both satisfied.

Therefore, by (
47
) and
Lemma

5.8
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|A-B\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{2/3}}\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(A,B,C)\in\Omega\\
\left|A-B\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(A,B,C)\not\in\Omega\\
\left|A-B\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}+(2n)^{3}\exp\left(-\varepsilon n^{1/3}\right)\right)\sum_{0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n}\alpha_{A,B,C},

where
\Omega
denotes the set of tuples
(A,B,C)
with
\left|A+B-(\boldsymbol{m}_{1}+\boldsymbol{m}_{2})\right|\leq n^{2/3}
and
\left|C-\boldsymbol{m}_{3}\right|\leq n^{2/3}
.

Therefore, (
41
) holds with parameters
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2(\gamma+1)}
and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
, when
n
is sufficiently large.
∎

To complete the lower bound proof, we need to prove
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.8
.
As discussed earlier,
Lemma

5.8
handles the case where the parameters
(A,B,C)
are far from the center.
Specifically, it roughly says that
\alpha_{A,B,C}
achieves its maximum approximately at
\boldsymbol{m}=\left(\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2(\Delta-1)n}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2n}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
, and decays exponentially as the parameters move away from this center.
This phenomenon of exponential decay away from the center has been well established in prior work on computational hardness in the super-critical regime, such as in
[
37
]
.
The proof of
Lemma

5.8
follows a similar approach, and is thereby deferred to
Section

B.2
.

Next, it remains to prove
Lemma

5.7
, which addresses the case where the parameters
(A,B,C)
are close to the center.
This can be established by
the local limit theorem for large deviation.

Proof of
Lemma

5.7
.

By definition of
\alpha_{A,B,C}
and
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\\
|S\setminus T|=A,|T\setminus S|=B,|S\cap T|=C\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\\
|S\setminus T|=A,|T\setminus S|=B,|S\cap T|=C\end{subarray}}\lambda^{A+B+2C}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\\
|S\setminus T|=A,|T\setminus S|=B,|S\cap T|=C\end{subarray}}\lambda^{A+B+2C}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right]\right)^{\Delta},

where
\mathcal{I}(G)
denotes the collection of independent sets in
G
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}
be the uniform distribution over subsets
S,T\subseteq[2n]
such that
\left|S\setminus T\right|=A
,
\left|T\setminus S\right|=B
, and
\left|S\cap T\right|=C
.
Given the construction of
\mathcal{G}(2n,1)
, the probability that
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
forms an independent set in
G\sim\mathcal{G}(2n,1)
depends only on the sizes of
S\setminus T
,
T\setminus S
and
S\cap T
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(2n,1)}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right]=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\sim\mathcal{G}(2n,1)\\
(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}\end{subarray}}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right].

(48)

Note that for any
G\in\mathcal{G}(2n,1)
,
the probability
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right]
remains the same, since
\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}
is a uniform distribution over possible subsets. Thus, we can reformulate (
48
) as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(2n,1)}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}(G_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{2n}{A,B,C,2n-A-B-C}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\\
\left|S\setminus T\right|=A,\left|T\setminus S\right|=B,\left|S\cap T\right|=C\end{subarray}}\boldsymbol{1}[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G_{0})],

(49)

where
G_{0}=(L,R,E)\in\mathcal{G}(2n,1)
with edges defined such that
(\ell_{2i-1},r_{2i}),(\ell_{2i},r_{2i-1})\in E
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, and the multinomial coefficient
\binom{2n}{A,B,C,2n-A-B-C}=\frac{(2n)!}{A!B!C!(2n-A-B-C)!}
counts the number of possible subsets
S,T\subseteq[2n]
in
\mathcal{R}_{A,B,C}
.

Next, we express both the numerator and denominator in (
49
) as coefficients of generating polynomials.
Specifically, the denominator, the multinomial coefficient
\binom{2n}{A,B,C,2n-A-B-C}
, corresponds to the coefficient of the term
x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}
in the generating polynomial
D=(1+x+y+z)^{2n}
:

\displaystyle\binom{2n}{A,B,C,2n-A-B-C}=[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]\underbrace{\left(1+x+y+z\right)^{2n}}_{=:D}.

Similarly, the numerator in (
49
) can also be expressed as the coefficient of the term
x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}
in another generating polynomial
N=\left(1+2x+2y+2z+x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{n}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[2n]\\
\left|S\setminus T\right|=A,\left|T\setminus S\right|=B,\left|S\cap T\right|=C\end{subarray}}\boldsymbol{1}[\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\mathcal{I}(G_{0})]=[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]\underbrace{\left(1+2x+2y+2z+x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{n}}_{=:N}.

(50)

To see this, note that the left-hand-side of (
50
) counts the number of independent sets
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
in
G_{0}
with
\left|S\setminus T\right|=A
,
\left|T\setminus S\right|=B
and
\left|S\cap T\right|=C
. For all
1\leq i\leq n
, the number of independent sets
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\subseteq\{\ell_{2i-1},\ell_{2i},r_{2i-1},r_{2i}\}
satisfying
\left|S\setminus T\right|=a
,
\left|T\setminus S\right|=b
and
\left|S\cap T\right|=c
is given by the coefficient of the term
x^{a}y^{b}z^{c}
in the generating polynomial
N^{1/n}:=1+2x+2y+2z+x^{2}+y^{2}
.
Then, equation (
50
) follows from the product rule for generating polynomials.

Thus,
\alpha_{A,B,C}
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}=\frac{\lambda^{A+B+2C}}{\left(\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]D\right)^{\Delta-1}}\left(\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]N\right)^{\Delta}=\frac{\left(\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]N^{\star}\right)^{\Delta}}{\left(\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]D^{\star}\right)^{\Delta-1}},

(51)

where
D^{\star}(x,y,z)=D\left(\frac{x}{\Delta-1},\frac{y}{\Delta-1},\frac{z}{(\Delta-1)^{2}}\right)
and
N^{\star}(x,y,z)=N\left(\frac{x}{\Delta-2},\frac{y}{\Delta-2},\frac{z}{(\Delta-2)^{2}}\right)
are the re-weighted polynomials,
and the last equality follows from critical point
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}
.

For the denominator, the coefficient
\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]D^{\star}
can be interpreted as:

\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]D^{\star}=F_{D}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=(A,B,C)\right],

where
F_{D}
is some factor depending only on
\Delta
and
n
but independent of
(A,B,C)
,
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X}_{2n}
are independent and identically distributed random variables defined as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{(\Delta-1)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(1,0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(0,1,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(0,0,1)&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}.\end{cases}

By a straightforward calculation, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\Sigma_{D}:=\mathrm{Cov}[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}u-u^{2}&-u^{2}&-uv\\
-u^{2}&u-u^{2}&-uv\\
-uv&-uv&v-v^{2}\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{where $u=\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}}$ and $v=\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}$.}

By
Lemma

2.9
with
d=3
, there exist constants
C_{D,\mathrm{LB}},C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{D,\mathrm{LB}}\leq\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=(A,B,C)\right]}{n^{-3/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma_{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq C_{D,\mathrm{UB}},

(52)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(A,B,C)-2n\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\right]=(A,B,C)-\boldsymbol{m}
.

Similarly, for the numerator, the coefficient
\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]N^{\star}
can be interpreted as:

\left[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}\right]N^{\star}=F_{N}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}=(A,B,C)\right],

where
F_{N}
is some factor depending only on
\Delta
and
n
but independent of
(A,B,C)
,
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y}_{n}
are independent and identically distributed random variables defined as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}=\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{(\Delta-2)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(1,0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{2(\Delta-2)}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(0,1,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{2(\Delta-2)}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(0,0,1)&\text{with probability }\frac{2}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(2,0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}},\\
(0,2,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}},\end{cases}

whose expectation and covariance are given by:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{2}{\Delta^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\Sigma_{N}:=\mathrm{Cov}[\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}s-u^{2}&-u^{2}&-uv\\
-u^{2}&s-u^{2}&-uv\\
-uv&-uv&v-v^{2}\end{bmatrix},

where
s=\frac{2}{\Delta}
,
u=\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{\Delta^{2}}
, and
v=\frac{2}{\Delta^{2}}
.

By
Lemma

2.9
, there exist constants
C_{N,\mathrm{LB}},C_{N,\mathrm{UB}}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}\leq\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}=(A,B,C)\right]}{n^{-3/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq C_{N,\mathrm{UB}},

(53)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(A,B,C)-n\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}\right]=(A,B,C)-\boldsymbol{m}
.

Let
\Sigma:=2\Delta\Sigma_{N}^{-1}-(\Delta-1)\Sigma_{D}^{-1}
.
Combining (
51
), (
52
), and (
53
), we have:

\displaystyle\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}}\leq\frac{\alpha_{A,B,C}}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}},

(54)

Therefore, we can choose parameters
Z=\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}}
and
\gamma=\frac{C^{\Delta}_{N,\mathrm{UB}}C^{\Delta-1}_{D,\mathrm{UB}}}{C^{\Delta}_{N,\mathrm{LB}}C^{\Delta-1}_{D,\mathrm{LB}}}
.
To conclude the proof of
Lemma

5.7
, it remains to show that
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}=Q(\theta_{A}+\theta_{B},\theta_{C})
, where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{A},\theta_{B},\theta_{C})
, for a positive-definite quadratic form
Q(\cdot,\cdot)
.
It is straightforward to verify:

\displaystyle\Sigma=2\Delta\Sigma_{N}^{-1}-(\Delta-1)\Sigma_{D}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{(\Delta-1)(\Delta^{3}-4\Delta^{2}+6\Delta-4)}\begin{bmatrix}2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3}&2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3}&4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3}\\
2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3}&2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3}&4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3}\\
4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3}&4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3}&8-16\Delta+12\Delta^{2}-4\Delta^{3}+\Delta^{4}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\kappa_{1}=(2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3})\kappa
,
\kappa_{2}=(4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3})\kappa
, and
\kappa_{3}=(8-16\Delta+12\Delta^{2}-4\Delta^{3}+\Delta^{4})\kappa
, where
\kappa=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{(\Delta-1)(\Delta^{3}-4\Delta^{2}+6\Delta-4)}
.
Therefore, the quadratic form
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}=\underbrace{\kappa_{1}\cdot(\theta_{A}+\theta_{B})^{2}+2\kappa_{2}\cdot(\theta_{A}+\theta_{B})\theta_{C}+\kappa_{3}\theta_{C}^{2}}_{=:Q(\theta_{A}+\theta_{B},\theta_{C})}.

The positive-definiteness of
Q(\cdot,\cdot)
follows from:

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\cdot\kappa_{3}-\kappa_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left[(2-2\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3})(8-16\Delta+12\Delta^{2}-4\Delta^{3}+\Delta^{4})-(4-4\Delta+\Delta^{3})^{2}\right]\cdot\kappa^{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}(\Delta-1)(\Delta-2)^{2}\left(\Delta^{2}-2\Delta+2\right)\cdot\frac{\Delta^{4}}{(\Delta-1)^{2}(\Delta^{3}-4\Delta^{2}+6\Delta-4)^{2}}>0,

for
\Delta\geq 3
.
∎

5.3
Critical Ising model on random regular bipartite graphs

In this section, we will prove
Theorem

5.2
. The structure of this section is similar to
Section

5.2
. We first show the spectral independence lower bound via anti-concentration in
Section

5.3.1
, and then establish the anti-concentration in
Sections

5.3.2
and
5.3.3
.

We focus on critical instances of the anti-ferromagnetic Ising model on random bipartite graph with maximum degree at most
\Delta
at the critical inverse temperature
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
, with zero external field.
The random bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
is constructed as follows:

•

Fix a constant
\Delta\geq 3
. Let
L=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
and
R=\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n}\}
be the vertex sets.

•

Sample
\Delta
perfect matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{\Delta}
between
L
and
R
uniformly and independently at random, and construct the graph
G=(L,R,E)
where
E=\cup_{i=1}^{\Delta}M_{i}
.

We use
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
to denote the law of the random bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
.

5.3.1
Spectral correlation via anti-concentration

Similar to the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, the lower bound in
Theorem

5.2
can be implied by the anti-concentration of the Gibbs measure on the random instances
G=(L,R,E)\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
.

Recall
L=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
and
R=\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n}\}
. For any subsets of indices
S,T\subseteq[n]
, the configuration
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
takes
+1
spins on
\{\ell_{i},r_{j}\mid i\in S,j\in T\}
, and
-1
otherwise. Formally, the configuration
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall u\in L\cup R,\quad\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}(u)=\begin{cases}+1,&u=\ell_{i}\text{ for some }i\in S,\text{ or }r_{j}\text{ for some }j\in T\\
-1,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

For integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
, we define:

\displaystyle\alpha_{s,t}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{s,t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\sigma)\right],

where
\Omega_{s,t}
is the collection of configurations
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\{-1,+1\}^{L\cup R}
with subsets
S,T\subseteq[n]
of indices satisfying
\left|S\right|=s
and
\left|T\right|=t
. Here,
w_{G}(\sigma)
represents the weight of configuration
\sigma
in the Ising model on graph
G=(V,E)
. Specifically:
w_{G}(\sigma)=\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\sigma)\right)
with critical inverse temperature
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
, where
m_{G}(\sigma)
is the number of monochromatic edges, i.e. the number of edges
uv\in E
with
\sigma_{u}=\sigma_{v}
.

The following lemma establishes an anti-concentration property, which provides a key to proving the spectral correlation described in
Theorem

5.2
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|s-t|>\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{s,t}>\varepsilon\cdot\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\alpha_{s,t}

(55)

holds for all sufficiently large
n
and all integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
.

Similar to the argument in
Remark

5.5
,
Lemma

5.9
captures an anti-concentration of the Ising Gibbs measure on the random instance
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
.

Theorem

5.2
follows from
Lemma

5.9
. To see this, we first need to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.
