For any bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with positive measure in
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
, it holds

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu_{G}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{n}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]+\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]\right),

where the vector
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\cup R}
is defined as:

\boldsymbol{s}_{u}=\begin{cases}1&u\in L,\\
-1&u\in R.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\mu=\mu_{G}
denote the Gibbs distribution of the critical anti-ferromagnetic Ising model on
G=(L,R,E)
.
By the symmetry of
\pm 1
spins, we have
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{u}\right]=0
for all
u\in L\cup R
. Hence,

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in L\cup R,\quad\Psi_{\mu}(u,v)=4\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{u}=\sigma_{v}=+1\right]-1.

The quadratic form
\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}
is thus expressed as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]+\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]\right)

\displaystyle-4\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]+\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]\right).

(56)

By Courant-Fischer theorem, the maximum eigenvalue of the influence matrix can be bounded by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\Psi_{\mu}\right)\geq\frac{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{s}}=\frac{1}{2n}\boldsymbol{s}^{\intercal}\Psi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{s}.

(57)

Lemma

5.10
then follows from (
56
) and (
57
).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
(via
Lemma

5.9
).

Let
Z_{G}=\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,+1\}^{L\cup R}}w_{G}(\sigma)
denote the partition function, and for vertices
u,v\in L\cup R
, define

Z_{G}^{u\leftarrow+,v\leftarrow+}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\{-1,+1\}^{L\cup R}\\
\sigma_{u}=\sigma_{v}=+1\end{subarray}}w_{G}(\sigma),

which represents the portion of the partition function
Z_{G}
contributed by the configuration
\sigma
that both
\sigma_{u}
and
\sigma_{v}
are assigned with
+1
spins.

First, we observe that the anti-concentration inequality (
55
) stated in
Lemma

5.9
translates to the following bound:

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}+Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}\right]}=\Omega(n^{3/2}).

(58)

To prove this, we first express the expected value
\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]\\
i,j\in S\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]}|S|^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right],

where
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
is a configuration that takes
+1
spin on
\{\ell_{i},r_{j}\mid i\in S,j\in T\}
for
S,T\subseteq[2n]
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]=\sum_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]}|T|^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right],

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]=\sum_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]}|S||T|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right],

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]=\sum_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]}|S||T|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

Thus, the numerator of (
58
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}+Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[n]}\left(\left|S\right|-\left|T\right|\right)^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}(s-t)^{2}\alpha_{s,t}.

Now, applying the anti-concentration inequality (
55
) from
Lemma

5.9
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}(s-t)^{2}\alpha_{s,t}\geq n^{3/2}\eta^{2}\varepsilon\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\alpha_{s,t}=n^{3/2}\eta^{2}\varepsilon\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}\right].

Thus, inequality (
58
) holds by assuming
Lemma

5.9
.

Next, applying the probabilistic method as described in
Lemma

2.12
to inequality (
58
),
it ensures the existence of a graph
G=(L,R,E)
such that:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{\ell_{i}}=\sigma_{r_{j}}=+1\right]+\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{G}}\left[\sigma_{r_{i}}=\sigma_{\ell_{j}}=+1\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}+Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{\ell_{i}\leftarrow+,r_{j}\leftarrow+}-Z_{G}^{r_{i}\leftarrow+,\ell_{j}\leftarrow+}\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[Z_{G}\right]}=\Omega(n^{3/2}).

Finally, by
Lemma

5.10
, this implies
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi_{\mu_{G}})=\Omega(n^{1/2})
.
∎

5.3.2
A local limit theorem for large deviation

It remains to prove the anti-concentration claimed in
Lemma

5.9
,
which is the main technical lemma for establishing the lower bound on the mixing time. Instead of establishing
Lemma

5.9
, we first prove an anti-concentration property for a variant of
\alpha_{s,t}
.

Alternatively, we consider the distribution
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)
over
\Delta
-regular bipartite (multi-)graphs
G=(L,R,E)
,
which differs slightly from
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)
by allowing parallel edges.
Here, the set of edges
E=\uplus_{i=1}^{\Delta}M_{i}
is the multiset union of the
\Delta
perfect matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{\Delta}
between
L
and
R
, sampled uniformly and independently at random.

We also similarly define
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
for the distribution
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)
of multigraph. Formally,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{s,t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\sigma)\right],

where
\Omega_{s,t}
is the collection of configurations
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\in\{-1,+1\}^{L\cup R}
with subsets
S,T\subseteq[n]
of indices satisfying
\left|S\right|=s
and
\left|T\right|=t
.

The following lemma describes the anti-concentration property for
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|s-t|>\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}>\varepsilon\cdot\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}

(59)

holds for all sufficiently large
n
and all integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
.

The key idea of the proof is similar to the critical hardcore model, which is as follows:

•

Near the center
: We express
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
as a ratio of probabilities that a random walk reaches the lattice point
(s,t)
at some point in time.
This allows us to leverage a local limit theorem to obtain a tight approximation of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
around the “center of mass” at

(s,t)\approx\boldsymbol{m}:=\left(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}\right).

•

Far from the center
: For values of
(s,t)
that deviate significantly from this center, we show that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
decays exponentially.
This phenomenon is well studied and is critical in proving computational hardness in the super-critical regime
[
58
,
37
]
.

The following lemmas formalize these intuitions.

Lemma 5.12
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
. There exist a finite
\gamma=\gamma(\Delta)>1
and a constant
Q=Q(\Delta)>0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
There exists a factor
Z=Z(\Delta,n)
, depending only on
\Delta
and
n
,
such that for any integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
satisfying

\left\|(s,t)-\boldsymbol{m}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2n^{3/4},

where
\boldsymbol{m}:=\left(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}\right)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle Z\leq\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}}{\exp\left(-\frac{Q}{n}\cdot(\theta_{s}+\theta_{t})^{2}\right)}\leq\gamma\cdot Z,

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{s},\theta_{t}):=(s,t)-\boldsymbol{m}
represents the deviation from the “center of mass”
\boldsymbol{m}
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Fix any constant
\Delta\geq 3
.
There exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta>0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
n
.
For integers
0\leq s,t\leq 2n
satisfying both the following conditions:

1.

\left|s+t-(m_{1}+m_{2})\right|>n^{3/4}
;

2.

\left|s-t\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{3/4}
,

where
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},m_{2})=\left(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}\right)
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\leq\exp\left(-\varepsilon\cdot n^{1/2}\right)\cdot\max_{0\leq s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\leq 2n}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}.

With
Lemmas

5.12
and
5.13
, we can now prove the anti-concentration claimed in
Lemma

5.9
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.11
(via
Lemmas

5.12
and
5.13
).

It is sufficient to show the anti-concentration property as described in (
59
).

Note that if
Lemma

5.13
holds with parameters
\varepsilon,\eta>0
, then it automatically holds for all smaller positive parameters
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
and
\eta^{\prime}<\eta
.
Therefore, we can assume without loss of generality that
Lemma

5.13
holds with parameters
\varepsilon,\eta\in\left(0,0.5\right)
.

Assume
n
is sufficiently large.
By
Lemma

5.12
, for any integer tuples
(s,t)
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
with
s+t=s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}
,
if
\left\lVert(s,t)-\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2n^{3/4}
and
\left\lVert(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2n^{3/4}
,
then it holds

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\leq{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}},

(60)

where
\gamma=\gamma(\Delta)
is the constant factor fixed in
Lemma

5.7
.

Thus, for any integer
k
with
\left|k-(m_{1}+m_{2})\right|\leq n^{3/4}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s+t=k\\
\left|s-t\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\overset{(\star)}{\leq}\gamma\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s+t=k\\
\eta\cdot n^{3/4}<\left|s-t\right|\leq 2\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\leq\gamma\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s+t=k\\
\left|s-t\right|>\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t},

(61)

where inequality
(\star)
follows from (
60
) and the fact that both
s
and
t
are
2n^{3/4}
-close to the center
m_{1}=m_{2}
when
\left|s+t-(m_{1}+m_{2})\right|\leq n^{3/4}
and
\left|s-t\right|\leq 2\eta\cdot n^{3/4}
are both satisfied.

Therefore, by (
61
) and
Lemma

5.13
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|s-t\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{3/4}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,t)\in\Omega\\
\left|s-t\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,t)\not\in\Omega\\
\left|s-t\right|\leq\eta\cdot n^{3/4}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}+n^{2}\exp\left(-\varepsilon n^{1/2}\right)\right)\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t},

where
\Omega
denotes the set of pairs
(s,t)
with
\left|s+t-(m_{1}+m_{2})\right|\leq n^{2/3}
.

Therefore, (
59
) holds with parameters
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2(\gamma+1)}
and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
, when
n
is sufficiently large.
∎

To complete the lower bound proof, we need to prove
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.8
.
As discussed earlier,
Lemma

5.8
handles the case where the parameters
(s,t)
are far from the center.
Specifically, it says that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
achieves its maximum approximately at its “center of mass”
\boldsymbol{m}=\left(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}\right)
, and decays exponentially as the parameters move away from this center.
This phenomenon of exponential decay away from the center has been well established in prior work on computational hardness in the super-critical regime, such as in
[
37
]
.
The proof of
Lemma

5.8
follows a similar approach, and is thereby deferred to
Section

B.1
.

Next, it remains to prove
Lemma

5.12
, which addresses the case where the parameters
(s,t)
are close to the center.
This can be established by
the local limit theorem for large deviation.

Proof of
Lemma

5.12
.

By the definition of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
, the expected contribution
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[n]\\
\left|S\right|=s,\left|T\right|=t\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[n]\\
\left|S\right|=s,\left|T\right|=t\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)}\left[\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[n]\\
\left|S\right|=s,\left|T\right|=t\end{subarray}}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)\right]\right)^{\Delta}

(62)

where
m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})
is the number of monochromatic edges.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{s,t}
be the uniform distribution over pairs of subsets
S\in\binom{[n]}{s}
and
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
. By symmetry, the expected value of
\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)
over
G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)
only relies on
s
,
t
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}\left(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1),(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{R}_{s,t}}\left[\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}\left(\boldsymbol{1}_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}\right)\right)\right].

(63)

Note that for any fixed graph
G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)
, the expected value of
\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}})\right)
over distribution
(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{R}_{s,t}
is the same as
\mathcal{R}_{s,t}
is a uniform distribution over fixed-size subsets
S
and
T
. Therefore, (
63
) can be further expressed as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)\right]=\left(\binom{n}{s}\binom{n}{t}\right)^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[n]\\
\left|S\right|=s,\left|T\right|=t\end{subarray}}\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G_{0}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)

(64)

where
G_{0}=(L,R,E)
is a perfect matching with
(\ell_{i},r_{i})\in E
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Next, we express both the numerator and denominator in (
64
) as coefficients of generating polynomials.
Specifically, the denominator, the binomial coefficients
\binom{n}{s}\binom{n}{t}
, corresponds to the coefficient of the term
x^{s}y^{t}
in the generating polynomial
D=(1+x)^{n}(1+y)^{n}
:

\displaystyle\binom{n}{s}\binom{n}{t}=[x^{s}y^{t}]\underbrace{\left(1+x\right)^{n}\left(1+y\right)^{n}}_{=:D}.

(65)

Similarly, the numerator in (
64
) can also be expressed as the coefficient of the term
x^{s}y^{t}
in another generating polynomial
N=\left(\exp\left(2\beta\right)xy+x+y+\exp\left(2\beta\right)\right)^{n}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[n]\\
\left|S\right|=s,\left|T\right|=t\end{subarray}}\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G_{0}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)=[x^{s}y^{t}]\underbrace{\left(\exp\left(2\beta\right)xy+x+y+\exp\left(2\beta\right)\right)^{n}}_{=:N}.

(66)

This follows from a similar argument as in the proof of (
50
).
Thus, by (
62
), (
64
), (
65
) and (
66
),
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}=\frac{\left([x^{s}y^{t}]N\right)^{\Delta}}{\left([x^{s}y^{t}]D\right)^{\Delta-1}}.

(67)

For the denominator, the coefficient
[x^{s}y^{t}]D
can be interpreted as:

\displaystyle[x^{s}y^{t}]D=F_{D}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=(s,t)\right],

where
F_{D}
is some factor depending on
\Delta
and
n
but independent of
(s,t)
, and
\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\boldsymbol{X}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}
are independent and identically distributed random variables defined as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\begin{cases}(0,0)&\text{with probability }1/4\\
(1,0)&\text{with probability }1/4\\
(0,1)&\text{with probability }1/4\\
(1,1)&\text{with probability }1/4.\end{cases}

By a straightforward calculation, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]=\left(1/2,1/2\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma_{D}:=\mathrm{Cov}[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]=\begin{bmatrix}1/4&0\\
0&1/4\end{bmatrix}.

By
Lemma

2.9
with
d=2
, there exist constants
C_{D,\mathrm{LB}},C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{D,\mathrm{LB}}\leq\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=(s,t)\right]}{n^{-1}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma_{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq C_{D,\mathrm{UB}},

(68)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}:=(s,t)-n\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\right]=\left(s-m_{1},t-m_{2}\right)
.

Similarly, the coefficient
[x^{s}y^{t}]N
can be interpreted as

\displaystyle[x^{s}y^{t}]N=F_{N}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}=(s,t)\right],

where
F_{N}
is some factor depending on
\Delta
and
n
but independent of
(s,t)
, and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{1},\boldsymbol{Y}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y}_{n}
are independent and identically distributed random variables defined as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}=\begin{cases}(0,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{\exp\left(2\beta\right)}{2(1+\exp\left(2\beta\right))}\\
(0,1)&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{2(1+\exp\left(2\beta\right))}\\
(1,0)&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{2(1+\exp\left(2\beta\right))}\\
(1,1)&\text{with probability }\frac{\exp\left(2\beta\right)}{2(1+\exp\left(2\beta\right))}.\end{cases}

Recall that
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-2}
. A straightforward calculation yields

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}\right]=\left(1/2,1/2\right)\text{ and }\Sigma_{N}=\mathrm{Cov}[\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}]=\begin{bmatrix}1/4&-1/(4\Delta-4)\\
-1/(4\Delta-4)&1/4\end{bmatrix}.

By
Lemma

2.9
, there exist constants
C_{N,\mathrm{LB}},C_{N,\mathrm{UB}}>0
satisfying

\displaystyle C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}\leq\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}=(s,t)\right]}{n^{-1}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq C_{N,\mathrm{UB}},

(69)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(s,t)-n\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{1}\right]=\left(s-m_{1},t-m_{2}\right)
.
Combining (
67
), (
68
) and (
69
), it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}}\leq\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\leq\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}},

(70)

where
\Sigma=\Delta\Sigma_{N}^{-1}-(\Delta-1)\Sigma_{D}^{-1}=\frac{4(\Delta-1)}{\Delta-2}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}
. Hence,
Lemma

5.12
holds with
Q=\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{\Delta-2}
,
Z=\frac{C_{N,\mathrm{LB}}^{\Delta}}{C_{D,\mathrm{UB}}^{\Delta-1}}\frac{F_{N}^{\Delta}}{F_{D}^{\Delta-1}}
and
\gamma=\frac{C^{\Delta}_{N,\mathrm{UB}}C^{\Delta-1}_{D,\mathrm{UB}}}{C^{\Delta}_{N,\mathrm{LB}}C^{\Delta-1}_{D,\mathrm{LB}}}
.
∎

5.3.3
Removing parallel edges

Finally, we prove that the expected contribution
\alpha_{s,t}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
are close in value.

Lemma 5.14
.

For sufficiently large
n
and all pairs of integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
, it holds that

\displaystyle\exp(-10^{4}\Delta^{3})\alpha_{s,t}\leq\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\leq\alpha_{s,t}.

With this comparison lemma, we can verify (
58
) in
Lemma

5.9
for
\alpha_{s,t}
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.9
.

Let
\eta
and
\varepsilon
be the constants in
Lemma

5.11
.
By
Lemmas

5.11
and
5.14
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|s-t\right|>\eta n^{3/4}}\alpha_{s,t}\geq\sum_{\left|s-t\right|>\eta n^{3/4}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}>\varepsilon\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\geq\varepsilon\exp\left(-10^{4}\Delta^{3}\right)\sum_{0\leq s,t\leq n}\alpha_{s,t}.

Thus,
Lemma

5.9
holds with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\exp\left(-10^{4}\Delta^{3}\right)\varepsilon
and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
.
∎

It only remains to prove
Lemma

5.14
, which is obtained by a coupling argument.

Proof of
Lemma

5.14
.

For any (multi)graph
\widetilde{G}=(V,E)
and configuration
\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T}
, let
G
be the graph obtained by removing parallel edges in
\widetilde{G}
. By the definition of
w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})=\exp\left(2\beta\cdot m_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right)
and
\beta=-\beta_{c}(\Delta)<0
, it holds that
w_{\widetilde{G}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\leq w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})
, implying
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}\leq\alpha_{s,t}
by the definition of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
and
\alpha_{s,t}
. We now focus on proving the lower bound of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
.

Fix subsets
S,T\subseteq[n]
with
\left|S\right|=s
and
\left|T\right|=t
. It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}}{\alpha_{s,t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}=\frac{\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]\right)^{\Delta}}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\Delta}\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G_{i-1}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,i-1)}\left[w_{G_{i-1}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{H\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[w_{H}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G_{i}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,i)}\left[w_{G_{i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]},

Therefore, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G_{i-1}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,i-1)}\left[w_{G_{i-1}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{H\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)}\left[w_{H}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G_{i}\sim\mathcal{G}(n,i)}\left[w_{G_{i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}\geq\exp\left(-10^{4}\Delta^{2}\right)

for all
1\leq i\leq\Delta
.
Without loss of generality, we only consider the case where
i=\Delta
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)\\
G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)\end{subarray}}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

Therefore, it suffices to prove that for any fixed perfect matching
H\sim\mathcal{G}(n,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]w_{H}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\geq\exp\left(-10^{4}\Delta^{2}\right)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

(71)

Without loss of generality, we assume that the matching
H=(L,R,\{(\ell_{i},r_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n})
.
Recall that
G=(L,R,E)\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)
is the union of
\Delta-1
independent random perfect matchings.
Let
e_{(i-1)n+1},e_{(i-1)n+2},\ldots,e_{in}
be the edges in the
i
-th matching shuffled uniformly at random.
Denote the graph
(L,R,E\setminus\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{i}\})
by
G^{-i}
(in particular, we use the convention
G^{-0}=G
).
We now claim that for all
1\leq i\leq n(\Delta-1)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-(i-1)}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]}\geq 1-\frac{\exp\left(-16\beta\right)}{n}\geq\exp\left(-\frac{10^{4}}{n}\right),

(72)

where the last inequality follows from
\exp\left(-2\beta\right)=\exp\left(2\beta_{c}(\Delta)\right)\leq 3
and
n
being sufficiently large.
Assuming the correctness of (
72
), the inequality (
71
) holds by telescoping.

To simplify the notations, we will use

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\mid e_{j}\in H\right]

\displaystyle B_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\mid e_{j}\not\in H\right].

Note that when
e_{i}\not\in H
,
H\setminus G^{-(i-1)}=H\setminus G^{-i}
and we have
w_{H\setminus G^{-(i-1)}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})=w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})
.
After taking the expectation, this implies that

\displaystyle B_{i-1,i}=B_{i,i}.

(73)

Moreover, we claim that the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle A_{i,i}\leq\exp(-16\beta)B_{i,i},

(74)

which implies that

\displaystyle A_{i,i}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-16\beta)\left(\frac{1}{n}A_{i,i}+\frac{n-1}{n}B_{i,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp(-16\beta)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

(75)

In order to prove (
72
), by the law of total expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-(i-1)}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{n}A_{i-1,i}+\frac{n-1}{n}B_{i-1,i}

\displaystyle(\text{by \eqref{eq:ABi-relationship}})

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{n-1}{n}B_{i,i}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]-\frac{1}{n}A_{i,i}

\displaystyle(\text{by \eqref{eq:cor-3-multi}})

\displaystyle\quad\geq\left(1-\frac{\exp(-16\beta)}{n}\right)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)}\left[w_{G}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right].

Now, we only left to prove (
74
).
We finish the proof by constructing a coupling
\mathcal{C}
between
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)
condition on the event
e_{i}\in H
and the event
e_{i}\not\in H
, such that
(G_{1}=(L,R,E_{1}),G_{2}=(L,R,E_{2}))\sim\mathcal{C}
satisfying
|E_{1}\oplus E_{2}|\leq 4
:
we sample
G_{2}=(L,R,E_{2})
from
\mathcal{G}(n,\Delta-1)
condition on the event
e_{i}\not\in H
. In this case, suppose
e_{i}=(\ell_{u},r_{v})
(since
e_{i}\not\in H
) and
e_{j}=(\ell_{w},r_{u})
is the edge that origins from the same matching, i.e.
\lfloor\frac{i-1}{n}\rfloor=\lfloor\frac{j-1}{n}\rfloor
. We construct
E_{1}
by substituting
e_{i}
with
(\ell_{u},r_{u})
and
e_{j}
with
(\ell_{w},r_{v})
.

With this coupling
\mathcal{C}
, by definition,
A_{i,i}-\exp(-16\beta)B_{i,i}
equals to

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(G_{1},G_{2})\sim\mathcal{C}}\left[w_{G_{1}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G_{1}^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})-\exp\left(-16\beta\right)w_{G_{2}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})w_{H\setminus G_{2}^{-i}}(\boldsymbol{1}_{S,T})\right]\leq 0,

as the size of symmetric difference between edge sets of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is at most
4
.
This finishes the proof of (
74
) and hence the whole proof.
∎

6
Deterministic Algorithm

In this section, we show that the noising/denoising framework can be applied to deterministic counting.
This results in sub-exponential time deterministic algorithms for approximating the partition function for the critical hardcore and Ising models,
thereby proving
Theorem

1.5
.

Let
\mathrm{wt}:2^{[n]}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative weight function over all subsets of
[n]
.
We normalize
\mathrm{wt}(\cdot)
into a probability distribution
\mu
as follow

\displaystyle\forall T\subseteq[n],\quad\mu(T)=\frac{\mathrm{wt}(T)}{Z},

where
Z=\sum_{T}\mathrm{wt}(T)
is the partition function for
\mu
.
For
\theta\in(0,1)
and
S\subseteq[n]
, we define

\displaystyle Z_{S,\theta}:=\sum_{T\supseteq S}\mathrm{wt}(T)(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|}.

(76)

This quantity
Z_{S,\theta}
corresponds to the partition function for the distribution
((1-\theta)*\mu)(\cdot\mid S)
, which is defined as
(1-\theta)*\mu
(as described in (
20
)) conditioned on
S
being occupied.

Lemma 6.1
.

Fix
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
Suppose for every
S\subseteq[n]
,
\hat{Z}_{S,\theta}
is an approximation of
Z_{S,\theta}
within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, let
k=\left\lceil\mathrm{e}^{2}n\cdot\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}+\log\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil
. The quantity:

\hat{Z}=\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}\hat{Z}_{S,\theta}

approximates the partition function
Z
within relative error
(1\pm(\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon))
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the continuous-time down walk described in
Definition

3.1
.
Fix any subset
S\subseteq[n]
.
According to the law of total probability applied to
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{\theta}=S\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{T\subseteq[n]}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{0}=T\right]\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{\theta}=S\mid X_{0}=T\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{T\supseteq S}\mu(T)\theta^{\left|S\right|}(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}.

As a well-defined probability space, we know that:

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{\theta}=S\right]=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\sum_{T\supseteq S}\mu(T)\theta^{\left|S\right|}(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}.

This implies

\displaystyle Z=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\sum_{T\supseteq S}\mathrm{wt}(T)\theta^{\left|S\right|}(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}.

By definition,
Z_{S,\theta}\leq Z_{\varnothing,\theta}\leq Z
.
Now, let
k=\left\lceil\mathrm{e}^{2}n\cdot\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}+\log 2/\varepsilon\right\rceil
. We consider the contribution of sets
S\subseteq[n]
with
\left|S\right|\geq k
in the partition function:

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{S:\left|S\right|\geq k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}}{Z_{\varnothing,\theta}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=k}^{n}\binom{n}{\ell}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\ell}\leq\sum_{\ell=k}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}n}{\ell}\cdot\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}n}{k}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{k}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\frac{\mathrm{e}n}{k}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}}\leq\varepsilon.

This implies that
\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}
approximates
Z
as follows:

\displaystyle Z\geq\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}\geq Z-\varepsilon Z_{\varnothing,\theta}\geq(1-\varepsilon)Z.

Now, suppose we have an approximation
\hat{Z}_{S,\theta}\in[(1-\varepsilon_{0})Z_{S,\theta},(1+\varepsilon_{0})Z_{S,\theta}]
of
Z_{S,\theta}
.
Then,

\hat{Z}=\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}\hat{Z}_{S,\theta}\geq(1-\varepsilon_{0})\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}\geq(1-\varepsilon_{0})(1-\varepsilon)Z\geq(1-\varepsilon_{0}-\varepsilon)Z,

and

\hat{Z}=\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}\hat{Z}_{S,\theta}\leq(1+\varepsilon_{0})\sum_{S:\left|S\right|<k}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\left|S\right|}Z_{S,\theta}\leq(1+\varepsilon_{0})Z.\qed

According to
Lemma

6.1
,
to estimate
Z
,
we can enumerate every subset
S\subseteq[n]
with
\left|S\right|<k
and estimate
Z_{S,\theta}
, which is typically more sub-critical and thus expected to be easier to estimate.
The overhead of this enumeration in the running time is bounded by:

\displaystyle O(n^{k})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\cdot n\log n+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot\log n\right)\right).

This is formalized in the following corollary of
Lemma

6.1
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Suppose there exist
\theta\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and
T>0
such that for every
S\subseteq[n]
, there is a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which can approximate
Z_{S,\theta}
in time
T
within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a deterministic algorithm that can approximate the partition function
Z
within relative error
(1\pm(\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon))
in time
T\cdot\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\cdot n\log n+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot\log n\right)\right)
.

6.1
Applications to critical hardcore and Ising models

It is well established that, within the
\delta
-uniqueness regime, the correlation decay method provides an approximation deterministically for the partition function
Z_{S,\theta}
, with a running time of
(\frac{n}{\varepsilon_{0}})^{O(\frac{\log\Delta}{\delta})}
.

Theorem 6.3

(
[
46
]
)
.

Let
G=([n],E)
be a graph with
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta
, and
\mu
be the Gibbs distribution for either the hardcore model or the Ising model on
G
.
Moreover, let
\tau_{\Lambda}
be a pinning on
\Lambda\subseteq[n]
, and
u\in[n]\setminus\Lambda
be a free vertex.
Given any
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, there exists a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{M}(\mu,\tau_{\Lambda},u)
that can approximate the marginal probability
\mu^{\tau_{\Lambda}}_{u}
within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})
, with the following guarantees on the running time:

•

Hardcore model
: If the model is the hardcore model and the parameter satisfies
\lambda\leq(1-\theta)\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
, the algorithm
\mathcal{M}(\mu,\tau_{\Lambda},u)
halts in time
(1/\varepsilon_{1})^{O(\frac{\log\Delta}{\theta})}
.

•

Ising model
: If the model is the critical Ising model, the algorithm
\mathcal{M}((1-\theta)*\mu,\tau_{\Lambda},u)
halts in time
(1/\varepsilon_{1})^{O(\frac{\log\Delta}{\theta^{2}})}
.

Given
S\subseteq[n]
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, let
\nu=\theta*\mu
. It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\theta)^{\left|S\right|}\mathrm{wt}(S)}{Z_{\varnothing,\theta}}=\nu(\boldsymbol{1}_{S}\uplus\boldsymbol{0}_{V\setminus S})=\nu^{\boldsymbol{1}_{S}}_{V\setminus S}(\boldsymbol{0})\cdot\nu_{S}(\boldsymbol{1})=\nu^{\boldsymbol{1}_{S}}_{V\setminus S}(\boldsymbol{0})\cdot\frac{Z_{S,\theta}}{Z_{\varnothing,\theta}},

which implies that
Z_{S,\theta}={(1-\theta)^{\left|S\right|}\mathrm{wt}(S)}/{\nu^{\boldsymbol{1}_{S}}_{V\setminus S}(\boldsymbol{0})}
.
Thus, to approximate
Z_{S,\theta}
with relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
, we only need to approximate
\nu^{\boldsymbol{1}_{S}}_{V\setminus S}(\boldsymbol{0})
with relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
, which can be achieved by using
Theorem

6.3
with
\varepsilon_{1}=\Theta(\varepsilon_{0}/n)
.

This leads to the following consequences for the deterministic approximation of
Z_{S,\theta}
, given an arbitrary subset
S\subseteq[n]
, parameter
\theta\in(0,1)
, and error bound
\varepsilon_{0}>0
:

•

Hardcore model
: For the critical hardcore model, the value of
Z_{S,\theta}
can be approximated within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
in deterministic time
(n/\varepsilon_{0})^{O\left(\frac{\log\Delta}{\theta}\right)}
.

•

Ising model
: For the critical Ising model, the value of
Z_{S,\theta}
can be approximated within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon_{0})
in deterministic time
(n/\varepsilon_{0})^{O\left(\frac{\log\Delta}{\theta^{2}}\right)}
.

We can then apply
Corollary

6.2
to obtain sub-exponential time deterministic approximation algorithms for the partition function of the critical hardcore and Ising models.

By taking
\theta=\Theta(n^{-1/2})
for the hardcore model and
\theta=\Theta(n^{-1/3})
for the Ising model, we obtain the following results.

Theorem 6.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a deterministic algorithm that approximates
the partition function
Z
of the critical hardcore model (where
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
)
on graphs with
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta

within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
This algorithm runs in time

\displaystyle\exp\left(O(\sqrt{n}\log^{2}(n/\varepsilon))\right).

Theorem 6.5
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a deterministic algorithm that approximates
the partition function
Z
of the critical Ising model (where
(\Delta-1)\tanh\left|\beta\right|=1
, and
\lambda=1
)
on graphs with
n
vertices and maximum degree
\Delta

within relative error
(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
This algorithm runs in time

\displaystyle\exp\left(O(n^{2/3}\log^{2}(n/\varepsilon))\right).

7
Conclusion

This paper explores both upper and lower bounds on the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the critical hardcore and Ising models. The upper bounds are derived from an information-theoretic reformulation of localization schemes.
To obtain sharper bounds for the critical Ising model with interaction norm
1
, we further establish an
O(\sqrt{n})
-spectral independence bound at criticality.
The lower bounds are obtained by the universality of spectral independence and the
anti-concentration
phenomenon of the Gibbs distribution of critical hardcore/Ising model on typical random symmetric regular bipartite graphs.

To conclude, we list several open problems that remain to be addressed:

•

What is the mixing time for general anti-ferromagnetic two-spin systems at criticality? The current approach for proving upper bounds on the mixing time handles the hardcore model and the Ising model separately (see also the discussion in
Remark

3.24
). A potential direction is to design a new noising-denoising localization process that unifies the field dynamics and proximal sampler.

•

How to close the gap between the current upper and lower bounds on the mixing time for hardcore and graphical Ising models at criticality?

•

Can we design an efficient deterministic algorithm for approximate counting at criticality, with or even without the bounded-degree assumption?

Appendix A
Spectral/Entropic Stability from Spectral Independence

In this section, we formally prove
Lemmas

3.17
and
3.22
,
establishing spectral/entropic stability from spectral independence under the respective noising processes.
We refer the readers to a recent work
[
16
]
for more discussion on spectral/entropic stability with respect to the continuous-time down walk.

A.1
Spectral stability from SI under field dynamics (Proof of
Lemma

3.17
)

Proof.

We slightly abuse the derivative notation
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}
to denote the limit operator
\lim_{\theta\to\eta^{+}}\frac{1}{\theta-\eta}
.
We first claim that for any
S\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq 2^{[n]}
, the derivative of
\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]
satisfies:

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=\eta^{+}}=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\cdot\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[v\in R\right]\cdot\left(f_{\eta}(S\cup\{v\})-f_{\eta}(S)\right)^{2}.

(77)

We first examine the derivative of the probability
Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,T)
for
S,T\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq 2^{[n]}
(
S\subseteq T
):

\displaystyle Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,T)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\eta}=S\text{ and }X_{1-\theta}=T\right]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\eta}=S\right]}=\frac{\sum_{T\subseteq R}\mu(R)(1-\theta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|T\right|}(\theta-\eta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}}{\sum_{S\subseteq R}\mu(R)(1-\eta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|S\right|}}.

(78)

The derivative of the numerator in (
78
) satisfies

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\left(\sum_{T\subseteq R}\mu(R)(1-\theta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|T\right|}(\theta-\eta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}\right)\right|_{\theta=\eta^{+}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{S\subseteq R}-\left(\left|R\right|-\left|S\right|\right)\mu(R)(1-\eta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|S\right|-1},&\text{if }S=T,\\
\sum\limits_{T\subseteq R}\mu(R)(1-\eta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|S\right|-1},&\text{if }\left|T\setminus S\right|=1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Thus, the derivative of
Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,T)
is

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,T)}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\Bigg|_{\theta=\eta^{+}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1-\eta}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[\left|S\right|-\left|R\right|\right]&\text{if }S=T,\\
\frac{1}{1-\eta}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[T\subseteq R\right]&\text{if }\left|T\setminus S\right|=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Consequently, the transition rule of the Markov kernel
Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(\cdot,\cdot)
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(S,T)=\begin{cases}1-\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q(S,\cdot)}\left[v\in R\right]\cdot h+o(h)&\text{if }S=T,\\
\frac{1}{1-\eta}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[v\in R\right]\cdot h+o(h)&\text{if }\left|T\setminus S\right|=1,\\
o(h)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(79)

Similarly, there exists
m(S,\theta)\neq\infty
such that the derivative of
f_{\theta}(S)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_{\theta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[f\right]
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}f_{\theta}(S)}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\left(\sum_{S\subseteq T}\frac{\mu(T)(1-\theta)^{\left|T\right|-\left|S\right|}}{\sum_{S\subseteq R}\mu(R)(1-\theta)^{\left|R\right|-\left|S\right|}}f(T)\right)=m(S,\theta).

Therefore,
f_{\eta+h}(S)
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{\eta+h}(S)=f_{\eta}(S)+m(S,\eta)\cdot h+o(h).

(80)

Thus, from (
79
), (
80
), and the fact that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(S,\cdot)}\left[f_{\eta+h}\right]=f_{\eta}(S)
, the variance
\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(S,\cdot)}\left[f\right]
is bounded as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(S,\cdot)}\left[f_{\eta+h}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq T}Q_{\eta\to\eta+h}(S,T)\left(f_{\eta+h}(T)-f_{\eta}(S)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\cdot\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S}\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[v\in R\right]\cdot\left(f_{\eta}(S\cup\{v\})-f_{\eta}(S)\right)^{2}\cdot h+o(h).

This proves (
77
).

Assuming
C(\eta)
-spectral independence of the distribution
Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
, we have the following for all test functions
g=\frac{\widetilde{\nu}}{Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}
, where
\widetilde{\nu}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S}p_{v}\left(\frac{q_{v}}{p_{v}}-1\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S}p_{v}\left(\frac{q_{v}}{p_{v}}-1\right)^{2}+(1-p_{v})\left(\frac{1-q_{v}}{1-p_{v}}-1\right)^{2}\leq C\cdot\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[g\right],

(81)

where
p_{v}=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[v\in R\right]
and
q_{v}=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{R\sim\widetilde{\nu}}\left[v\in R\right]
.
Assuming the test function
g=f/f_{\eta}(S)
in (
81
), the marginal probability
q_{v}
is given by:

\displaystyle q_{v}=\sum_{S\cup\{v\}\subseteq R}Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,R)\cdot\frac{f(R)}{f_{\eta}(S)}\overset{(\star)}{=}\sum_{S\cup\{v\}\subseteq R}p_{v}\cdot Q_{\eta\to 1}(S\cup\{v\},R)\cdot\frac{f(R)}{f_{\eta}(S)}=p_{v}\cdot\frac{f_{\eta}(S\cup\{v\})}{f_{\eta}(S)},

(82)

where equality
(\star)
follows the definition of
Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
. Specifically,
Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)
is the distribution that samples
R
according to the law of
(1-\eta)*\mu
conditioned on
S\subseteq R
.

Combining (
77
), (
81
) and (
82
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(S,\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=\eta^{+}}\leq\frac{C(\eta)}{1-\eta}\cdot\mathrm{Var}_{Q_{\eta\to 1}(S,\cdot)}\left[f\right].

This establishes the spectral stability claimed by the lemma.
∎

A.2
Entropic stability from SI under proximal sampler (Proof of
Lemma

3.22
)

We remark that de Bruijn’s
identity (see
[
61
]
and
[
51
, Lemma 4]
) relates the derivative of KL divergence
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\nu_{t}\parallel\mu_{t})
with the Fisher information
\mathsf{FI}(\nu_{t}\parallel\mu_{t})
, and
Lemma

3.22
can be shown by calculating the Fisher information
\mathsf{FI}(\nu_{t}\parallel\mu_{t})
(to obtain (
83
) below) and applying
[
20
, Lemma 40]
. For completeness, we present the following self-contained proof for
Lemma

3.22
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.22
.

Let
\mu=\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
be the Gibbs distribution of Ising model specified by interaction matrix
J=L^{\intercal}L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and external fields
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Fix
\eta\in[0,1)
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
be a feasible solution. Let
\widetilde{\mu}:=Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
and
f=\frac{\nu}{\widetilde{\mu}}
be the test function, where
\nu
is a distribution absolutely continuous with respect to
\widetilde{\mu}
, which is also absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{J,\boldsymbol{h}}
.
We first claim that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the derivative of
\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]
satisfies:

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\eta\to\theta}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)}\left[f_{\theta}\right]}{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=\eta^{+}}=\frac{1}{2(1-\eta)^{2}}(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu))^{\intercal}J(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu)),

(83)

where
\boldsymbol{b}(\mu):=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\mu}\left[\boldsymbol{x}\right]
denotes the mass center of distribution
\mu
.

We first obtain the law of the denoising process
Q_{\eta\to\eta+t}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
for all
0<t<1-\eta
. By the definition of denoising process, for any measurable set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
we have

\displaystyle Q_{\eta\to\eta+t}(\boldsymbol{x},A)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\eta-t}\in A\mid X_{1-\eta}=\boldsymbol{x}\right]

\displaystyle(\text{law of total probability})\quad

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}}\left[\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X_{1-\eta-t}\in A\mid X_{1-\eta}=\boldsymbol{x}\text{ and }X_{0}=\boldsymbol{z}\right]\right].

By Bayes’ formula, i.e.
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y\mid X,Z\right]=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\mid Y,Z\right]\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y\mid Z\right]}{\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\mid Z\right]}
, the density function
p_{\eta,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z},t}
for the conditional distribution
(X_{1-\eta-t}\mid X_{1-\eta}=\boldsymbol{x}\text{ and }X_{0}=\boldsymbol{z})
satisfies

\displaystyle p_{\eta,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z},t}(\boldsymbol{y})\propto

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2(\eta+t)(1-\eta-t)}(\boldsymbol{y}-(\eta+t)L\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{y}-(\eta+t)L\boldsymbol{z})\right)

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\eta t(\eta+t)}((\eta+t)\boldsymbol{x}-\eta\boldsymbol{y})^{\intercal}((\eta+t)\boldsymbol{x}-\eta\boldsymbol{y})\right)

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\eta(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{x}-\eta L\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{x}-\eta L\boldsymbol{z})\right)

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1-\eta}{2(1-\eta-t)t}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\frac{t}{1-\eta}L\boldsymbol{z}-\frac{1-\eta-t}{1-\eta}\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\intercal}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\frac{t}{1-\eta}L\boldsymbol{z}-\frac{1-\eta-t}{1-\eta}\boldsymbol{x}\right)\right).

Thus, the distribution
(X_{1-\eta-t}\mid X_{1-\eta}=\boldsymbol{x}\text{ and }X_{0}=\boldsymbol{z})
satisfies a normal distribution with mean
\frac{t}{1-\eta}L\boldsymbol{z}+\frac{1-\eta-t}{1-\eta}\boldsymbol{x}
and covariance
\frac{(1-\eta-t)t}{1-\eta}I_{r}
.
Therefore, the entropy satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\eta\to\eta+t}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)}\left[f_{\eta+t}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}\\
\boldsymbol{e}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},I)\end{subarray}}\left[f_{\eta+t}\left(\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)\log f_{\eta+t}\left(\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)\right],

(84)

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:=\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{t}{1-\eta}\left(L\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{x}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{t(1-\eta-t)}{1-\eta}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}
.
Similarly, for any fixed
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, the distributions
Q_{\eta+t\to 1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\cdot)
and
\widetilde{\mu}:=Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
satisfies:

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{\eta+t\to 1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{y})}{Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})}

\displaystyle\propto\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2(\eta+t)(1-\eta-t)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-(\eta+t)L\boldsymbol{y})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-(\eta+t)L\boldsymbol{y})\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\eta(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{x}-\eta L\boldsymbol{y})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{x}-\eta L\boldsymbol{y})\right)}

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t(1-\eta-t)(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star}=tL\boldsymbol{y}+(1-\eta-t)\boldsymbol{x}-(1-\eta)\boldsymbol{\gamma}=tL(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z})+\sqrt{t(1-\eta)(1-\eta-t)}\boldsymbol{e}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle f_{\eta+t}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}{Q_{\eta+t\to 1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{y})}f(y)}{\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}Q_{\eta+t\to 1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{y})}=\frac{\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t(1-\eta-t)(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})\right)f(y)}{\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t(1-\eta-t)(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{y}\sim\nu}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t(1-\eta-t)(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})\right)\right]}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{y}\sim\widetilde{\mu}}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t(1-\eta-t)(1-\eta)}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\star})\right)\right]},

Hence, the entropy of
f
in (
84
) can be further expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{\eta\to\eta+t}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)}\left[f_{\eta+t}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}\\
\boldsymbol{e}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\left(g(t)\right)}{D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\left(g(t)\right)}\log\frac{N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\left(g(t)\right)}{D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\left(g(t)\right)}\right],

(85)

where
g(t)=\frac{t}{(1-\eta)(1-\eta-t)}
, and
N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)
is defined by

\displaystyle N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t):=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\cdot t-(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\sqrt{t}\right)\right],

and
D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)
is defined accordingly by taking expectation over
\mu
. By expanding
N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}
around the origin, we have

\displaystyle N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)

\displaystyle=1-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\right]\cdot\sqrt{t}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})-(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\right]\cdot t+o(t)

(86)

Similarly, We can also expand
D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)
around the origin to obtain an analogue of (
86
). Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}{D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}

\displaystyle=1+\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\widetilde{\mu}}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\right]\right)\cdot\sqrt{t}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})-(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\right]\cdot t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})-(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\right]\cdot t

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\widetilde{\mu},\boldsymbol{y}\sim\nu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z})\right]\cdot t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\widetilde{\mu},\boldsymbol{y}\sim\mu}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})^{\intercal}L^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{e}\boldsymbol{e}^{\intercal}L(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{z})\right]\cdot t+o(t).

(87)

Let
A_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}
denote the coefficients of
\sqrt{t}
in (
87
), and
B_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}
denote the coefficients of
t
in (
87
). Then,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}{D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}\log\frac{N_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}{D_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}(t)}=A\sqrt{t}+Bt+\frac{1}{2}A^{2}t+o(t).

(88)

By a straightforward calculation
8
8

8

We remark that the
o(t)
in (
88
) relies on
\boldsymbol{e}
(denoted by
o_{\boldsymbol{e}}(t)
). Though, the remainder term can be bounded by
t
times
R(\boldsymbol{e})=\exp\left(C\cdot\left\lVert\boldsymbol{e}\right\rVert_{1}\right)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{e}\right\rVert_{1})
, where
C
is some universal constant. The expectation
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[R(\boldsymbol{e})\right]
is finite, implying that the expectation of
o_{\boldsymbol{e}}(t)
is indeed
o(t)
.
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}\\
\boldsymbol{e}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)\end{subarray}}\left[A_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}\\
\boldsymbol{e}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)\end{subarray}}\left[B_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\right]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{z}\sim\widetilde{\mu}\\
\boldsymbol{e}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)\end{subarray}}\left[A^{2}_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{e}}\right]=(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu))^{\intercal}J(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu)),

where
\boldsymbol{b}(\mu):=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\mu}\left[\boldsymbol{x}\right]
denotes the center of
\mu
and
J=L^{\intercal}L
. Together with (
85
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Ent}_{Q_{t\to 1}(x,\cdot)}\left[f_{t}\right]}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{t=\eta^{+}}=\frac{1}{2(1-\eta)^{2}}(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu))^{\intercal}J(\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu})-\boldsymbol{b}(\nu)).

This proves (
83
).

Recall that
\widetilde{\mu}:=Q_{\eta\to 1}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
is
C\left(\eta\right)
-spectrally independent under all external fields, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n},\quad\mathrm{Cov}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}*\widetilde{\mu})\preceq C(\eta)\cdot I.

By
[
20
, Lemma 40]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{b}(\nu)-\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu}))J(\boldsymbol{b}(\nu)-\boldsymbol{b}(\widetilde{\mu}))\leq\alpha\mathrm{Ent}_{\widetilde{\mu}}\left[f\right],

(89)

where
\alpha=C(\eta)\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{2}
.
Lemma

3.22
then follows from (
83
) and (
89
).
∎

Appendix B
Missing Proofs from
Section

5

In this section, we present the formal proofs of
Lemma

5.8
and
Lemma

5.13
.
We start with the proof of
Lemma

5.13
for the Ising model in
Section

B.1
, as it follows standard methods and is comparatively straightforward.
We then address
Lemma

5.8
for the hardcore model in
Section

B.2
, where the proof is more intricate and technically challenging due to the need for symmetrization.

B.1
Proof of
Lemma

5.13

As in the proof of
[
37
]
, we approximate
\log\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
via an optimization problem. For convenience, let
H(x)=-x\log x
be the entropy function.

Lemma B.1

(
[
37
]
)
.

For any integers
0\leq s,t\leq n
, the logarithm of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\log\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}-n\underbrace{\left(\Delta\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-(\Delta-1)f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})\right)}_{=:U(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\right|=O_{\Delta}\left(\log n\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\left(\frac{s}{n},\frac{t}{n}\right)
,
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{4}H(\theta_{i})+2\beta(\theta_{1}+\theta_{4})
,
f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}H(\rho_{i})+H(1-\rho_{i})
. Furthermore, the maximum is taken over all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in[0,1]^{4}
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}=1,\quad\theta_{2}+\theta_{4}=\rho_{1},\quad\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}=\rho_{2}.

Proof.

The coefficient
[x^{s}y^{t}]N
of the generating function
N
in (
66
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\binom{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\exp\left(2\beta\cdot\left(\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{4}\right)\right)\leq[x^{s}y^{t}]N\leq n^{4}\max_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\binom{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\exp\left(2\beta\cdot(\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{4})\right),

where
\binom{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=\frac{n!}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\zeta_{i}!}
is the multinomial coefficient, and the maximum is taken over all
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
with

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}+\zeta_{3}+\zeta_{4}=n,\quad\zeta_{2}+\zeta_{4}=s,\quad\zeta_{3}+\zeta_{4}=t.

(90)

By the definition of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
in (
67
), the upper bound for
\log\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
follows immediately from Stirling’s approximation and that
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}/n
. For the lower bound, suppose
\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\theta)
achieves the maximum at
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
for
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\left(\frac{s}{n},\frac{t}{n}\right)
. We may round
n\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
to the nearest integer point
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
satisfying (
90
). Hence, the difference between
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})
and
f_{N}\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta}/n\right)
can be bounded by
O(\log n/n)
, and the lower bound for
\log\widetilde{\alpha}_{s,t}
can be established by Stirling’s approximation.
∎

Lemma B.2

(
[
37
]
)
.

U(\boldsymbol{\rho})
(see
Lemma

B.1
) achieves maximum value at its unique critical point
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\star}=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
. Furthermore, the function
U(\rho,\rho)
has a unique critical point at
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.2
.

The former part was proved in
[
37
, Lemma 11, Lemma 12]
. We now aim to prove the latter. By definition,
U(\rho,\rho)
corresponds to the following optimization program:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle\Delta f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-(\Delta-1)f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})

(91)

\displaystyle\textrm{s.t.}

\displaystyle\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}=1

\displaystyle\theta_{2}+\theta_{4}=\rho

\displaystyle\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}=\rho

Applying the method of Lagrange multiplier, there exist functions
P(\rho),Q(\rho)\geq 0
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\exp\left(2\beta\right)P,\quad\theta_{2}=\theta_{3}=PQ,\quad\theta_{4}=\exp\left(2\beta\right)PQ^{2},\quad\text{ where }Q^{\Delta}=\left(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\right)^{\Delta-1}.

Then, combining (
91
), we have the following equations

\displaystyle Q^{\Delta}=\left(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\right)^{\Delta-1}=\left(\frac{\theta_{2}+\theta_{4}}{\theta_{1}+\theta_{3}}\right)^{\Delta-1}=\left(\frac{Q+\exp\left(2\beta\right)Q^{2}}{\exp\left(2\beta\right)+Q}\right)^{\Delta-1}=Q^{\Delta-1}\left(\frac{1+\exp\left(2\beta\right)Q}{\exp\left(2\beta\right)+Q}\right)^{\Delta-1}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle Q=\left(\frac{1+\exp\left(2\beta\right)Q}{\exp\left(2\beta\right)+Q}\right)^{\Delta-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}=Q^{\frac{\Delta}{\Delta-1}}.

Thus,
Q
should be the fixed point of critical Ising model, implying
Q=1
and
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.13
.

By
Lemma

B.1
, it suffices to show that there exist
\varepsilon,\eta>0
satisfying

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\rho})\leq-\varepsilon n^{-1/2}+U\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\star}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}
satisfies
\left|\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}-1\right|>n^{-1/4}
and
\left|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\right|\leq\eta n^{-1/4}
.
By
Lemma

B.2
, we may assume without loss of generality
9
9

9

Lemma

B.2
indicates that the maximum value only achieves at the point
(1/2,1/2)
, implying the values
U(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
outside the
(0.25,0.75)^{2}
box is less than
U(1/2,1/2)-\varepsilon
for some constant
\varepsilon>0
.
that
0.25<\rho_{1},\rho_{2}<0.75
. Note that for any
0.25<x_{1},x_{2}<0.75
,

\displaystyle 0\leq 2H\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}\right)-H(x_{1})-H(x_{2})\leq\min_{0.25\leq x\leq 0.75}\left|H^{\prime\prime}(x)\right|\cdot\left|x_{1}-x_{2}\right|^{2}\leq 4\left|x_{1}-x_{2}\right|^{2}.

Therefore,
U(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\leq U(\rho,\rho)+8(\Delta-1)\eta n^{-1/2}
, where
\rho
is the average of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
.
Since
\left|\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}-1\right|>n^{-1/4}
, we have
\left|2\rho-1\right|>n^{-1/4}
.
Let
\rho_{l/r}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm n^{-1/4})
, by
Lemma

B.2
, we have

\displaystyle U(\rho,\rho)\leq\max\left\{U\left(\rho_{l},\rho_{l}\right),U\left(\rho_{r},\rho_{r}\right)\right\}=U\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)-Q(\Delta)n^{-1/2}+O_{\Delta}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right),

and the last inequality follows from
Lemma

B.1
and
Lemma

5.12
(
Q
is the constant in
Lemma

5.12
).
Hence, we can pick
\eta=\frac{Q(\Delta)/4}{8(\Delta-1)}
and get

\displaystyle U(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\leq U\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)-\frac{Q(\Delta)}{2}n^{-1/2}

when
n
is sufficiently large. This concludes the proof.
∎

B.2
Proof of
Lemma

5.8

As in the previous proof, we approximate
\log\alpha_{A,B,C}
via an optimization problem. For convenience, let
H(x)=-x\log x
be the entropy function.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}=\left(1,2\lambda^{1/\Delta},2\lambda^{1/\Delta},2\lambda^{2/\Delta},\lambda^{2/\Delta},\lambda^{2/\Delta}\right)
.
Then, we define
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})
as

\displaystyle f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{6}H(\theta_{i})+\theta_{i}\log p_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})=\sum_{i=1}^{4}H(\rho_{i})

where
H(x)=-x\log x
is the entropy function.

For integers
0\leq A,B,C\leq 2n
with
A+B+2C\leq 2n
, the logarithm of
\alpha_{A,B,C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\log\alpha_{A,B,C}-n\underbrace{\left(\Delta\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-2(\Delta-1)f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})\right)}_{=:U(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\right|=O_{\Delta}\left(\log n\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\left(1-\frac{A+B+C}{2n},\frac{A}{2n},\frac{B}{2n},\frac{C}{2n}\right)
, and the maximum is taken over all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in[0,1]^{6}
satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta_{i}=1,\quad\theta_{2}+2\theta_{5}=2\rho_{2},\quad\theta_{3}+2\theta_{6}=2\rho_{3},\quad\theta_{4}=2\rho_{4}.

(92)

Remark B.4
.

We remark that
U(\boldsymbol{\rho})
is defined on

\displaystyle\Omega=\{\boldsymbol{\rho}\in[0,1]^{4}\mid\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}+\rho_{3}+\rho_{4}=1\text{ and }\rho_{2}+\rho_{3}+2\rho_{4}\leq 1\}.

The latter constraint is to guarantee the existence of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
satisfying (
92
).

Proof of
Lemma

B.3
.

By formula (
51
) of
\alpha_{A,B,C}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}=\frac{([x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]\hat{N})^{\Delta}}{([x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]D)^{\Delta-1}},

where
\hat{N}(x,y,z)=N(\lambda^{1/\Delta}x,\lambda^{1/\Delta}y,\lambda^{2/\Delta}z)
.
According to the definition of
N
in (
50
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{N}=(p_{1}+p_{2}x+p_{3}y+p_{4}z+p_{5}x^{2}+p_{6}y^{2})^{n}.

Hence, the coefficient of the generation function
\hat{N}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\binom{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{6}p_{i}^{\zeta_{i}}\leq[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]\hat{N}\leq n^{6}\max_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\binom{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{6}p_{i}^{\zeta_{i}},

where the maximum is taken over all parameters
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}^{6}
satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{6}\zeta_{i}=n,\quad\zeta_{2}+2\zeta_{5}=A,\quad\zeta_{3}+2\zeta_{6}=B,\quad\zeta_{4}=C.

(93)

The upper bound of
\log\alpha_{A,B,C}
follows from the Stirling’s approximation, where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}/n
.
The function
\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is the approximation for the coefficient
[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]\hat{N}
; and
f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})
is the approximation for the coefficient
[x^{A}y^{B}z^{C}]D
.
For the lower bound, suppose
\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
achieves the maximum value at
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
for the given
A,B,C
. We may round
n\boldsymbol{\theta}
to the nearest integer point
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
satisfying (
93
). Hence, the difference between
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})
and
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}/n)
can be bounded by
O(\log n/n)
, and the lower bound can be established together with Stirling’s approximation.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Let
U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})=U(1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4},\rho_{2},\rho_{2},\rho_{4})
be the symmetric version of
U(\boldsymbol{\rho})
defined on
\Omega=\{(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})\in[0,1]^{2}\mid 2\rho_{2}+2\rho_{4}\leq 1\}
. Then
U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})
achieves maximum value at its unique critical point at
(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})=\left(\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
.

Proof of
Lemma

B.5
.

We first show that
U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})
has a unique critical point at
(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})=\left(\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
.
By definition,
U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})
corresponds to the following optimization program:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle\Delta f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-2(\Delta-1)f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})

(94)

\displaystyle\textrm{s.t.}

\displaystyle\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}+\theta_{5}+\theta_{6}=1

\displaystyle\theta_{2}+2\theta_{5}=2\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\theta_{3}+2\theta_{6}=2\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\theta_{4}=2\rho_{4}.

By the method of Lagrange multiplier, there exist
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}
and constants
P,Q_{1},Q_{2},R\geq 0
, such that they satisfy (
94
) and the following equations

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=p_{1}P,\quad\theta_{2}

\displaystyle=p_{2}PQ_{1},\quad\theta_{3}=p_{2}PQ_{2},\quad\theta_{4}=p_{4}PR,\quad\theta_{5}=p_{5}PQ_{1}^{2},\quad\theta_{6}=p_{5}PQ_{2}^{2}.

(95)

Moreover,
f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
(we use the fact that
p_{2}=p_{3}
and
p_{5}=p_{6}
).

We first show that
\theta^{\star}
,
P,Q_{1},Q_{2},R
are uniquely determined by (
94
) and (
95
), so that they can be seen as implicit functions of
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
.
By (
94
), we have
\theta_{2}+2\theta_{5}=\theta_{3}+2\theta_{6}
.
Let
x=\lambda^{1/\Delta}
so that
p_{2}=2x
and
p_{5}=x^{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle P(2xQ_{1}+(xQ_{1})^{2})=P(2xQ_{2}+(xQ_{2})^{2}),

which implies
P(xQ_{1}+1)^{2}=P(xQ_{2}+1)^{2}
.
Since,
Q_{1},Q_{2}\geq 0
, we can conclude that
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=:Q
.
Hence (
95
) can be simplifies to

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=p_{1}P,\quad\theta_{2}=\theta_{3}=p_{2}PQ,\quad\theta_{4}=p_{4}PR,\quad\theta_{5}=\theta_{6}=p_{5}PQ^{2}.

(96)

By (
94
) and (
96
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{4}}{\rho_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{4}}{\theta_{2}+2\theta_{5}}=\frac{p_{4}R}{p_{2}Q+p_{5}Q^{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta_{i}}{\theta_{2}+2\theta_{5}}=\frac{p_{1}+2p_{2}Q+p_{4}R+2p_{5}Q^{2}}{p_{2}Q+p_{5}Q^{2}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}Q+p_{5}Q^{2}}+\frac{\rho_{4}}{\rho_{2}},

where, recall
x=\lambda^{1/\Delta}
so that
p_{2}=2x
and
p_{5}=x^{2}
, the later equation implies that

\displaystyle\frac{p_{1}\rho_{2}+1-\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}{1-\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}=(1+xQ)^{2}.

Hence
Q
and
R
are uniquely determined by
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
.
By
\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta_{i}=1
and (
96
),
P
is also uniquely determined by
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
.
Again, by (
96
),
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}
are uniquely determined by
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
.

Now, we can conclude that
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}
,
P,Q,R
are implicit functions of
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{4}
determined by (
94
) and (
96
).
Combining (
94
), (
96
), and the fact that
p_{2}=p_{3}
,
p_{5}=p_{6}
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=f_{N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{6}-\theta^{\star}_{i}\log\frac{\theta^{\star}_{i}}{p_{i}}=-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta^{\star}_{i}\log P+2(\theta^{\star}_{2}+2\theta^{\star}_{5})\log Q+\theta^{\star}_{4}\log R\right)

\displaystyle=-(\log P+4\rho_{2}\log Q+2\rho_{4}\log R).

This also implies

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})

\displaystyle=-\Delta\left(\log P+4\rho_{2}\log Q+2\rho_{4}\log R\right)-2(\Delta-1)f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho}),

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left(1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4},\rho_{2},\rho_{2},\rho_{4}\right).

Therefore, by taking derivative on both sides of the first constraint in (
94
), we have

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{i=1}^{6}\frac{\partial\theta^{\star}_{i}}{\partial\rho_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta_{i}^{\star}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{P}\cdot\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho_{2}}+\left(\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}+2\theta_{5}+2\theta_{6}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{Q}\cdot\frac{\partial Q}{\partial\rho_{2}}+\cdot\frac{\theta_{4}}{R}\cdot\frac{\partial R}{\partial\rho_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P}\cdot\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho_{2}}+\frac{4\rho_{2}}{Q}\cdot\frac{\partial Q}{\partial\rho_{2}}+\frac{2\rho_{4}}{R}\cdot\frac{\partial R}{\partial\rho_{2}},

(97)

where the last equation follows from the constraints in (
94
). Similarly, a same type of equation holds for
\rho_{4}
as well. Finally, combining (
97
), the critical point
(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})
of
U^{\mathrm{sym}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{2}}=-\left(4\Delta\log Q+2(\Delta-1)\frac{\partial f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}{\partial\rho_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{4}}=-\left(2\Delta\log R+2(\Delta-1)\frac{\partial f_{D}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}{\partial\rho_{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=0

By a straightforward calculation, we have

\displaystyle\log Q

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta}\log\frac{\rho_{2}}{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}=\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta}\log\frac{\lambda^{1/\Delta}Q+\lambda^{2/\Delta}Q^{2}}{1+2\lambda^{1/\Delta}Q+\lambda^{2/\Delta}R}

\displaystyle\log R

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta}\log\frac{\rho_{4}}{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}=\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta}\log\frac{\lambda^{2/\Delta}R}{1+2\lambda^{1/\Delta}Q+\lambda^{2/\Delta}R},

where we use (
96
) the fact that (
94
) implies
2(1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4})=2\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}+\theta_{4}
.
Let
y_{2}=\lambda^{1/\Delta}Q
and
y_{4}=\lambda^{2/\Delta}R
. It is equivalent to the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{2}=\lambda\left(\frac{1+y_{2}}{1+2y_{2}+y_{4}}\right)^{\Delta-1}&\hskip 56.9055pt(a)\\
y_{4}=\lambda^{2}\left(\frac{1}{1+2y_{2}+y_{4}}\right)^{\Delta-1}&\hskip 56.9055pt(b)\end{cases}

(98)

Let
d=\Delta-1
.
It is direct to verify that
(y_{2},y_{4})=(\hat{x}_{d},\hat{x}_{d}^{2})
is a solution of the above system, where
\hat{x}_{d}
is the unique fixed point of the tree recursion:
\hat{x}_{d}=\lambda(\frac{1}{1+\hat{x}_{d}})^{d}
.
Since
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)=\frac{d^{d}}{(d-1)^{d+1}}
, we have
(y_{2},y_{4})=(\frac{1}{d-1},\frac{1}{(d-1)^{2}})
.
By (
96
) and (
94
), one can verify that the point
(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})=(\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}})
is a critical point.

In this paragraph, we verify that the system in (
98
) has at most
1
solution.
We resolve
y_{4}
via (b) in (
98
) and plug it into (a) in (
98
) to obtain

\displaystyle(y_{2}+1)\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{y_{2}}\right)^{1/d}-1\right)=y_{2}\left(\lambda(y_{2}+1)^{-d}+1\right).

By taking logarithm on both sides and rearranging terms, it holds that

\displaystyle F(y_{2}):=\log\left((y_{2}+1)\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{y_{2}}\right)^{1/d}-1\right)\right)-\log\left(y_{2}\left(\lambda(y_{2}+1)^{-d}+1\right)\right)=0.

To prove the uniqueness of
y
, it suffices to show that
F^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
for all
y\in[0,\lambda]
. Note that

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(\frac{\lambda}{y}\right)^{1/d}}{dy\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{y}\right){}^{1/d}-1\right)}+\frac{d\lambda}{\left(y+1\right)\left(\left(y+1\right){}^{d}+\lambda\right)}-\frac{1}{y^{2}+y}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{dy}+\frac{d\lambda}{\left(y+1\right)\left(\left(y+1\right){}^{d}+\lambda\right)}-\frac{1}{y^{2}+y}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d+1)\lambda\left((d-1)y-1\right)-\left(y+1\right){}^{d}\left(d+y+1\right)}{dy\left(y+1\right)\left(\left(y+1\right){}^{d}+\lambda\right)}.

When
(d-1)y-1\leq 0
, we have
F^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
.
Otherwise, to make
F^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
, we need

\displaystyle\lambda\leq\frac{\left(y+1\right){}^{d}\left(d+y+1\right)}{(d+1)\left((d-1)y-1\right)}.

(99)

It can be verified easily that (
99
) holds when
d=2
. We assume without loss of generality that
d\geq 3
.
Since
y\in[\frac{1}{d-1},\lambda]
, we can simplify the r.h.s. as follow

\displaystyle\frac{\left(y+1\right){}^{d}\left(d+y+1\right)}{(d+1)\left((d-1)y-1\right)}\geq\lambda_{c}(\Delta)\frac{d-1}{d+1}\frac{d+\lambda+1}{(d-1)\lambda-1}.

The r.h.s. is greater than
\lambda
for
\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta)
and
d\geq 3
. Hence, we have shown the uniqueness of critical point.

Finally, we prove that
U^{\mathrm{sym}}
cannot achieves the maximum value on the boundary.
Recall
y_{2}=\lambda^{1/\Delta}Q
and
y_{4}=\lambda^{2/\Delta}R
.
By (
94
) and (
96
), it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{y_{2}+2y_{2}^{2}}{1+y_{2}}=\frac{2\rho_{2}}{1-2\rho_{4}-2\rho_{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{y_{4}}{y_{2}+y_{2}^{2}}=\frac{\rho_{4}}{\rho_{2}}.

(100)

Recall that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{2}}

\displaystyle=-4\log\left(\frac{\rho_{2}}{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}\cdot\left(\frac{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}{\rho_{2}}\cdot Q\right)^{\Delta}\right)

\displaystyle=-4\log\left(\frac{\rho_{2}}{\lambda\left(1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}\right)}\cdot\left(\frac{1+2y_{2}+y_{4}}{1+y_{2}}\right)^{\Delta}\right)

(101)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{4}}

\displaystyle=-2\log\left(\frac{\rho_{4}}{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}\cdot\left(\frac{1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}}{\rho_{4}}\cdot R\right)^{\Delta}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\log\left(\frac{\rho_{4}}{\lambda^{2}\left(1-2\rho_{2}-\rho_{4}\right)}\cdot\left(1+2y_{2}+y_{4}\right)^{\Delta}\right).

(102)

We are now ready to verify that
U^{\mathrm{sym}}
does not achieve its maximum value on the boundary, i.e., the function does not achieve its maximum value when
\rho_{2}\rho_{4}(1-2\rho_{2}-2\rho_{4})\to 0^{+}
. We follow a similar proof as in
[
37
]
.

Case 1:
\rho_{4}\to 0^{+}

Note that
1+2y_{2}+y_{4}
can be upper bounded by a constant depending on
\rho_{2}
.
This can be confirmed by (
100
).
Therefore, by (
102
),
\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{4}}\to+\infty
as
\rho_{4}\to 0^{+}
.

Case 2:
\rho_{2}\to 0^{+}

We first show that
y_{4}
can be bounded by a constant. Let
K=\frac{2\rho_{2}}{1-2\rho_{2}-2\rho_{4}}
. By (
100
) and
y_{2}\geq 0
, it holds that
y_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\sqrt{K^{2}+6K+1}+K-1\right)=\frac{K}{1-K+\sqrt{K^{2}+6K+1}}\leq K
. Therefore,
y_{4}\leq 2y_{2}\cdot\frac{\rho_{4}}{\rho_{2}}\leq\frac{2\rho_{4}}{1-2\rho_{2}-2\rho_{4}}
. Hence,
\frac{1+2y_{2}+y_{4}}{1+y_{2}}
is upper bounded by a constant.
According to (
101
), we have
\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{2}}\to+\infty
as
\rho_{2}\to 0^{+}
.

Case 3:
2\rho_{2}+2\rho_{4}\to 1^{-}

By the argument in Case 2,
y_{2}\to+\infty
as
2\rho_{2}+2\rho_{4}\to 1^{-}
. Therefore, by (
102
),
\frac{\partial U^{\mathrm{sym}}}{\partial\rho_{4}}\to-\infty
as
2\rho_{2}+2\rho_{4}\to 1^{-}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

5.8
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.8
.

By
Lemma

B.3
, it suffices to prove there exist constants
\varepsilon,\eta>0
satisfying

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\rho})\leq-\varepsilon n^{-2/3}+U(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\star}),

(103)

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\star}=\left(\frac{(\Delta-1)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{\Delta-1}{\Delta^{2}},\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}\right)
, and
\boldsymbol{\rho}
satisfies

1.

\left|\rho_{2}+\rho_{3}-\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{\Delta^{2}}\right|>n^{-1/3}
or
\left|\rho_{4}-\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}\right|>n^{-1/3}
;

2.

\left|\rho_{2}-\rho_{3}\right|\leq\eta n^{-1/3}
.

By
Lemma

B.5
and the continuity of function
U
, there exist constants
0<\alpha\leq 1/2
and
\beta>0
such that for every
\rho_{4}\in[0,1/2]
,
\sup_{\rho_{2}\in[0,\alpha]}U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2},\rho_{4})\leq U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2}^{\star},\rho_{4}^{\star})-\beta
.
This implies that there exists constant
\kappa
such that
0<2\kappa\leq\alpha
,
4(\Delta-1)\sqrt{2\kappa}\leq\beta/2
; and when
n
is sufficiently large,
\min\left\{\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\right\}\leq\kappa
, we have
\max\left\{\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\right\}\leq\kappa+\eta n^{-1/3}\leq 2\kappa
and

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\rho})

\displaystyle\leq U^{\mathrm{sym}}((\rho_{2}+\rho_{3})/2,\rho_{4})+2(\Delta-1)(2H(\left(\rho_{2}+\rho_{3}\right)/2))

\displaystyle\leq U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2}^{\star},\rho_{4}^{\star})-\beta+2(\Delta-1)(2\sqrt{2\kappa})\leq U^{\mathrm{sym}}(\rho_{2}^{\star},\rho_{4}^{\star})-\beta/2.

Hence (
103
) holds when
